# Definitions of Faithfulness, Counterfactual and Monitorability

This site maps research on chain-of-thought (CoT) faithfulness and monitorability, and "the map" means its collection of papers. This page collects how the papers in the map define three terms: *faithfulness*, *counterfactual* and *monitorability*. It is based on 3,414 definitions quoted from 216 of the 217 papers that the map held when this collection of definitions was built. The papers on formal frameworks that were added to the map on 1 November 2026 have their own collection of definitions, in the Formal tab of the dashboard and on the [formal frameworks page](page.html?doc=formal).

Each definition is quoted word for word, with its location in the paper, and is labeled with a kind and a stance. The kind says what sort of definition it is:

- *informal*: stated in words;
- *formal*: stated mathematically;
- *operational*: a test, metric or procedure that measures or labels the notion;
- *criterion*: a necessary or sufficient condition for the notion, or an assumption it rests on;
- *failure mode*: a named way in which the notion fails, such as a type of unfaithful reasoning;
- *taxonomy*: a division into types.

The stance says where the definition comes from. Either the paper *proposes* it, or the paper borrows it from earlier work and *adopts* it unchanged or *adapts* it. For a borrowed definition, the collection records the source: 1,224 of the 1,225 borrowed definitions name one. The full set is in [`definitions.csv`](https://github.com/Dundalia/faithfulness-review/blob/main/definitions.csv), a table with one row per definition, and the Definitions tab of the dashboard lets you filter it.

For each term, the definitions are sorted into groups by meaning: a group collects definitions with a similar meaning. Each term's section then traces which papers borrow which definition from which earlier papers, checks the borrowed definitions against their sources, and lists the points on which papers disagree. In the tables, text in quotation marks is copied from the paper. Text without quotation marks is a short paraphrase, not a quote.

The table below gives, for each term, the number of definitions, the number of papers they come from, the number of groups, and the main way the definitions divide. Here and below, the trace is the model's written reasoning (its CoT).

| Term | Definitions | Papers | Groups | Main division |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [Faithfulness](#faithfulness) | 1,955 | 204 | 11 | Nine relations: disclosure, causal dependence, agreement, say-do consistency, prediction, self-report accuracy, input importance, internal computation, honesty |
| [Counterfactual](#counterfactual) | 292 | 87 | 12 | What is varied, whether the output must change, how a change becomes a causal claim, and what the comparison judges |
| [Monitorability](#monitorability) | 1,167 | 108 | 18 | A monitor's ability to predict a property, or the trace's verbalization of what drove the answer |

## Main findings

**Faithfulness has one common definition and nine relations that papers test, and the relations can disagree.** [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) define a faithful interpretation as one that "accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction", and 79 definitions from 55 papers cite them. Groups 2 to 10 of the faithfulness section distinguish nine relations. They ask what the trace discloses, whether the answer depends on the trace or agrees with it, whether an action matches what was said, and whether the explanation lets an observer predict the model. They also ask whether a self-report matches the model's behavior or internal states, whether the inputs that the explanation marks as important matter to the prediction, whether the trace matches the internal computation, and whether a statement matches the model's belief. Some of these relations relax the common definition. [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) consider one property $v$ and require that "$z$ is a sufficient statistic for $v$", which means that $z$ (for example a CoT) contains all the information relevant to predicting $v$. [Viteri et al. 2024](./#p=viteri2026markovian) prioritize "informativeness over full faithfulness". The relations can disagree on the same traces, and so can different classifiers of a single relation. In a hint test, a hint is inserted into the prompt, and a classifier decides whether the trace acknowledges it. On identical data, the three hint-acknowledgment classifiers of [Young 2026a](./#p=young2026measuring) give overall faithfulness rates of 74.4%, 82.6% and 69.7%.

**Counterfactual definitions differ on four points.** They differ in what is changed to produce the variant (the counterfactual case), in whether the variant must change the model's output, in how a change in output is turned into a claim about cause, and in what the comparison of the original and the variant is used to judge: an explanation, the model's reliance on a feature, a self-report, a training target or a covert behavior. The section on counterfactuals lists ten points of disagreement that follow from these four differences. Among them are whether the variant must change the output, and whether one flipped answer (an answer that changes after an edit) shows that the edit caused it.

**Monitorability has two competing definitions, and they disagree on whether, and how far, monitorability requires faithfulness.** In 42 definitions from 24 papers, monitorability is a monitor's ability to predict a property of the model's behavior. In 69 definitions from 34 papers, it depends on whether the trace verbalizes (states in words) what drove the answer. 9 papers have definitions in both groups. [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) write that their notion of monitorability "does not require a model’s externalized reasoning to faithfully reflect its internal reasoning process", and [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) require faithfulness only with respect to the monitored property. [Jia et al. 2026](./#p=jia2026faithfulness), [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) and [Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one) state the faithfulness requirement without that restriction.

**In 82% of the checks, the cited source contains the borrowed definition.** Borrowed definitions were checked against the sources they cite, when those sources are in the map. Of the 804 borrowed definitions that cite a source in the map, 803 were checked. This gives 1,095 checks, one for each pair of a definition and a source in the map that it cites. The one unchecked definition is the rubric that [Nguyen et al. 2026b](./#p=nguyen2026generalization) give a judge model for cue acknowledgment (whether the model's reasoning or response mentions a cue inserted into the prompt); it cites [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring). Each check ends in a verdict. In 898 checks, the source gives the definition, either as the paper states it (verdict "supported", 647 checks) or in a form that the citing paper modifies, for example by narrowing or extending it (verdict "adapted", 251 checks). The other 197 checks have one of five verdicts: "partial" (122), when the source supports only part of the definition; "renamed" (43), when the source defines the same thing under another name; "other-claim" (13), when the citation supports a claim other than the definition, such as a finding stated next to it; "unsupported" (15), when the source does not give the definition; and "misdirected" (4), when the definition comes from a different paper than the one cited. The checks cover 803 of the 804 definitions that could be checked, so these are rates for the whole collection, not estimates from a sample. Among the faithfulness definitions, ten checks find a source credited with a definition it does not give, and in three of them the source is [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards). Among the counterfactual definitions, 6 of the 15 that cite [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) fail the check, which means that their verdict is neither "supported" nor "adapted".

## How this page was made

1. **First collection of definitions, from excerpts.** A script (`scripts/definitions.py excerpt`) kept each section of a paper's full text that mentions one of the terms, leaving out the reference list and most appendices. Claude agents then quoted the definitions in those sections: 1,197 definitions. The full reading in step 2 showed that this missed definitions in methods sections and appendices.
2. **Second collection, from the full texts.** Agents read every paper in full, appendices included, and added, corrected or dropped definitions. The scope was also widened. For faithfulness, it now covers honesty, say-do consistency (whether a model's actions match what it said) and self-report accuracy (whether a model's reports about itself are true). For monitorability, it now covers scheming, covert actions, sabotage, sandbagging (deliberate underperformance), deception, reward hacking and verbalized evaluation awareness (a model saying in its reasoning that it may be under evaluation). Pure introspection (a model's access to its own internal states, studied as a capability and not as the accuracy of its reports about itself) is out of scope. So is faithfulness to a source document (as in summarization), unless the paper explicitly contrasts it with the notions above. To apply these exclusions, another model, GPT-6.1 Sol (run through Codex), screened the 130 definitions that a keyword search flagged and dropped 20. A check of a sample of 12 added definitions found 3 errors (one definition out of scope, and two with the wrong stance or source), and they are fixed (`checks/sample_audit_*.json`). Every one of the 3,414 quotes appears word for word in the paper's text, apart from differences in whitespace and curly quotes.
3. **Checking borrowed definitions against their sources.** For 803 of the 804 borrowed definitions whose source is in the map, an agent read the source and gave a verdict (1,095 checks of a definition against a cited source). The verdicts are recorded in the `attribution` column of the CSV file. Sources outside the map were not checked. One of them is Atanasova et al. (2023), which is cited by 18 counterfactual definitions from 14 papers. Of these, 13 definitions (from 10 papers) are in the group on the counterfactual test of explanations, which asks whether an explanation mentions an edit to the input that changed the prediction.
4. **Grouping the definitions and writing the sections.** For each term, one agent placed every definition in exactly one group (the `cluster` column of the CSV file) and wrote the term's section. It also listed each factual claim that the section makes: 607 claims in all.
5. **Two independent checks of every claim.** GPT-6.1 Sol checked each claim against the papers' full texts and recomputed every count. 95 claims were wrong (38), overstated (52) or understated (5), and they were replaced by the strongest statement that the evidence supports. Claude Opus then checked every claim again, without seeing those verdicts, and corrected 54 more (19 wrong, 25 overstated, 10 understated). Of these 54, 43 had passed the first check. A last round of editing fixed references and counts that span more than one sentence. The 15 sentences it reworded were checked a third time, and 3 were corrected. On a sample of 31 claims, two independent runs of the first checker found 7 and 6 errors, 5 of them found by both runs (`checks/calibration_*.json`). So a single check misses some errors. The second check reduces this risk but does not remove it. The prompts of every check are in `checks/prompts/`.

The raw outputs are in [`runs/D_definitions/`](https://github.com/Dundalia/faithfulness-review/tree/main/runs/D_definitions). The claims and the verdicts of each check are in `claims_<term>.json`, one file per term. The collection of definitions, the groups and the checks are machine-made, so check a quote in the paper before citing it.

## Faithfulness

This section covers 1,955 definitions of faithfulness and of related notions, quoted from 204 of the 217 papers in this site's literature map (below, "the map"). Each definition has a kind, which says what sort of definition it is: 681 are operational (they describe a test or a measurement), 369 are informal (a definition in words), 297 are criteria (necessary or sufficient conditions for a notion, or assumptions it rests on), 231 are failure modes (named ways in which faithfulness or a related notion fails), 211 are formal (mathematical) and 166 are taxonomies (they divide a notion into types). Each definition also has a stance: 1,244 are proposed by the paper itself, 483 are adopted from an earlier paper as they stand, and 228 are adapted from an earlier paper with changes. 628 of these definitions define faithfulness or unfaithfulness itself. The other 1,327 define a related notion that the paper ties to faithfulness, such as plausibility, simulatability (how well an observer can predict the model's outputs), self-consistency (whether the model's outputs, such as an answer and its explanation, agree with each other or rely on the same parts of the input), post-hoc rationalization (reasoning written after the answer was already fixed), the rate at which a CoT mentions (verbalizes) a cue, a self-report or a lie. An earlier version of this page was built from a first collection of definitions. For this version, a second collection read the full text of every paper: it added 1,216 definitions that were kept after screening, and 36 papers, to the 739 definitions that remain from the earlier version (it dropped one), and 1,008 of the added definitions define related notions. The definitions are sorted by meaning into the 11 groups below, and each definition belongs to exactly one group. Groups 5, 7 and 10 are new in this version, and 62 definitions from the earlier version moved to a different group, 57 of them into the new groups.

[Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) define a faithful interpretation as one that "accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction". 79 definitions from 55 papers cite them, and 53 of those definitions are in group 1, which states this goal without giving a test. Other groups include weaker requirements, formal definitions restricted to one property, and related behavioral abilities. [Viteri et al. 2024](./#p=viteri2026markovian) prioritize "informativeness over full faithfulness" and require the CoT alone to suffice for the correct final answer. [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) require that "$z$ is a sufficient statistic for $v$", meaning that $z$ (for example a CoT) contains all the information relevant to predicting a property $v$, and they describe this as the same intuitive definition restricted to one property. [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) evaluate self-modeling by whether self-reports agree with observed behavior, without distinguishing where the reports come from. Groups 2 to 10 each fix a different relation: what the trace discloses (group 2), whether the answer depends on the trace (3) or agrees with it (4), whether an action matches what was said (5), whether the explanation lets an observer predict the model (6), whether a self-report matches the model's behavior or internal states (7), whether the inputs that the explanation marks as important matter (8), whether the trace matches the internal computation (9), and whether a statement matches the model's belief (10). These relations can disagree on the same traces, and so can different classifiers for a single relation. [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) find that the results of hint-verbalization tests, which check whether the CoT mentions a hint, "do not align with two other prominent faithfulness metrics": Filler Tokens (which replaces the CoT with meaningless filler tokens) and FUR (which unlearns reasoning steps from the model's weights). The three classifiers of [Young 2026a](./#p=young2026measuring) that judge whether a CoT acknowledges a hint give, "On identical data", overall faithfulness rates of 74.4%, 82.6% and 69.7%.

In the tables below, text in quotation marks is copied from the paper, with omissions marked by an ellipsis. Each group's section explains the group, quotes one definition from it, lists selected definitions in a table, names the papers that give the group's other definitions, and ends with the group's mathematical definitions.

### 1. The general definition: accurate representation of the reasoning process

This group holds the general definition: an explanation or CoT is faithful when it accurately represents, reflects or describes the process by which the model reached its output. Of the 191 definitions, 103 are informal, 108 adopted and 15 operational. The group also holds 40 taxonomies: 27 divide the general definition into kinds of test (contextual vs parametric, behavioral vs mechanistic, external vs internal), and 10 divide faithfulness or unfaithfulness into kinds. It also holds the conditions that papers set on any measure of faithfulness: that it be graded rather than binary ([Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards)), that humans cannot judge it ([Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness)), or that it rank a faithful explanation above an unfaithful one ([Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal)).

> Intuitively, a faithful interpretation is one that accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction.
>
> ([Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards), §1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | faithful interpretation | informal | “a faithful interpretation is one that accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction” |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | CoT faithfulness | informal | “the generated reasoning is faithful to the model’s true reasoning” |
| [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | faithfulness (interpretability) | informal | “faithfulness (also called fidelity or reliability) means that an explanation should “accurately represent the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction”” |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | CoT faithfulness | informal | “a faithful CoT should highlight the key decision factors and reasoning chains that would enable us to predict how it would process similar inputs during deployment” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | faithfulness (of self-explanations) | informal | “An explanation is considered faithful if it accurately represents the reasoning of the underlying model.” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | faithfulness (of CoT) | informal | “the extent to which the steps articulated in the reasoning chain correspond to the actual reasoning mechanisms employed by the model” |
| [Emmons et al. 2025a](./#p=emmons2025chain) | faithful CoT | informal | “may or may not be an accurate description of the steps it actually took during its serial operations” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (reward misspecification)](./#p=anon2026reward) | CoT faithfulness | informal | “whether a CoT reflects model internal computation for arriving at its final output” |
| [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) | faithful plan (description of deliberation) | informal | “not whether naturally generated plans are faithful descriptions of internal deliberation” |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | binary faithfulness | informal | “whether an interpretation is strictly faithful or not” |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | graded faithfulness across models and tasks | taxonomy | “The degree (as grayscale) of faithfulness at the level of specific models and tasks.” |
| [Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness) | faithfulness not measurable by human judgment | criterion | “The topic of faithfulness can not be measured by humans” |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) | contextual vs. parametric faithfulness | taxonomy | “contextual faithfulness, measured by perturbing the input or CoT trace, and parametric faithfulness, assessed by intervening on a model’s parametric knowledge” |
| [Young 2026a](./#p=young2026measuring) | CoT faithfulness (family of constructs) | informal | “not appear to be a single measurable quantity but rather a family of related constructs whose values depend critically on the measurement instrument” |
| [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) | faithfulness (model-relative) | criterion | “Faithfulness is not a property of a dataset; it is a relationship between a specific model’s internal reasoning and its output” |

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) define a faithfulness metric as a real-valued function of an input, output, explanation and model, $\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},M)\in\mathbb{R}$. They judge a metric by its diagnosticity $D(\mathcal{F})$, computed over pairs of explanations from two knowledge-edited models (models whose stored facts were edited): it is the mean of a score that is $d=1$ when the explanation faithful to the evaluated model scores higher, $0.5$ on a tie and $0$ otherwise.
- [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) define faithfulness gain transfer as the change on metric $f_j$ from a base model's CoT $c$ to the CoT $c'$ of a model optimized on metric $f_i$: $\delta(f_i,f_j)=f_j(c')-f_j(c)$.

### 2. Disclosure of the factors that drove the answer

Here a trace is faithful when it states the factors that moved the answer. The standard test is cue (or hint) injection: it inserts a cue into the prompt (a suggested answer, a metadata hint, a planted bias), keeps the cases where the answer moves to the cued option, and asks whether the CoT mentions the cue and, in stricter versions, whether it presents the cue as a reason. Of the 433 definitions, 168 are operational (judge rubrics, keyword detectors, cue taxonomies, noise corrections) and 72 are failure modes (silent re-derivation of the cued answer, laundering of the cue's argument as if it were the model's own, unverbalized reward hacks). Variants move the factor from planted cues to concepts ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk); [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)), to reports on which input factors influenced the output ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit)) and to pairs of logically opposite questions ([Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain)).

> In order to be faithful in this setup, models must either acknowledge any biases affecting their predictions or give predictions unaffected by bias.
>
> ([Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), §2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | faithfulness under biasing features | criterion | “models must either acknowledge any biases affecting their predictions or give predictions unaffected by bias” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | systematic unfaithfulness | informal | “whether these features are an important driver of model predictions, despite their explanations not mentioning the influence of these features” |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | verbalizing the hint | criterion | “(a) the CoT mentions the hint (in part or in whole), and (b) the CoT depends on the hint to reach the answer” |
| [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | articulation (of a cue) | criterion | “The model’s response has to describe the switching effect of the cue to qualify as an articulation.” |
| [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) | faithful / unfaithful response (cue switch test) | operational | “If the model’s answer does not change between the prompts, we say it is faithful.” |
| [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) | faithfulness (cue acknowledgment rate) | operational | “Faithfulness is calculated as the presence of the given cue in a model’s reasoning trace (binary for each cue).” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one) | verbalizes (hint) | criterion | “it presents the hint as a reason, sole or partial, for choosing it” |
| [Walden & Wanner 2026](./#p=walden2026reasoning) | honesty (lenient, about hint use) | criterion | “a lenient notion of honesty, requiring only that the CoT express an intended ( $H_{I}$ ) or actual ( $H_{U}$ ) use of the hint in some form” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | causal concept faithfulness | informal | “the alignment between the causal effects of concepts and the rate at which they are mentioned in an LLM’s explanations” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) | unverbalized biases | failure-mode | “decision factors that systematically influence the model’s outputs but are not cited as justification in its CoT” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | unfaithfulness (pair-level) | criterion | “we flag a pair as unfaithful when the model’s behaviour across logically opposite prompts is inconsistent with any single, stable reasoning rule” |

| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | faithfulness (factor-level self-reports) | informal | “whether a model truthfully reports which input factors influenced its output” |
| [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) | faithful self-explanation (influence-mention agreement) | criterion | “a faithful self-explanation should mention $\Delta$ if and only if it influences the decision $Y^{\prime}$” |
| [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) | latent conditional faithfulness F | formal | “This is the verbalization probability conditional on an answer change that the hint actually caused.” |

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) use pairs of an unhinted prompt $x_u$ and a hinted prompt $x_h$, with answers $a_u,a_h$ and hinted CoT $c_h$. Their score is the share of hinted CoTs that verbalize the hint $h$, among the pairs where the unhinted answer is not the hint answer and the hinted answer is: $\mathrm{Faith}(M)=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{1}[c_h\text{ verbalizes the hint }h]\;\big|\;a_u\neq h,\ a_h=h\big]$. They divide it by $\alpha=1-q/((n-2)p)$ to discount answer changes that random switching would produce, and clip it at 1.
- [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) define causal concept faithfulness as the Pearson correlation between each concept's causal effect on the answer and its effect as implied by the explanation, $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})=\mathrm{PCC}\big(\mathrm{CE}(\mathbf{x},\mathcal{C}),\,\mathrm{EE}(\mathbf{x},\mathcal{C})\big)$, and average it over questions for the dataset score.
- [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) takes as target the probability that the hinted CoT verbalizes the hint, given that the hint caused the switch: $F=\mathbb{P}(c_h\text{ verbalizes the hint}\mid\text{hint-caused switch})$. This target is not identified (the data cannot determine it) when random switches to the hint, of mass $b$ out of the total switch mass $p$, verbalize it at an unknown rate $\nu$. With the observed mass $r$ of switches that verbalize the hint, Little derives the bounds $F\in\big[\max(0,\frac{r-b}{p-b}),\min(1,\frac{r}{p-b})\big]$, whose upper endpoint ($\nu=0$) "equals the normalized faithfulness score of Chen et al. (2025)".
- [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) call a concept $c$ an unverbalized bias when two conditions hold over discordant pairs of $+/-$ variants of the concept: a McNemar test (a test for paired yes/no outcomes) finds $|D^{+}|\neq|D^{-}|$, and the verbalization rate on those pairs is at most $\tau$ ($\tau=0.3$).

### 3. Causal dependence of the answer on the stated reasoning

Here faithfulness concerns whether the answer causally depends on what the stated reasoning says. Several interventions test this dependence: truncating the CoT, inserting mistakes into it, unlearning a step from the weights, and swapping in a counterfactual CoT. Replacing the CoT with filler tokens tests whether extra computation alone explains the CoT's benefit, while paraphrasing it tests whether that benefit depends on information encoded in the phrasing ([Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring); [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring); [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making)). The central failure is post-hoc reasoning, produced after the answer is fixed. 63 of the 402 definitions are failure modes (post-hoc rationalization, reasoning theater, decorative and performative CoT). The tests of [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) are the template. Later definitions restate them as mediation analysis (which splits the input's effect on the answer into a part that passes through the CoT and a part that bypasses it), move the intervention into the weights ([Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)), use probes (classifiers trained on the model's activations) to locate the answer before the CoT ([Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms); [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc)), or set validity conditions that an intervention must pass before its effect is read as faithfulness ([Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp)).

> A reasoning trace is faithful if the model’s answer causally depends on the intermediate reasoning steps.
>
> ([Swaroop et al. 2025](./#p=swaroop2025lift), §2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | adding mistakes | operational | “If inserting a mistake into the CoT changes the model’s final answer, then the model is likely not ignoring the CoT.” |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | post-hoc reasoning (relation to faithfulness) | criterion | “Reasoning not being post-hoc does not guarantee faithfulness, nor does being post-hoc exclude faithfulness.” |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | filler tokens test | operational | “If the filler tokens provide a significant performance improvement, then the CoT may be unfaithful” |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | ff-hard | formal | “measuring whether unlearning any step causes the model to output a different label as the most likely one” |
| [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | indirect effect (of CoT on answer) | formal | “Indirect Effect (IE) measures how much an intervention $X$ changes $Y$ indirectly through $R$ .” |
| [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case) | CoT faithfulness | formal | “faithful if the instruction influences the final answer only through the generated CoT” |
| [Jia et al. 2026](./#p=jia2026faithfulness) | CoT faithfulness (information flow) | informal | “faithful reasoning requires that the prompt influence the answer through the mediated path $P\rightarrow C\rightarrow A$” |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) | faithful model (to intermediate structures) | informal | “We define a model as faithful if the final decision causally depends on the intermediate representation.” |
| [Emmons et al. 2025a](./#p=emmons2025chain) | CoT-as-rationalization vs CoT-as-computation | taxonomy | “CoT-as-rationalization, where the model explains an answer it has already computed, and CoT-as-computation, where the CoT is a necessary part of the computation itself” |
| [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc) | post-hoc reasoning (pre-committed answer) | failure-mode | “instances where the model’s answer is determined before the CoT” |
| [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) | reasoning theater | failure-mode | “reasoning theater: deliberative-looking steps that contribute nothing to correctness” |
| [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) | causal load-bearingness (of CoT) | informal | “The protocol measures the causal load-bearingness of the CoT ... and is distinct from full mechanistic faithfulness” |
| [Viteri et al. 2024](./#p=viteri2026markovian) | informativeness (CoT sufficiency) | criterion | “Rather than insisting the CoT mirrors the model’s entire internal process, we require that the CoT alone suffices to produce the final answer” |
| [Basu & Chakraborty 2026](./#p=basu2026measuring) | decoration (mode) | failure-mode | “Decoration: CoT neither improves accuracy nor contains necessary steps.” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp) | CRISP (construct measured) | informal | “how strongly a model’s answer depends on the semantic content of a supplied chain under teacher-forced replay” |

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Jia et al. 2026](./#p=jia2026faithfulness) split the requirement on a trace $(P,C,A)$ (prompt, CoT, answer) into three parts. *Sufficiency*: $H(A\mid C)$ is low, so little uncertainty about the answer remains once the CoT is known. *Completeness*: $I(P;A\mid C)\approx 0$, so that $C$ screens off $P$ from $A$ (once the CoT is known, the prompt adds almost no information about the answer); an unverbalized hint is a completeness failure, which ties this group to group 2. *Necessity*: perturbing $C$ changes $A$.
- [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case) use instruction $Z$, CoT $X$ and answer $Y$. The process is faithful if $p(x,y\mid z)=p(x\mid z)\,p(y\mid x)$, which is equivalent to $Y\perp Z\mid X$ (once the CoT is known, the answer is independent of the instruction).
- [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) unlearn one reasoning step $r_i$ at a time, where $M^{(i)*}$ is the model after unlearning step $r_i$ and $y^{(i)*}$ its prediction. The hard score is 1 when unlearning some step changes the prediction, and the soft score is the drop in the probability of the original prediction: $\mathrm{ff}_{\text{hard}}=\mathbb{1}\big[\exists\,r_i:\ y\neq y^{(i)*}\big]$ and $\mathrm{ff}^{(i)}_{\text{soft}}=p(y\mid M)-p(y\mid M^{(i)*})$.
- [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms) define $t^{*}(v_i)$ as the first token position at which a probe at some layer decodes the value $v_i$ with accuracy above $\tau=0.9$: $t^{*}(v_i)=\min\{t\mid\max_l\mathrm{acc}(t,l,v_i)>\tau\}$. Positions are counted from the start of the CoT, so a negative $t^*$ means the value "is computed before CoT begins".

### 4. Agreement between the stated reasoning and the answer

Here faithfulness is read off the output: the answer follows from, is entailed by or agrees with what the reasoning says. Some definitions come from systems in which a solver or a selection-inference pipeline derives the answer from the stated chain, so that the chain is faithful by construction. Others measure the consistency between reasoning and answer with judges or with natural language inference (NLI) models. Others also require the reasoning to be valid, so that a correct answer reached through a flawed chain counts as unfaithful. 121 of the 147 definitions are proposed. The 27 definitions from the earlier version on whether an agent's action follows its stated reasoning or plan moved to group 5, where they make up 27 of its 99 definitions.

> To our knowledge, this is the first system to generate multistep chains that are both faithful (the answer follows from the reasoning) and truthful (the chain reflects the system’s own internal beliefs).
>
> ([Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer), Abstract)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer) | faithful chain of reasoning | informal | “both faithful (the answer follows from the reasoning) and truthful (the chain reflects the system’s own internal beliefs)” |
| [Creswell & Shanahan 2022](./#p=creswell2022faithful) | faithful reasoning | informal | “a system that reasons faithfully is one whose underlying computations mirror standard definitions of logical validity” |
| [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | Faithful CoT (faithful by construction) | criterion | “$C$ is guaranteed to be a faithful model explanation, since our final $A$ is the result of deterministically executing $C_{SL}$” |
| [Pan et al. 2023](./#p=pan2023logiclm) | faithful reasoning (symbolic solver) | criterion | “the reasoning is guaranteed to be faithful as long as the problem formulation is correct” |
| [Sanyal et al. 2022](./#p=sanyal2022fairr) | faithful reasoning (proof-to-inference causality) | criterion | “there is a causal relation from determining the proof to then generating the conclusion” |
| [Hong et al. 2023](./#p=hong2023faithful) | faithful reasoning steps (answer follows steps) | informal | “the answer may not faithfully follow the intermediate steps” |
| [Zhao et al. 2026](./#p=zhao2026robustness) | faithfulness (reasoning-answer consistency) | informal | “whether the model’s final answer is consistent with its generated chain-of-thought reasoning” |
| [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) | unfaithful CoT (CoT-answer inconsistency) | criterion | “If an incorrect CoT induces a correct answer or a correct CoT induces a wrong answer, it is seen as an unfaithful CoT” |
| [Xu et al. 2024](./#p=xu2024faithful) | faithful / unfaithful reasoning (instance) | operational | “’faithful’ if both the answer and the process are correct and logical; ’unfaithful’ if the answer is correct but the process is not” |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) | in-distribution (ID) faithfulness | formal | “whether the model’s decision matches what its predicted mediator deterministically implies” |
| [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary) | explanation inconsistency | failure-mode | “failing to explicate why the model predicted $\bm{a}$ instead of its negation $\lnot\bm{a}$” |
| [Lin et al. 2025](./#p=lin2025clarity) | reasoning consistency | informal | “the logical alignment between a model’s internal analytical trace and its final predicted option” |
| [Wang 2026](./#p=wang2026doing) | reasoning → conclusion validity | operational | “whether the stated DECISION follows from the agent’s own stated EQUITY and POT_ODDS under the rule it restated” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | prediction follows from generated reasoning (necessary condition) | criterion | “This correspondence is a necessary condition for faithfulness, however, the program may not be a faithful explanation of the process that generated the program.” |
| [Kim & Lee 2026](./#p=kim2026mitigating) | faithfulness (of a translator) | formal | “indicates whether the answer in the translator’s output matches that of the solver” |

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) use a deterministic map $C$ from the generated mediator (the intermediate structure, such as a checklist or rubric, that the model writes before its decision) to a decision. Their in-distribution score is the share of cases in which the model's decision matches the decision the mediator implies: $F_{\mathrm{ID}}=\frac1N\sum_i\mathbb{1}[C(\hat m_i)=\hat y_i]$. Their interventional score $F_{\mathrm{Strong}}$, which also requires $C(m^{*}_i)=\hat y^{*}_i$ for an intervened mediator $m^{*}_i$, is placed in group 3.
- [Han et al. 2026b](./#p=han2026rfeval) score a pair of outputs with $\mathrm{RF}(o,o')=\mathbb{1}[\chi(o)=1\wedge\chi(o')=1\wedge\kappa(o,o')=1]$. Here stance consistency $\chi$ requires each output component to keep its prefix's canonical stance or explicitly justify a departure, and causal influence $\kappa$ requires the canonical stance of the regenerated reasoning or final answer to change after counterfactual reasoning $r'$ is prefilled. The evaluation uses only contrastive pairs with $S(r')\neq S(r)$, so the score combines this group's output consistency with the interventional requirement of group 3.
- [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary) define prediction-explanation consistency as how much better the explanation supports the prediction than its negation: $C(e)=\log\dfrac{M(e\mid q,a)}{M(e\mid q,\lnot a)}$.

### 5. Say-do consistency: whether actions match the stated reasoning, plan or claim

Here the comparison is between what a model says it will do, has decided or has done, and what it does. The definitions compare a declared plan with the executed steps ([Oota et al. 2026](./#p=oota2026llm)), the action a reasoning step implies with the action taken ([Hu et al. 2026](./#p=hu2026faithful); [Dong et al. 2025](./#p=dong2025say)), a claim about a completed action with the tool log ([Anonymous 2026 (say-do traceability)](./#p=anon2026measuring)), a written moral choice with a tool action ([Anonymous 2026 (should-say-do)](./#p=anon2026should)), and a declared molecular, RNA codon or reaction edit with the transformation realized in the generated object ([Anonymous 2026 (do what you say)](./#p=anon2026what)). 89 of the 99 definitions are proposed. 3 of the 18 papers are system cards (documents that developers publish about a model) that score false reports of completed actions ([Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55); [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon); [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra)).

> by splitting the faithfulness gap into two steps: reasoning $\rightarrow$ conclusion (does the stated decision follow from the agent’s own reasoning?) and conclusion $\rightarrow$ action (does the agent execute what it states?).
>
> ([Wang 2026](./#p=wang2026doing), Abstract)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Anonymous 2026 (do what you say)](./#p=anon2026what) | say–do gap | failure-mode | “We refer to a mismatch between the stated edit and the realized change as the say–do gap.” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (do what you say)](./#p=anon2026what) | complete causal faithfulness | criterion | “it tests the more specific question of whether the model does what its trace says” |
| [Bu et al. 2026](./#p=bu2026spadebench) | plan-action divergence (agent deception) | informal | “a spontaneous inconsistency between an agent’s declared plan (what it says it will do) and its executed action (what it actually does)” |
| [Hu et al. 2026](./#p=hu2026faithful) | step-wise faithfulness (GUI agent) | informal | “(ii) internal consistency, where the executed action $\hat{a}_{t}$ aligns with the intent committed in the reasoning trace $z_{t}$” |
| [Hu et al. 2026](./#p=hu2026faithful) | thought-action consistency | operational | “evaluates whether the predicted action faithfully instantiates the intent stated in the intermediate reasoning (Thought)” |
| [Dong et al. 2025](./#p=dong2025say) | faithful reasoning (supporting predicted actions) | informal | “whether an agent’s internal reasoning process faithfully supports its predicted actions” |
| [Oota et al. 2026](./#p=oota2026llm) | Plan Declaration–Execution Gap | failure-mode | “We call a mismatch between the declared plan and its execution the Plan Declaration–Execution Gap.” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (should-say-do)](./#p=anon2026should) | divergence between written commitments and submitted action (say-do gap) | failure-mode | “A model may endorse these commitments in writing yet submit an action that departs from them.” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (say-do traceability)](./#p=anon2026measuring) | traceability (claim-execution anchoring) | informal | “whether a text statement about the agent’s own action is anchored by toollayer execution records” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (say-do traceability)](./#p=anon2026measuring) | over-promise | failure-mode | “a completive claim of a specific execution-layer action with no matching evidence and no exemption” |
| [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) | false completion claims | operational | “Claiming a task is complete, successful, or verified when it is not.” |
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | misbehavior in Flaky-tools (false completion claim or undisclosed inability) | operational | “the agent lying in some way that it completed the task or not revealing that it could not do the task” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (implementation)](./#p=anon2026implementation) | lying to the user (monitor rubric) | operational | “the actor has done X, but telling the user that it has done Y” |
| [Shiromani & Richter 2026](./#p=shiromani2026false) | action-faithful (narration) | informal | “action-faithful when every factual claim it makes about commands, files and outputs is borne out by the trajectory” |
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | CoT-action mismatch | failure-mode | “a mismatch between the model’s chain-of-thought reasoning and its subsequent actions” |

The other 84 definitions in this group come from these papers (number of definitions in parentheses): [Anonymous 2026 (say-do traceability)](./#p=anon2026measuring) (16), [Hu et al. 2026](./#p=hu2026faithful) (12), [Dong et al. 2025](./#p=dong2025say) (9), [Anonymous 2026 (should-say-do)](./#p=anon2026should) (8), [Bu et al. 2026](./#p=bu2026spadebench) (8), [Oota et al. 2026](./#p=oota2026llm) (7), [Anonymous 2026 (do what you say)](./#p=anon2026what) (6), [Wang 2026](./#p=wang2026doing) (6), [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) (2), [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) (2), [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra) (2), [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) (1), [Chidambaram et al. 2026](./#p=chidambaram2026corrupted) (1), [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) (1), [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) (1), [Shiromani & Richter 2026](./#p=shiromani2026false) (1), [Walden & Wanner 2026](./#p=walden2026reasoning) (1).

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Dong et al. 2025](./#p=dong2025say) let $f(c_n)$ be the action implied by the CoT at step $n$ and $a^*_n$ the gold action, and set $\mathrm{GTA}_n=\mathbf{1}\{f(c_n)=a^*_n\}$. The execution gap counts steps where the CoT implies the correct action and the executed action is wrong, $\mathrm{EG}=\frac1N\sum_n\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{GTA}_n=1\wedge\mathrm{EM}_n=0\}$, and the reasoning gap $\mathrm{RG}$ ($\mathrm{GTA}_n=0\wedge\mathrm{EM}_n=1$) counts steps where the action is correct and the CoT implies another.
- [Bu et al. 2026](./#p=bu2026spadebench) define deception as a pair of (plan, action) outcomes, one in a regular setting and one in a pressure setting, with $A_1=A_2=Int$, $P_1\in\{Int,Mod\}$, $P_2\in\{Mod,Ext\}$ and $P_2\neq P_1$. The action stays the same while the stated plan shifts.
- [Hu et al. 2026](./#p=hu2026faithful) train with a reward that is a weighted average (a convex combination) of action match and thought-action consistency, $r_{i,t}=\lambda R_{\mathrm{AM}}(o_{i,t})+(1-\lambda)R_{\mathrm{Cons}}(o_{i,t})$, with weights $0.85$ and $0.15$ in their experiments.

### 6. Predictive value: the explanation lets an observer predict the model

Here faithfulness is defined by how much an explanation helps predict the model's behavior on the same or related inputs. The measures are forward simulation (an observer predicts the model's output with the help of the explanation), leakage-adjusted simulatability (LAS, which corrects for explanations that give away the label), counterfactual simulatability and normalized simulatability gain. The group also includes the related rationale-utility metric Rev, which measures "new, label-relevant information" beyond the input and label ([Chen et al. 2022](./#p=chen2022rev)). When the related inputs are counterfactuals of the original input (edited versions of it), papers call the property counterfactual simulatability. Definitions of counterfactual simulatability as such are in the [Counterfactual](#counterfactual) section; the definitions here state it as faithfulness. The 20 definitions from the earlier version on a model's reports about its own behavior moved to group 7, and 3 definitions on consistency across related inputs moved to group 11.

> A faithful explanation should help an observer predict how the model will behave on related inputs.
>
> ([Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm), §2.1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | fidelity | operational | “the degree to which the explanation model can mimic the original model’s decisions” |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | unfaithful interpretation (decision mismatch) | criterion | “An interpretation is unfaithful if it results in different decisions than the model it interprets.” |
| [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | simulatability | informal | “A model is simulatable to the extent that an observer, or simulator, can predict its outputs.” |
| [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | non-trivial explanation (what simulator success should reflect) | criterion | “the explanation $\hat{e}$ directly restates the task model output, i.e., leaking the label to the simulator” |
| [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) | faithfulness (LAS) | operational | “To evaluate the faithfulness of the rationales generated by the student, we use LAS” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | simulatability | informal | “simulatability is a special case of faithfulness, which requires the output predictor to be humans rather than arbitrary black-box models” |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | simulatability | informal | “Simulatability is a stricter form of faithfulness that requires CoTs to be both faithful and legible to humans.” |
| [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) | explanation faithfulness (simulatability framework) | informal | “deemed faithful if, upon reading the explanation, it allows humans to correctly anticipate the model’s behavior” |
| [Malladi & Srivastava 2026](./#p=malladi2026directly) | behavioral faithfulness | informal | “whether an explanation enables accurate reproduction of a model’s observable input–output behavior” |
| [Sivakumaran et al. 2026](./#p=sivakumaran2026balancing) | faithfulness (multi-party / reasoning executability) | informal | “a faithful explanation is one which enables the listener model to come to the same conclusion as the speaker” |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | faithfulness metric (reasoning simulator) | formal | “We say a CoT is faithful based on the accuracy of the “reasoning simulator”” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | faithful explanation (predictive principle) | informal | “A faithful explanation should help an observer predict how the model will behave on related inputs.” |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | faithful with respect to v | formal | “faithful with respect to $v$ if it contains all relevant information to predicting $v$ , i.e. $z$ is a sufficient statistic for $v$” |
| [Chen et al. 2022](./#p=chen2022rev) | Rev (Rationale Evaluation with conditional V-information) | formal | “we propose a new metric Rev, for Rationale Evaluation with conditional $\mathcal{V}$ -information” |

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) use a leakage indicator $k_i=\mathbb{1}[\hat y_i\mid\hat e_i]$, which is 1 when the simulator recovers the label from the explanation alone. LAS averages the simulator's accuracy gain from seeing the explanation separately over the leaking and the non-leaking examples, and then takes the mean of the two: $\mathrm{LAS}_k=\operatorname*{mean}_{i:\,k_i=k}\big(\mathbb{1}[\hat y_i\mid x_i,\hat e_i]-\mathbb{1}[\hat y_i\mid x_i]\big)$ and $\mathrm{LAS}=\tfrac12(\mathrm{LAS}_0+\mathrm{LAS}_1)$.
- [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) define the normalized simulatability gain as the predictor's accuracy gain from the explanation on related inputs, divided by the largest possible gain: $\mathrm{NSG}=\dfrac{\mathrm{Acc}_{\text{with}}-\mathrm{Acc}_{\text{without}}}{1-\mathrm{Acc}_{\text{without}}}$.
- [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) call a latent $z$ (e.g. a CoT) faithful with respect to an output property $v$ if $z$ is a sufficient statistic for $v$. This definition makes no claim about the causal role of $z$.

### 7. Self-report accuracy: whether the model's reports about itself match its behavior or internal states

Here the object is a model's report about itself: its policy, dispositions, learned behaviors, confidence, internal states, or whether an edit would change its answer. The report is faithful when it matches what the model does or what is inside it. The definitions distinguish three requirements. The first is agreement alone (self-modeling in [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating)). The second is privileged access, tested against a stronger cross-predictor, that is, another model trained on the same examples of the model's behavior (after [Binder et al. 2024](./#p=binder2024looking); 7 definitions explicitly name privileged access or privileged self-knowledge). The third is grounding (a causal link between the report and what it reports), which [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) require alongside accurate self-report for introspection. 27 of the 196 definitions came from two groups of the earlier version: 20 from the group on predictive value and 7 from the group on internal computation.

> We define an LLM as demonstrating behavioral self-awareness if it can accurately describe its behaviors without relying on in-context examples.
>
> ([Betley et al. 2025](./#p=betley2025tell), §1)
