|---|---|---|---|
| [Betley et al. 2025](./#p=betley2025tell) | behavioral self-awareness | informal | “if it can accurately describe its behaviors without relying on in-context examples” |
| [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) | faithfulness (of self-report) | informal | “An AI’s claims about itself should be accurate.” |
| [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) | grounding | informal | “Grounding requires reporting the true cause.” |
| [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) | correct confabulator | failure-mode | “a correct confabulator: accurately self-reporting through an ungrounded mechanism” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | self-modeling | informal | “A model succeeds at self-modeling whenever its self-reports agree with its observed input-output behavior” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspection routes)](./#p=anon2026introspection) | routes to an accurate self-report (introspection, self-simulation, post-hoc rationalization) | taxonomy | “a self-report can be accurate without being introspective” |
| [Blandfort & Pawar 2026](./#p=blandfort2026strangers) | generic or secondhand self-model | failure-mode | “Verbal testimony describes other models about as well as the self: a generic prior in first-person clothing.” |
| [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl) | faithful self-explanation | informal | “what the LM says about its behavior in one context and what it actually does in another” |
| [Sherburn et al. 2024](./#p=sherburn2024language) | faithful explanation | informal | “faithful if it accurately describes the model’s behavior on a sufficiently wide range of held out in-distribution and out-of-distribution examples” |
| [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) | privileged access | operational | “the target model is better explained by itself than by other explainer models” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) | honest report (vs. accurate self-knowledge) | criterion | “models can sincerely describe their actions incorrectly” |
| [Kutsyk & Zieliński 2026](./#p=kutsyk2026revealing) | hallucinated behavior (self-report) | failure-mode | “Hallucinated behavior: a distinctive behavioral claim that does not match the target.” |
| [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology) | explanation without metacognitive insight (addition) | failure-mode | “a capability which it does not have “metacognitive” insight into” |

The other 183 definitions in this group come from these papers (number of definitions in parentheses): [Blandfort & Pawar 2026](./#p=blandfort2026strangers) (20), [Kutsyk & Zieliński 2026](./#p=kutsyk2026revealing) (19), [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (16), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) (12), [Anonymous 2026 (introspection routes)](./#p=anon2026introspection) (10), [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl) (10), [Betley et al. 2025](./#p=betley2025tell) (8), [Nguyen et al. 2026a](./#p=nguyen2026can) (8), [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) (8), [Binder et al. 2024](./#p=binder2024looking) (7), [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) (7), [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) (6), [Song et al. 2025](./#p=song2025language) (6), [Anonymous 2026 (honesty adapters)](./#p=anon2026traininghonesty) (5), [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) (5), [Dietz et al. 2026](./#p=dietz2026split) (5), [Plunkett et al. 2025](./#p=plunkett2025self) (5), [Sherburn et al. 2024](./#p=sherburn2024language) (5), [Yoshida et al. 2026](./#p=yoshida2026introspecting) (5), [Shenoy et al. 2026](./#p=shenoy2026introspection) (4), [Goel et al. 2025](./#p=goel2025learning) (3), [Lee et al. 2026b](./#p=lee2026training) (2), [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) (1), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) (1), [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) (1), [Golchin & Wetter 2026](./#p=golchin2026monitorability) (1), [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (1), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) (1), [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) (1).

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) define a skill score: the agreement between self-reports and the model's own measured behavior, minus the score of the best dummy predictor (a trivial baseline) on the same model's targets, $\mathrm{skill}_t(M;\mathcal{D})=s_t(M;\mathcal{D})-b_t(M;\mathcal{D})$.
- [Nguyen et al. 2026a](./#p=nguyen2026can) define the recognition gap as the drop in how often a model claims a prior output as intended when that output was prefilled (written into the model's turn for it): $\Delta=P(\text{claim}\mid\text{control})-P(\text{claim}\mid\text{prefill})$.

### 8. Input importance: the explanation marks the inputs the prediction depends on

This group carries over feature-attribution tests from interpretability research before large language models. Comprehensiveness measures the loss in confidence after the marked inputs are removed, and sufficiency measures the change in confidence when only those inputs are kept ([DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser)). Attribution agreement compares the inputs that influence the prediction with those that influence the explanation ([Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring); [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring)), and the CC-SHAP measure of Parcalabescu & Frank measures self-consistency, a "necessary, but not sufficient test for faithfulness". Work on self-explanations edits the input according to the model's own explanation. [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) require an opposite label for counterfactual explanations, and an "unknown" classification after masking based on attributions or after redaction. [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) instead score all three styles by the fraction of edited inputs whose prediction changes. [Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness) state that erasure scores are valid only when the masked inputs are in-distribution. The CoT literature reaches this group mainly through the CC-SHAP of [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring): 15 of the 31 definitions that borrow from Parcalabescu & Frank are here.

> rationales extracted for an instance in this case ought to have meaningfully influenced its prediction for the same. We call these faithful rationales.
>
> ([DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser), §4.2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) | comprehensiveness | formal | “We construct a contrast example for $x_{i}$ , $\tilde{x}_{i}$ , which is $x_{i}$ with the predicted rationales $r_{i}$ removed.” |
| [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) | sufficiency | formal | “the degree to which the snippets within the extracted rationales are adequate for a model to make a prediction” |
| [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) | faithful by construction (hard rationale selection) | criterion | “We view these as inherently faithful, because by construction we know which snippets the decoder used to make a prediction.” |
| [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) | faithful explanation (attention) | criterion | “(i) Attention weights should correlate with feature importance measures (e.g., gradient-based measures)” |
| [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention) | faithful explanation (attention; exclusivity) | criterion | “if alternative attention distributions exist that produce similar results to those obtained by the original model” |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | unfaithful interpretation system (Model Assumption) | criterion | “An interpretation system is unfaithful if it results in different interpretations of models that make the same decisions.” |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | faithful heat-map interpretation (Linearity Assumption) | criterion | “the contributions of different parts of the input are independent from each other” |
| [Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness) | erasure-metric definition of importance | criterion | “if information (tokens) is truly important, then masking them should result in worse model performance compared to masking random information” |
| [Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness) | in-distribution validity of erasure metrics (MaSF test) | criterion | “Erasure-based metrics are only valid when the input masked according to the importance measure is in-distribution” |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | faithful redaction explanation | criterion | “The explanation is faithful if the model can not classify the redacted paragraph.” |
| [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) | faithful self-explanation (self-consistency check) | criterion | “a self-explanation is considered faithful if editing the input according to it indeed flips the prediction” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | unfaithfulness (perturbing unimportant features) | operational | “observe different answers on perturbing unimportant features identified by an explanation” |
| [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) | feature importance agreement | operational | “input tokens important for label prediction should be important for rationale generation and vice versa” |
| [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | faithful explanation (input-contribution condition) | criterion | “the tokens given as input to the model contribute similarly to the model’s answer prediction and to the explanation it generates” |
| [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) | CC-Sp (attribution-based self-consistency) | operational | “Spearman rank correlation captures agreement in feature prioritization while abstracting from the attribution scale” |

The other 84 definitions in this group come from these papers (number of definitions in parentheses): [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) (11), [Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness) (10), [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention) (9), [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) (8), [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) (7), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) (6), [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) (5), [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) (4), [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) (4), [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) (3), [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) (2), [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) (1), [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) (1), [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) (1), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) (1), [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) (1), [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (1), [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) (1), [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift) (1), [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) (1), [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (1), [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) (1), [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) (1), [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) (1), [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) (1), [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) (1).

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) let $m(\cdot)_j$ be the predicted probability of class $j$ and $r_i$ the rationale for $x_i$. Comprehensiveness is the drop in that probability when the rationale is removed, and sufficiency the drop when only the rationale is kept: $\mathrm{comprehensiveness}=m(x_i)_j-m(x_i\setminus r_i)_j$ and $\mathrm{sufficiency}=m(x_i)_j-m(r_i)_j$. [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) apply versions based on KL divergence to CoT tokens: $\mathrm{COMP}=\mathrm{KL}\big(p(Y\mid x,T)\,\|\,p(Y\mid x,T\setminus R)\big)$ and $\mathrm{SUFF}=\mathrm{KL}\big(p(Y\mid x,T)\,\|\,p(Y\mid x,R)\big)$.
- [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) compare $C(P)$ and $C(E)$, the per-token Shapley contribution ratios of the input to the prediction and to the explanation: $\text{CC-SHAP}=1-\mathrm{DIV}\big(C(P)\,\|\,C(E)\big)$. The authors call it a self-consistency measure, not a faithfulness measure.

### 9. Correspondence between the explanation and the model's internal computation

Here the target is correspondence between explanations and the model's internal computation, studied with probes, attribution graphs, circuits, sparse autoencoder (SAE) concepts and readouts of activations. [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) define a faithful step as one that "accurately describes a process that took place at some point in the model". [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) distinguish behavioral causal load-bearingness (whether the CoT causally matters for the answer) from "full mechanistic faithfulness". [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) offer graph comparisons and explicitly describe their evidence as correlational. [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) score the agreement between the use of a task strategy as a probe detects it and as the CoT states it (CIA, a macro F1 score). They call any disagreement between the two ($B_{\text{CoT}}\neq B_{\text{INT}}$) "parametric unfaithfulness", whether the CoT states a strategy that the probe shows unused or misreports a strategy used internally. [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) audit "places where the model’s internal representations differ from what it verbalized". Two definitions in [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology) concern the interpretability model, that is, the replacement model built to explain the network. Transcoders (the parts of the replacement model that stand in for the network's MLP layers) are not guaranteed to learn a "causally faithful model of the original MLP", and indirect effects that the attribution graph predicts but that are absent from the underlying model constitute "mechanistic unfaithfulness".

> A CoT step is faithful iff it accurately describes a process that took place at some point in the model.
>
> ([Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness), Def. 1 (§2.2))

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) | CoT step faithfulness | informal | “A CoT step is faithful iff it accurately describes a process that took place at some point in the model.” |
| [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) | CoT faithfulness | informal | “A CoT is faithful iff (1) it contains a complete reasoning path the model followed to reach its answer” |
| [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology) | chain-of-thought faithfulness (three cases) | taxonomy | “cases where the model genuinely performs the steps it says it is performing” |
| [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology) | mechanistic faithfulness (replacement model) | informal | “it is not guaranteed that they learn a causally faithful model of the original MLP” |
| [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) | full mechanistic faithfulness | informal | “full mechanistic faithfulness, which would require asking whether the CoT reflects the model’s internal computation” |
| [Wang et al. 2026c](./#p=wang2026verbalising) | CoT faithfulness | informal | “CoT is faithful when its visible rationale consistently reflects how reasoning-relevant information is computed in the model’s internal” |
| [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) | faithfulness as internal concept grounding | informal | “does an LLM’s CoT engage the same internal concepts that also support the LLM’s direct prediction” |
| [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) | NeuroFaith faithfulness | formal | “the proportion of concepts it contains being mechanistically important, i.e. having positive attribution score” |
| [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) | faithful reasoning trace | criterion | “faithful reasoning traces should align with the model’s internal computational process” |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) | faithful CoT (to internal representation) | criterion | “regardless of whether the answer is correct, since CIA measures faithfulness rather than correctness” |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) | parametric unfaithfulness (B_CoT=1, B_INT=0) | failure-mode | “the probe detects that the answer is generated by direct parametric recall rather than by summing the displayed partial products” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (computation alignment)](./#p=anon2026computation) | computation alignment | informal | “the computation process by which the student produces the final answer should be consistent with the problem-solving steps described in” |
| [Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable) | decodability (necessary condition for faithfulness) | criterion | “Decodability is a necessary condition for faithfulness” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) | behavioural CoT faithfulness measurement (limitation) | criterion | “cannot separate a model whose answer is supported by a different mechanism than its CoT suggests from one whose answer is simply robust to perturbation” |
| [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) | divergence between internal representations and verbalization (white-box investigator) | operational | “places where the model’s internal representations differ from what it verbalized” |

The other 92 definitions in this group come from these papers (number of definitions in parentheses): [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) (18), [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) (16), [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) (14), [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) (8), [Wang et al. 2026c](./#p=wang2026verbalising) (6), [Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable) (5), [Anonymous 2026 (computation alignment)](./#p=anon2026computation) (4), [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology) (4), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (4), [Boppana et al. 2026](./#p=boppana2026reasoning) (3), [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) (1), [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) (1), [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) (1), [Anwar et al. 2026b](./#p=anwar2026training) (1), [Basu & Chakraborty 2026](./#p=basu2026measuring) (1), [Bhupatiraju & Nyaupane 2026](./#p=bhupatiraju2026stated) (1), [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) (1), [Singhal & Kumar 2026](./#p=singhal2026believe) (1), [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) (1), [Sun et al. 2025](./#p=sun2026refine) (1).

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) define NeuroFaith for an explanation $e(x)$ that contains concepts $c_1,\dots,c_p$, using an interpreter attribution $I_\Gamma$ along circuit $\Gamma$. The score is the share of concepts with positive attribution: $F(x,e)=\frac1p\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{1}_{I_\Gamma(c_i)>0}$.
- [Anonymous 2026 (computation alignment)](./#p=anon2026computation) define the computation alignment of a sample $s$ as the node overlap between the attribution circuits traced from a token critical to the reasoning and from a token critical to the answer: $A(s)=\dfrac{|N^{*}_{r}\cap N^{*}_{y}|}{|N^{*}_{r}\cup N^{*}_{y}|}$.
- [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) remove from the direct-prediction pass the SAE concepts that it shares with the CoT pass, and measure the drop in answer probability: $\Delta p=P(\hat y\mid\mathbf r_{\text{pred}})-P(\hat y\mid\mathbf r'_{\text{pred}})$.

### 10. Honesty and dishonesty: statements against the model's own beliefs

Here a statement is judged against what the model itself believes or knows. A lie is a statement the model believes to be false ([Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating)). The MASK design (Ren et al. 2025, which is not in the map) reads the model's belief from a neutral prompt and reads dishonesty from a contradicting answer under pressure; 10 definitions from 5 papers adopt or adapt it. The group also holds confessions, which are self-reports given after the answer and trained and scored for honesty (29 definitions from [Joglekar et al. 2025](./#p=joglekar2025training)), and admissions under interrogation ([Li et al. 2025c](./#p=li2026spilling)). 122 of the 123 definitions define a related notion rather than faithfulness itself, and [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), [Sherburn et al. 2024](./#p=sherburn2024language) and [Walden & Wanner 2026](./#p=walden2026reasoning) tie these notions to faithfulness explicitly.

> We define an LLM-generated statement to be a lie if the LLM that generated the statement believes it to be false.
>
> ([Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating), Problem statement and testbed settings)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating) | lie (LLM-generated statement) | informal | “a lie if the LLM that generated the statement believes it to be false” |
| [Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating) | lie without deceptive intent (lie vs deception) | criterion | “our definition of lie does not require: Deceptive intent” |
| [Binder et al. 2024](./#p=binder2024looking) | honesty (honest model) | informal | “A language model is called honest if it accurately reports its beliefs and its level of confidence in its beliefs” |
| [Chang et al. 2026](./#p=chang2026catalyzing) | dishonest behaviour | informal | “outputting claims that contradict some knowledge or are not supported by what is accessible in context” |
| [Chang et al. 2026](./#p=chang2026catalyzing) | MASK honest / dishonest / ambiguous | operational | “it asserts what the model believes (honest), asserts the opposite (dishonest), or asserts neither, by hedging or refusing (ambiguous)” |
| [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) | deceptive (statement) | informal | “we call a statement deceptive when the model asserts what it does not itself hold true” |
| [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) | benign deception (not deception) | criterion | “Not all context-dependent non-literal behavior is deception.” |
| [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) | deception and dishonesty (white-box) | operational | “Holds a reason or judgment that contradicts what it presents, or knowingly leaves out something the reader needs to know” |
| [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) | honest vs deceptive response | informal | “code specifically designed to pass the visible test cases without solving the general problem” |
| [Joglekar et al. 2025](./#p=joglekar2025training) | confession | informal | “A confession is a model’s self-evaluation of its compliance with the letter and the spirit of any instructions or policies it was subject to.” |
| [Joglekar et al. 2025](./#p=joglekar2025training) | honest vs. accurate confession | criterion | “This is not to say that producing accurate confessions is an easy task, only honest ones.” |
| [Basani et al. 2026](./#p=basani2026brittle) | hallucinated confession | failure-mode | “it hallucinates a confession to a hidden objective it never actually followed” |
| [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer) | truthful (chain of reasoning) | informal | “truthful (the proof reflects the system’s beliefs)” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | dishonesty (unfaithful CoT as) | criterion | “unfaithful CoT explanations may be a form of model dishonesty, as opposed to a lack of capability” |
| [Sherburn et al. 2024](./#p=sherburn2024language) | honesty (explanations representing the model's beliefs) | informal | “This paper focuses on honesty, since we aim to elicit explanations representative of the model’s “beliefs”.” |

The other 108 definitions in this group come from these papers (number of definitions in parentheses): [Joglekar et al. 2025](./#p=joglekar2025training) (27), [Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating) (13), [Chang et al. 2026](./#p=chang2026catalyzing) (11), [Li et al. 2025c](./#p=li2026spilling) (11), [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) (7), [Basani et al. 2026](./#p=basani2026brittle) (5), [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) (4), [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) (4), [Anonymous 2026 (honesty adapters)](./#p=anon2026traininghonesty) (3), [Dietz et al. 2026](./#p=dietz2026split) (3), [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) (3), [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer) (3), [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) (3), [Walden & Wanner 2026](./#p=walden2026reasoning) (3), [Betley et al. 2025](./#p=betley2025tell) (1), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) (1), [Blandfort & Pawar 2026](./#p=blandfort2026strangers) (1), [Golchin & Wetter 2026](./#p=golchin2026monitorability) (1), [Kutsyk & Zieliński 2026](./#p=kutsyk2026revealing) (1), [Lee et al. 2026b](./#p=lee2026training) (1), [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) (1), [Shiromani & Richter 2026](./#p=shiromani2026false) (1).

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating) give a threshold rule for the Harm Pressure and Password Lock settings: an intervened model $M_H$ is honest on $x$ if $\mathrm{Acc}_{M_H}(x_p)\geq\mathrm{Acc}_M(x_n)-0.2$. The honesty rate is the fraction of such $x$ among those with $\mathrm{Acc}_M(x_n)\geq0.75$.
- [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) use a context $c^{+}$ that triggers deception and a neutral counterpart $c^{-}$ for the same question. A behavior is belief-stable deception when the model contradicts under $c^{+}$ a truth it reliably asserts under $c^{-}$.
- [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) define the training target as the parameters with the highest expected reward among those that are honest, $\arg\max_{\theta\in\Theta_H}\mathbb{E}_{x,y\sim\pi_\theta(x)}[R(x,y)]$, where $\Theta_H$ is the set of parameters whose policies produce honest responses with high probability.

### 11. Related notions defined by contrast with faithfulness

These 61 definitions contrast faithfulness with other properties or place it within broader frameworks. 32 concern plausibility, including convincingness to humans, factual correctness and logical soundness, and 22 concern consistency (2 concern both). 11 define or restate the self-consistency of [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring), stated through "output correspondences" or "input contribution correspondences", which Parcalabescu & Frank distinguish from faithfulness. 6 definitions of [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) concern consistency across related inputs. The remaining 9 definitions cover effectiveness, truthfulness, informativeness, transparency, explainability and interpretability, contrasts with monitorability and with mechanistic relevance, human ratings of rationales, and rationalizations, which [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) say "should not be used to explain the model". Definitions of monitorability itself are in the [Monitorability](#monitorability) section.

> “Plausibility” refers to how convincing the interpretation is to humans, while “faithfulness” refers to how accurately it reflects the true reasoning process of the model Herman (2017); Wiegreffe and Pinter (2019).
>
> ([Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards), §2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | plausibility | informal | ““Plausibility” refers to how convincing the interpretation is to humans” |
| [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | plausibility | informal | “plausibility (a.k.a. persuasiveness or understandability), which refers to “how convincing an explanation is to humans”” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | plausibility | informal | “Plausibility evaluates humans’ preference of an explanation based on its factual correctness and logical coherence” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | plausibility (of self-explanations) | informal | “An explanation is considered plausible if it is coherent with human reasoning and understanding.” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | plausible (vs unfaithful) | informal | “may be coherent and consistent with its prediction on that example (in which case we call it plausible)” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | plausibility (necessary condition) | criterion | “the plausibility of explanations, which is necessary for faithfulness, but as we demonstrate, is insufficient for establishing it” |
| [Höth et al. 2026](./#p=hoth2026atmanrl) | plausibility | informal | “plausibility reflects consistency with an external evaluator, not alignment with the model’s internal computation” |
| [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | self-consistency (relation to faithfulness) | criterion | “Yet self-consistency is a necessary, but not sufficient test for faithfulness.” |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | self-consistency | criterion | “any “faithfulness” test which relies on executing changes to the model’s input and observing changes to the prediction actually measures self-consistency rather than faithfulness” |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | self-consistency | informal | “Perturbing the reasoning chain while maintaining model parameters fixed measures self-consistency” |
| [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) | effectiveness (vs. faithfulness) | informal | “Effectiveness is result-oriented, focusing on whether CoT can enhance the quality of reasoning outcomes” |
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) | faithfulness (contrasted with monitorability) | informal | “without requiring its reasoning to causally reflect the underlying decision-making, as faithfulness does” |
| [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) | faithfulness (vs. mechanistic relevance) | informal | “CoT text can causally impact behavior even if it does not straightforwardly “explain” model internals” |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) | plausibility-based evaluation | informal | “This turns the metric into plausibility-based evaluation by enforcing a priority among decision factors.” |
| [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) | model faithfulness (as instance-level consistency) | taxonomy | “This includes model invariances and equivariances, encompassing robustness to spurious cues, bias, sycophancy, and model faithfulness.” |

The other 46 definitions in this group come from these papers (number of definitions in parentheses): [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) (5), [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) (4), [Ramnath et al. 2023](./#p=ramnath2024tailoring) (4), [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) (3), [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) (3), [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention) (3), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) (2), [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) (1), [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp) (1), [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) (1), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) (1), [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded) (1), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) (1), [Chen et al. 2022](./#p=chen2022rev) (1), [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) (1), [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) (1), [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) (1), [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) (1), [Han et al. 2026b](./#p=han2026rfeval) (1), [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith) (1), [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) (1), [Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing) (1), [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) (1), [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) (1), [Viteri et al. 2024](./#p=viteri2026markovian) (1), [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) (1), [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) (1), [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) (1), [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary) (1).

Mathematical definitions in this group:
- [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) place model faithfulness among instance-level consistency constraints, which relate the model's input-output pairs to one another without any of them describing the others: invariance (the output stays the same when the input is transformed), $f(g(x))=f(x)$, and equivariance (the output changes in a matching way), $f(g(x))=g'(f(x))$, for input and output transformations $g$ and $g'$.

### Lineage: where the borrowed definitions come from

711 of the 1,955 definitions are borrowed (483 adopted, 228 adapted), and they come from 179 papers. 512 of them cite at least one source in the map, 198 cite only sources outside it, and one names no source. For the most-borrowed sources, the table counts the definitions and the papers that borrow from each source (a definition that cites several sources counts once for each), and the groups that the borrowing definitions belong to. The last column uses short names for the groups: umbrella (group 1, the general definition), disclosure (2), causal dependence (3), agreement (4), prediction (6), self-report (7), input importance (8), mechanism (9, internal computation), honesty (10) and contrast (11).

| Source | Borrowing definitions | Borrowing papers | In the map | Groups of the borrowing definitions |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | 102 | 76 | yes | disclosure 58, umbrella 33, causal dependence 5, input importance 2, mechanism 2, agreement 1, contrast 1 |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | 127 | 70 | yes | causal dependence 83, umbrella 32, disclosure 9, mechanism 1, agreement 1, prediction 1 |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | 79 | 55 | yes | umbrella 53, contrast 14, prediction 4, causal dependence 3, input importance 2, mechanism 2, disclosure 1 |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | 91 | 48 | yes | disclosure 74, umbrella 14, causal dependence 1, contrast 1, mechanism 1 |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | 23 | 21 | yes | disclosure 10, umbrella 8, causal dependence 3, contrast 1, agreement 1 |
| [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | 31 | 20 | yes | input importance 15, contrast 8, umbrella 5, mechanism 2, causal dependence 1 |
| [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | 25 | 18 | yes | disclosure 18, umbrella 6, contrast 1 |
| [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | 14 | 14 | yes | agreement 7, umbrella 4, causal dependence 2, contrast 1 |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | 15 | 11 | yes | prediction 9, contrast 3, umbrella 3 |
| Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 | 12 | 11 | no | prediction 10, umbrella 1, input importance 1 |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | 15 | 10 | yes | causal dependence 9, umbrella 4, honesty 1, mechanism 1 |
| [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | 12 | 9 | yes | prediction 12 |
| [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) | 11 | 9 | yes | prediction 5, umbrella 3, input importance 3 |
| Atanasova et al. 2023 | 10 | 8 | no | umbrella 4, disclosure 4, contrast 1, input importance 1 |

| Ren et al. 2025 | 10 | 5 | no | honesty 10 |

Four sources are each borrowed by 48 or more papers, and most of each one's borrowing definitions fall in one group: [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) (58 of 102 definitions) and [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) (74 of 91) feed group 2, [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) (83 of 127) group 3, and [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) (53 of 79) group 1. Among the groups that are new in this version, group 7 contains 8 of the 9 definitions that borrow from [Binder et al. 2024](./#p=binder2024looking), and group 10 contains all 10 definitions that borrow from MASK (Ren et al. 2025). In group 5, each borrowed source is cited by only one borrowing paper within the group.

The paragraphs below also report the verdicts of a check of borrowed definitions against the sources they cite in the map, which the next section describes. For the faithfulness definitions, the check gives one of six verdicts: supported (the source gives this definition), adapted (the source gives it in another form), renamed (the source defines the same thing under another name), partial (the source supports only part of it), unsupported (the source does not give it) and other-claim (the cited passage supports a different claim).

**Jacovi & Goldberg's sentence and its paraphrases.** 65 definitions from 54 papers cite [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) outside group 11, where 14 more definitions cite them for a related term. Jacovi & Goldberg word the definition in two ways, "accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction" and "how accurately it reflects the true reasoning process of the model". 21 of the 65 definitions keep "reasoning process" and 8 keep "accurately represent", and the words that replace them in the others point toward different groups:

- toward internal computation: "the reasoning accurately describe the models internal computation" ([Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient)), "the degree to which its reasoning traces reflect its internal decision-making" ([Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training));
- toward behavior: "explanations should reflect how the model actually behaves, not just sound plausible" ([Malladi & Srivastava 2026](./#p=malladi2026directly)), "whether explanations support actual model behavior" ([Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted));
- toward causal role: "the degree to which a model’s stated reasoning genuinely causes its final prediction, rather than merely accompanying it" ([Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking));
- toward factors: "An explanation is faithful if it reflects the factors behind a model’s prediction" ([Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one)).

[Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) adapt the sentence in two ways at once (the check marks it adapted). They relax it to "the key decision factors and reasoning chains", and they add that these should "enable us to predict how it would process similar inputs during deployment". Their operational test uses only the first part.

**Plausibility drifts from persuasiveness to correctness.** For Jacovi & Goldberg, plausibility is "how convincing the interpretation is to humans", and [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) keep that sense ("a.k.a. persuasiveness or understandability"). [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) write instead that "Plausibility evaluates humans’ preference of an explanation based on its factual correctness and logical coherence", citing Herman 2017, Lage et al. 2019 and Jacovi & Goldberg. The same sentence recurs in [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent). [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) have "the factual correctness of a CoT and how convincingly it justifies the model’s response", and [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded) reduce plausibility to whether "the explanations are logically sound". The check marks these four restatements partial against Jacovi & Goldberg. Defining plausibility by logical soundness, as [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded) do, brings it closer to the definitions of group 4 that require valid reasoning, although [Xu et al. 2024](./#p=xu2024faithful) additionally require that the answer be correct: an instance is "’faithful’ if both the answer and the process are correct and logical".

**Lanham et al.'s tests become verdicts on single traces.** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) report early answering (truncating the CoT and asking for the answer) and adding mistakes as task-level measures of post-hoc reasoning. They read them with a hedge ("the model is likely not ignoring the CoT") and state that "Reasoning not being post-hoc does not guarantee faithfulness, nor does being post-hoc exclude faithfulness." Papers that borrow the tests apply them to single traces: [Tanneru et al. 2024](./#p=tanneru2024hardness) compute the area over the curve for each instance, and [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) write "If the prediction of the model changes, the CoT is considered faithful". The check marks these restatements adapted and supported. The filler-tokens test changes its question. Lanham et al. ask whether filler accounts for the CoT's accuracy gain ("If the filler tokens provide a significant performance improvement, then the CoT may be unfaithful"), while [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) call a CoT "unfaithful if this corruption does not change the original prediction", and the check marks this partial. Nine definitions in group 2 cite [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), and the check marks 4 supported, 3 partial and 2 other-claim. The three partial definitions extend Lanham et al.'s findings to omitted decision factors. The two other-claim checks find citations that support general statements about unfaithfulness, placed next to descriptions of cue tests that follow; Lanham et al. intervene on the CoT, not on cues in the input.

**Cue tests: from counterfactual simulatability to verbalization rates.** [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) framed unfaithfulness predictively, as reasoning that can be "misleading about how the system will make predictions on other examples (in which case we also call it unfaithful)". They measured it by drops in accuracy and by pairs of predictions that change when the biasing feature is added. [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) made verbalization itself the score and required both mention and dependence, and [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) required the CoT to "describe the switching effect of the cue". Later papers split the requirement. [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) score "the presence of the given cue in a model’s reasoning trace". [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) calls a chain faithful "when an LLM monitor can tell from it that the hint influenced the answer"; the check marks this definition adapted from Chen et al., and marks partial Little's statement "This is the monitor setup of Chen et al. (2025)", since Chen et al. score an abrupt switch to the hint answer as not verbalized. [Walden & Wanner 2026](./#p=walden2026reasoning) turn Chen et al.'s score into an "intent-based honesty score". [Young 2026a](./#p=young2026measuring) concludes that the resulting numbers form "a family of related constructs whose values depend critically on the measurement instrument".

**Simulatability becomes a faithfulness score.** Forward simulation (Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017, not in the map) motivates the leakage-adjusted simulatability of [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted), which [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott), [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) and [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) use to evaluate the faithfulness of rationales. [Chen et al. 2022](./#p=chen2022rev) instead compare LAS and RQ with their proposed Rev metric, which measures new, label-relevant information in rationales. [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) place simulatability under faithfulness, as "a special case of faithfulness, which requires the output predictor to be humans", and [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) call it "a stricter form of faithfulness". The normalized simulatability gain of [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) is adopted by [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl), [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) and [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient).

**Self-consistency and "parametric faithfulness".** [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) argue that tests that perturb the input or the CoT measure self-consistency, "a necessary, but not sufficient test for faithfulness". [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) use the argument to motivate parametric faithfulness, an intervention on the weights (unlearning a reasoning step), which [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) and [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) adopt. [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) keep both words but change both meanings. The check marks their "parametric faithfulness" partial against Chen et al., Hase & Potts and Zaman & Srivastava, and their self-consistency based on sampling partial against Parcalabescu & Frank and Tutek et al.

**Introspection: privileged access after Binder et al.** [Binder et al. 2024](./#p=binder2024looking) define introspection as accessing facts about the model that "cannot be derived (logically or inductively) from their training data alone", and test it by cross-prediction (comparing the model's predictions about itself with those of another model trained on its behavior). 7 papers borrow from them, and the check marks 4 of the 9 borrowings supported, 4 adapted and 1 other-claim. The reference model changes along the way. [Song et al. 2025](./#p=song2025language) compare against "another model with nearly identical internal knowledge", [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) claim that "a model can model its own behavior better than its training target", and [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) define self-modeling by agreement alone and keep the comparison across models as a separate test of introspection. The "behavioral self-awareness" of [Betley et al. 2025](./#p=betley2025tell) is adopted by [Kutsyk & Zieliński 2026](./#p=kutsyk2026revealing), [Plunkett et al. 2025](./#p=plunkett2025self) and [Shenoy et al. 2026](./#p=shenoy2026introspection).

**Honesty: lies relative to the model's belief from MASK, confessions from Joglekar et al.** Ren et al. 2025 (MASK) supply the test of honesty relative to the model's own belief to [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55), [Blandfort & Pawar 2026](./#p=blandfort2026strangers), [Chang et al. 2026](./#p=chang2026catalyzing), [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) and [Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating). The confessions of [Joglekar et al. 2025](./#p=joglekar2025training) are borrowed by four papers. The check marks partial the description by [Anonymous 2026 (honesty adapters)](./#p=anon2026traininghonesty) of a confession as a field that "each assistant response also carries", because Joglekar et al. request the confession as a separate output after the answer.

### Checking borrowed definitions against their sources

This section reports the check of borrowed definitions against the sources they cite. Of the 512 borrowed definitions that cite at least one source in the map, 511 were checked against their sources. There are 718 checks, one for each pair of a checked definition and a source in the map that it cites; they come from 161 papers and concern 69 sources. The cue-acknowledgment judge rubric of [Nguyen et al. 2026b](./#p=nguyen2026generalization) cites [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) but has no check. The 198 borrowed definitions that cite only sources outside the map were not checked. Across all borrowed definitions, the external source cited most often is Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 (12 definitions), followed by Atanasova et al. 2023, Hase & Bansal 2020 and Ren et al. 2025 (10 definitions each) and Barez et al. 2025 (8). The table gives the number of checks with each verdict and what each verdict means.

| Verdict | Checks | Meaning |
|---|---:|---|
| supported | 429 | the source gives the definition as the citing paper states it |
| adapted | 151 | the source gives it and the citing paper changes it |
| partial | 94 | the source supports part of what is attributed to it |
| renamed | 23 | the source gives the notion under another name |
| other-claim | 11 | the citation supports a different claim than the definition |
| unsupported | 10 | the source does not give it |
| total | 718 | |

580 of the 718 checks (81%) find the definition in the cited source, either as stated (429) or adapted (151). The other 138 checks concern 121 definitions from 79 papers. The verdicts support five conclusions.

1. **Ten checks find that the source does not give the definition credited to it (unsupported).** [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) are credited three times. [Sanyal et al. 2022](./#p=sanyal2022fairr) credit them for whether models are "actually learning to solve the task or rather depending on some shortcut patterns". [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) credit them for a causal definition (stated reasoning that "genuinely causes its final prediction"). [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) credit them for the finding that CoTs "often omit key steps or fabricate plausible but untrue rationales", which Jacovi & Goldberg's 2020 opinion piece predates. The other seven fall on seven sources, once each: [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) for [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness)'s simulatability metric, [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) for [Yu et al. 2026](./#p=yu2026outcome)'s perturbation test, [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) for a simulatability evaluation ([Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm)), [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) for training a CoT to acknowledge a cue only when it mattered ([Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would)), [Boppana et al. 2026](./#p=boppana2026reasoning) for a sense of "reasoning theater" that Boppana et al. do not use ([Nguyen et al. 2026b](./#p=nguyen2026generalization)), [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) for edits of the CoT ([Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking)) and [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary) for consistency across samples ([Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making)).
2. **Partial verdicts are concentrated on the most-borrowed sources.** Partial verdicts make up 18 of the 127 checks on [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), 14 of the 91 on [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), 11 of the 102 on [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) and 10 of the 79 on [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards). Besides the evidence on omitted factors credited to Lanham et al. and the restatements of plausibility credited to Jacovi & Goldberg (see the Lineage section), two papers turn a remark of Chen et al. about individual settings into an overall rate: [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc) ("in less than 20% of samples") and [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) ("less than $20\%$ of the time"). Chen et al. say that the reveal rate is "often below 20%" in individual settings, while their overall scores are "25% for Claude 3.7 Sonnet and 39% for DeepSeek R1". [Imrankhan 2026](./#p=imrankhan2026better) credits Turpin et al. with an "Unfaithful CoT Rate" that Turpin et al. do not define.
3. **Two citing papers account for 15 of the 138 checks with other verdicts.** [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) have 8 of them: they attach a faithful-if-the-answer-changes reading to Lanham et al.'s filler test (2 partial), rename the hint test (2 renamed, see point 4), describe Admoni et al.'s post-hoc attribution test as a CoT test (2 partial), and receive one partial each on Turpin et al. and [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal). [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) have 7 and misattribute both of their terms. Hong et al.'s "parametric faithfulness" is partial against [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) (twice), [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) and [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought), because the term comes from [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring), where it means an intervention on the weights. Their self-consistency "across multiple samples or under paraphrase" is partial against [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) and Tutek et al. and unsupported against [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary). The quoted definitions keep Hong et al.'s wording, so the error is theirs, not an error made when the definitions were collected.
4. **23 checks find that the source gives the notion under another name (renamed).** [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating) name their product Scheming and credit [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching), but they use an unconditional verbalization rate, where Turpin et al.'s "effective cue influence rate (ECR)" conditions verbalization on cue influence. [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) call Emmons et al.'s necessity condition a "faithfulness floor". [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) give the hint test of Chen et al. and Chua & Evans the label "Biasing Features", which is Turpin et al.'s. [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) call the simulation precision of [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) "explanation consistency".
5. **How the stance labels compare with the verdicts.** Of the 552 checks on definitions labeled adopted when the definitions were collected, 21 come back adapted and 18 renamed: in these cases the paper presents a definition as borrowed while changing it or its name. Of the 166 checks on definitions labeled adapted, 14 come back supported.

An earlier version of this page recorded ten mismatches between borrowed definitions and their sources. The verdicts above replace those ten records: 1 is now unsupported ([Sanyal et al. 2022](./#p=sanyal2022fairr)), 6 partial, 2 renamed ([Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training), [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating)) and 1 supported ([Höth et al. 2026](./#p=hoth2026atmanrl), whose contrast between plausible and unfaithful explanations [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) state). These verdicts agree with an independent check of the earlier version by another model (GPT-6.1 Sol) for Yang et al. (renamed) and for Hong et al.'s self-consistency definition (partial). They go further than that check for Höth et al., which it judged "partial support with an added causal specialization". The attribution of [Lobo et al. 2024](./#p=lobo2024impact) to Lanham et al. is partial: Lanham et al. contrast reasoning that drives the answer with post-hoc reasoning, but state that post-hoc reasoning neither guarantees nor excludes faithfulness. The earlier version left one case unsettled, the readings of Lanham et al.'s tests on single traces by [Tanneru et al. 2024](./#p=tanneru2024hardness) and [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring); it is now settled as adapted and supported.

<details><summary>All 138 checks with verdicts other than supported or adapted</summary>

For each check, the table gives the citing paper with the identifier of its definition in the full table of definitions, the cited source, the verdict and what the check found.

| Citing paper and definition ID | Cited source | Verdict | What the check found |
|---|---|---|---|

| [Dhoot & Hao 2026](./#p=dhoot2026hintfollowing) `dhoot2026hintfollowing#2` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | other-claim | Chen et al. are cited only for the metadata hint (Table 1); the follow-rate definition is the post's own, although Chen measures the analogous rate of switching to the hint answer as hint usage. |

[51]) are cited only for indirect hints that need intermediate computation, which matches their "complex hint"; the faithful/unfaithful labeling rule, including counting misattribution to a made-up source as unfaithful, is the citing paper's own. |
| [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning) `macdermott2025reasoning#5` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | other-claim | Lanham is cited for the finding that CoT reasoning is often unfaithful, which it supports; the hint-based measurement described in the next sentence does not describe Lanham, which uses no hints and measures faithfulness by editing the CoT. |
| [Plunkett et al. 2025](./#p=plunkett2025self) `plunkett2025self#2` | [Binder et al. 2024](./#p=binder2024looking) | other-claim | In the quoted passage Binder et al. are cited only for averaging several independent responses to get a stable estimate (Binder sample 20 times per prompt and average them), not for comparing reported attribute weights with learned weights, which is Plunkett et al.'s own measure. |
| [Plunkett et al. 2025](./#p=plunkett2025self) `plunkett2025self#2` | [Betley et al. 2025](./#p=betley2025tell) | other-claim | In the quoted passage Betley et al. are cited only for averaging over repeated prompts (they use 10 paraphrases per evaluation question and aggregate the scores), not for comparing reported attribute weights with learned weights, which is Plunkett et al.'s own measure. |
| [Senatorov et al. 2026](./#p=senatorov2026structural) `senatorov2026structural#2` | [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | other-claim | The source never describes reasoning traces as post-hoc rationalizations; it supplies the empirical finding (low indirect effects) that LLMs do not reliably use their intermediate reasoning steps, which is what the citation can support. |
| [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) `shiromani2026necessary#3` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | other-claim | The Turpin citation backs the preceding sentence (models can be influenced by factors they do not report), not the uncited characterization of prior tests, though Turpin et al.'s test does combine both checks: a change in the answer and whether the bias is named. |
| [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) `wiegreffe2021measuring#6` | [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | other-claim | Wiegreffe et al. cite Hase et al. (2020) not for the IR→Ô − I→Ô definition but for automated simulators correlating with human scores; the source reports a moderate model–expert correlation for example-level LAS (ρ = 0.29) and, in the crowdsourced study, that raw simulator accuracies (not LAS) correlate with ratings. |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) `zaman2025chainofthought#10` | [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | other-claim | Chua and Evans are cited for the finding that unfaithfulness rates of 80% or more agree with prior work, which their results for non-reasoning models support (best non-reasoning model articulates the professor cue 13% and the black squares 3% of the time); the rate itself is the complement of the source's articulation rate among switched answers. |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) `agarwal2024faithfulness#7` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. do perturb features the explanation relies on (the BBQ weak evidence, §4 'Perturbing Input Features Referenced by Explanations'), but they do not score unfaithfulness as the share of unchanged answers: a pair answered Unknown twice counts as faithful, a pair answered Unknown only once counts as unfaithful (79% of the stereotype-aligned unfaithful pairs they annotate), and their main metric is the share of unfaithful pairs that are stereotype-aligned. |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) `agarwal2024faithfulness#9` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham does insert mistakes, read an unchanged answer as post-hoc reasoning and report an AOC, but it inserts a single mistake into one step and regenerates the rest; it never adds 'more mistakes' or plots a 'fraction of mistakes', so the curve Agarwal describes is not Lanham's. |
| [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) `alon2026faithfulness#6` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. state the criterion as described (after a hint switches the answer, the CoT counts as faithful only if it acknowledges using the hint), but they did not introduce the hint-intervention setup: they say their prompt pairs are inspired by Turpin et al. (2023) and Chua and Evans (2025). |
| [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) `anon2026grace#4` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham did introduce an area-over-the-curve (AOC) metric, but it computes a separate AOC for early answering and for adding mistakes, truncating after every sentence; it never aggregates sub-protocols into one composite, never truncates at 25/50/75%, and never calls it 'area over completeness'. |
| [Anonymous 2026 (honesty adapters)](./#p=anon2026traininghonesty) `anon2026traininghonesty#5` | [Joglekar et al. 2025](./#p=joglekar2025training) | partial | Joglekar et al. do reward confessions only for honesty, using a judge that sees the transcript and tool calls but not the CoT, but a confession is not a field carried by each response: it is a separate output requested by a system message after the answer, and in training it is requested only on a fraction p of rollouts. |
| [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) `bhan2025did#6` | [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) | partial | The source does compute separate gradient attributions for the label and the rationale, but it computes no correlation between them: it occludes the top-k% tokens of one attribution and checks that the other output degrades more than under random occlusion, and it treats such agreement only as a necessary condition, not a graded faithfulness score. |
| [Bu et al. 2026](./#p=bu2026spadebench) `bu2026spadebench#3` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. are a study of faithfulness, but they do not test whether reasoning traces reflect final outputs: they call reasoning that is consistent with its prediction merely 'plausible' and show that such reasoning is still unfaithful when it omits the bias behind the prediction. |
| [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded) `cahlik2025reasoninggrounded#2` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source supports listing plausibility beside faithfulness and readability, but it defines plausibility as how convincing an interpretation is to humans, not as logical soundness, which it never mentions. |
| [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) `cheah2026training#2` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source distinguishes faithfulness from plausibility (how convincing an interpretation is to humans), which supports 'plausible explanations may simply sound correct', but it never mentions truthfulness, so the faithfulness-truthfulness distinction credited jointly to it and Atanasova et al. (2023) is not in this source. |
| [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) `chen2025consistent#1` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source supports plausibility and faithfulness as separable criteria (cited for both in the next sentences), but not consistency as a third metric orthogonal to faithfulness: its Corollary 2 treats different explanations for similar inputs and outputs as evidence of unfaithfulness. |
| [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) `chen2025consistent#3` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source supports plausibility as a matter of human judgement (how convincing an interpretation is to humans), but it never grounds plausibility in factual correctness or logical coherence, terms it does not use. |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) `chen2025reasoning#6` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source supports the convincingness part ('how convincing the interpretation is to humans'), but it never ties plausibility to the factual correctness of an explanation. |
| [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) `chuang2026faithlm#1` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. support the hint-intervention paradigm Chuang et al. cite, but they did not introduce it (they credit Turpin et al. 2023 and Chua and Evans 2025), and they do not define faithfulness as prediction sensitivity: their score is whether the CoT verbalizes a hint that changed the answer, with the change used only to select cases. |
| [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) `chuang2026faithlm#6` | [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | partial | The source supports CoT–answer mismatch as evidence of unfaithfulness, but not fidelity being defined by CoT–answer alignment: it defines faithfulness (which it also calls fidelity) as accurately reflecting how the model reaches the answer, and says the absence of any chain–answer inconsistency does not make a chain faithful. |
| [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) `chuang2026faithlm#6` | [Radhakrishnan et al. 2023](./#p=radhakrishnan2023question) | partial | Radhakrishnan et al. never use 'fidelity' and define faithfulness as reflecting the model's actual reasoning (after Jacovi & Goldberg); only their perturbation tests check whether the answer depends on the stated reasoning, while their biasing-context tests check for unverbalized influences, which CoT-answer alignment does not cover. |
| [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc) `cox2026posthoc#17` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. measure how often CoTs reveal the hints they use, but they do not report a rate below 20%: their overall scores are 25% (Claude 3.7 Sonnet) and 39% (DeepSeek R1), and 'often below 20%' describes individual settings, computed over examples where the hint is used, not over all samples. |
| [Cui et al. 2026](./#p=cui2026behavior) `cui2026behavior#3` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. (reference [37]) show unfaithful CoTs, but their unfaithfulness is reasoning that omits the bias behind the answer, not reasoning misaligned with the answer: 73% of the unfaithful explanations they annotate support the predicted answer, and misalignment appears only within the remaining 27%. |
| [Duzan & Stickland 2026](./#p=duzan2026chainofthought) `duzan2026chainofthought#28` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham does find that faithfulness varies by task and gets worse with model capability, but its tests measure whether the answer depends on the CoT, not whether models verbalize the factors driving their predictions, and it says post-hoc reasoning need not be unfaithful; presenting its result as evidence of failure to verbalize driving factors is not supported. |
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) `ferreira2026outofsight#5` | [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | partial | Baker et al. do say monitorability does not need causal CoTs, but they explicitly define faithfulness without any causal requirement (a sufficient statistic for the monitored property), so the clause 'as faithfulness does' contradicts their definition. |
| [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) `ferreira2026truthful#1` | [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | partial | The source supports that LLM self-explanations are often unfaithful and can create unsupported confidence, but not this claim about reasoning chains: it tests only counterfactual, feature-attribution and redaction explanations and evaluates no chain-of-thought. |
| [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) `gema2026chainofthought#1` | [Emmons et al. 2025a](./#p=emmons2025chain) | partial | Emmons et al. support only the narrower premise that reasoning appears in the CoT when it is needed to do the task; they reject faithful recording as the basis of monitoring and expect simple decisions such as following a cue to be unverbalized rationalization, so the faithfulness assumption, including recording decisions to act on a preference, is not theirs. |
| [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) `gema2026chainofthought#2` | [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | partial | The source does score switched answers whose CoT leaves the cue out as unfaithful, but its criterion is not a mention: its judge counts a CoT only if it states that the cue made the model pick the answer and excludes repeating the cue, so the case the paper says passes verbalization (mentioning the cue once, then endorsing it without recording the decision to act on it) is not an articulation under the source's test. |
| [Golchin & Wetter 2026](./#p=golchin2026monitorability) `golchin2026monitorability#4` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham supports that CoT can be unfaithful, but not the 'omitting the factors that drive the answer' failure: its evidence of unfaithfulness is answers that do not depend on the CoT, and its filler-token test found no evidence of unstated computation; omission is shown by co-cited Turpin, Chen and Arcuschin. |
| [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) `gurarieh2026faithfulness#25` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham does replace the whole CoT with filler tokens over a sweep of lengths, but it reports accuracy, not answer preservation, and uses the test only to ask whether extra compute alone yields the CoT gain; the answer-preservation scoring attributed to it is Gurarieh's. |
| [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) `gurarieh2026faithfulness#29` | [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | partial | Chen et al. support a simulator predicting the model's answer from its explanation, but not this metric's reproduction of the original answer on the same input, which Chen et al. reject because the explanation can leak the output there; their measure scores counterfactual inputs. |
| [Han et al. 2026b](./#p=han2026rfeval) `han2026rfeval#16` | [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | partial | The source supports the definition (verbalized reasoning that misrepresents the answer-producing process while looking plausible) but not 'frequently' for reasoning models: it reports Implicit Post-Hoc Rationalization in 0.37% of question pairs for DeepSeek R1 and 0.04% for Sonnet 3.7 with thinking, and calls its cases 'real, though rare'. |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) `hong2026making#1` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. assess, through injected hints, how well a CoT reflects internal reasoning, but they call this CoT faithfulness and never parametric faithfulness, a term from Tutek et al. (2025) (tutek2025measuring) for interventions on model parameters, which they contrast with context interventions. |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) `hong2026making#1` | [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | partial | Hase and Potts aim for CoTs that reflect the model's underlying reasoning and test this with cue (hint) counterfactuals, but they never call the property 'parametric faithfulness'; that term comes from Tutek et al. (tutek2025measuring). |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) `hong2026making#2` | [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | partial | The source supports a self-consistency category distinct from faithfulness to inner workings, but not the paper's definition of it: the source's self-consistency is agreement between a model's answer and its explanation, tested by input edits or by comparing input contributions, never consistency across multiple samples or under paraphrase, and the source files the hint-injection test (Biasing Features) under self-consistency, whereas the paper counts hint injection as parametric faithfulness. |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) `hong2026making#2` | [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | partial | Tutek et al. supply the two-way split and the term parametric faithfulness, but their self-consistency is the output's sensitivity to perturbing the reasoning chain with parameters fixed, not consistency across samples or paraphrases, and they hold that parametric faithfulness can only be measured by intervening on parameters, which the hint tests the paper files under it do not do. |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) `hong2026making#5` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. do check whether the CoT acknowledges using the injected hint when the answer switches, but they call this CoT faithfulness, not parametric faithfulness; that term is Tutek et al. (2025)'s (tutek2025measuring) for interventions on model parameters, not on the prompt. |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) `hong2026making#5` | [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) | partial | Zaman & Srivastava describe and reproduce this hint test (Biasing Features) only to critique it, and never call it parametric faithfulness: they reserve 'parametric alignment' for FUR's unlearning and argue that hint non-verbalization may reflect incompleteness. |
| [Imrankhan 2026](./#p=imrankhan2026better) `imrankhan2026better#1` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham supports that stated reasoning is not always a faithful account of what produced the answer, but it never introduces biasing features into the input or checks whether the CoT mentions them; that evidence is Turpin et al.'s, which Lanham only summarizes in related work. |
| [Imrankhan 2026](./#p=imrankhan2026better) `imrankhan2026better#2` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. define no 'Unfaithful CoT Rate': they measure unfaithfulness by the accuracy drop under biasing features (BBH) and the share of stereotype-aligned unfaithful pairs (BBQ), and check whether explanations mention the bias only as a manual validation on a sample (1 of 426). |
| [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) `jia2026decorative#15` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham did introduce early answering to test whether the answer needs the full CoT, but it has no step-removal test (its tests are early answering, adding mistakes, paraphrasing and filler tokens), and its Adding Mistakes test already injects errors and regenerates the rest, which Jia presents as its own complementary approach. |
| [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) `jia2026decorative#16` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. report that reasoning models often fail to verbalize hints they use, but not a rate below 20%: their overall scores are 25% (Claude 3.7 Sonnet) and 39% (DeepSeek R1), and 'often below 20%' describes individual settings. |
| [Jose et al. 2026](./#p=jose2026strategic) `jose2026strategic#1` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham raises concerns about CoT faithfulness, but it does not show CoTs failing to highlight key factors behind behavior: its evidence is answers that do not depend on the CoT, and its filler-token and paraphrase tests found no evidence of unstated or hidden reasoning; the omitted-factor evidence comes from co-cited Turpin, Chen and Chua and Evans. |
| [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms) `kudo2024llms#4` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. require the CoT to acknowledge the bias only when the bias affects the prediction, and count an explanation as faithful whenever the prediction is unaffected, so they do not define faithfulness as the conjunction of mentioning the hint and relying on it. |
| [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) `li2025better#3` | [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | partial | The source supports CoT–answer consistency (the answer following from the chain) as a faithfulness criterion, but not the correctness-mismatch rule: it treats an incorrect chain that reaches a correct answer as a plausibility failure and explicitly calls such an answer faithful. |
| [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift) `li2025drift#2` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham does read insensitivity to alterations as a faithfulness failure, but its alterations are to the model's own CoT and the response it measures is the final answer; it never alters the task input or examines changes in the rationale, which is what Li means. |
| [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift) `li2025drift#3` | [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | partial | The source supports perturbation-based evaluation and lists the same three test families, but it describes them as edits to the model's input judged by the change in prediction (counterfactual edits insert words into the input; biasing features are added to the input), not as perturbations of the rationale, which only Corrupting CoT does, and it argues these tests measure self-consistency rather than faithfulness. |
| [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) `li2025training#4` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. do show that verbalized explanations systematically omit the factors driving decisions, but they show it with behavioral input perturbations, not with attribution, feature visualization or mechanistic interpretability, which they mention only as possible future means. |
| [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) `li2025training#4` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. show that CoTs fail to verbalize hints that determine answers, but not with attribution, feature-visualization or mechanistic-interpretability techniques: their evidence is hinted versus unhinted prompt pairs, and they list probing internal activations as future work. |
| [Lin et al. 2026](./#p=lin2026reason) `lin2026reason#1` | [Lin et al. 2025](./#p=lin2025clarity) | partial | Lin et al. (CLARity) show that outcome-based RL on multiple-choice data yields correct answers backed by unreliable reasoning that contradicts the chosen option, but they say nothing about memorized answers, pre-RL data overlap, or retrieving an answer and rationalizing it afterwards. |
| [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) `little2026length#4` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. support an LLM checker labelling whether a hinted CoT verbalizes the hint, but their label requires that the CoT mention the hint, and they score an abrupt, unjustified switch to the hint answer as not verbalized (Fig. 6), whereas the monitor Little et al. equate with Chen's counts such switches, and hint-matching 'first instincts', as verbalizing. |
| [Lobo et al. 2024](./#p=lobo2024impact) `lobo2024impact#2` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham contrasts reasoning that drives the answer with post-hoc reasoning produced after the conclusion is fixed, but it defines faithfulness with Jacovi and Goldberg and says explicitly that being post-hoc neither guarantees nor excludes faithfulness, so it does not define a faithful CoT as one whose steps logically lead to the answer. |
| [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith) `lv2026geofaith#9` | [Xu et al. 2024](./#p=xu2024faithful) | partial | Xu et al. do report unfaithful CoTs (6% of correct CoT answers reached through flawed reasoning), but their faithfulness means a correct answer reached by valid logical steps as judged by human annotators, not fidelity to the model's actual decision process. |
| [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) `lyu2023faithful#3` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The quoted definition and the alias 'persuasiveness' are the source's, but 'understandability' is not an alias of plausibility there: the source gives 'human-interpretability' as the alias of readability, a criterion it lists separately from plausibility. |
