| [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning) `macdermott2025reasoning#5` | [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | partial | The source supports only that CoT can be unfaithful: it measures unfaithfulness without hints (reversed question pairs and illogical shortcuts on Putnam problems) and calls its cases rare, so neither the hint-based measurement nor 'often unfaithful' applies to it. |
| [Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness) `madsen2023faithfulness#11` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source states the converse: different interpretations for similar inputs and outputs show unfaithfulness (Corollary 2), and it says this test is 'more useful to disprove the faithfulness of an interpretation rather than prove it', so similar explanations on similar inputs do not make a method faithful; the source also conditions on similar outputs, which the citing sentence drops. |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) `madsen2024self#10` | [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | partial | Chen et al. support the plausibility definition and the finding that simulation precision does not correlate with plausibility, but they report no plausibility levels and never say self-explanations are "very convincing to humans". |
| [Maniyar et al. 2026](./#p=maniyar2026measuring) `maniyar2026measuring#4` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. do show that Answer is Always A leads to CoTs that rationalize the biased answer without mentioning the bias, but the up-to-36% accuracy drop comes from their Suggested Answer bias (zero-shot GPT-3.5); Answer is Always A lowers accuracy by at most 18.7%. |
| [Mao et al. 2026](./#p=mao2026taming) `mao2026taming#18` | [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | partial | Tutek et al. frame faithfulness as whether the CoT faithfully represents the model's internal reasoning, but the paper never mentions monitorability, so the claim that faithfulness is distinct from monitorability is not in the source. |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) `matton2025walk#20` | [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) | partial | DeYoung et al. evaluate faithfulness only by perturbing inputs (erasing the rationale tokens or keeping only them); nothing in ERASER intervenes on intermediate layers, so the 'or to intermediate layers' part is unsupported. |
| [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) `meek2025measuring#7` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. do score only cases where the cue switches the answer, but they do not mark a CoT faithful because it mentions the cue: verbalization also requires that the CoT depend on the hint, and mentions used only for final verification are excluded. |
| [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) `meek2025measuring#7` | [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | partial | The source's protocol does keep only cases where the cue switches the answer, but it does not mark a CoT faithful because it mentions the cue: its judge requires the CoT to describe the cue's switching effect and explicitly excludes simple repetition of text mentioning the cue. |
| [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) `paul2024making#5` | [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | partial | The source supports LAS as measuring how well rationales help a simulator predict the model's own outputs, but not the formula Paul et al. give for it: Acc(qr→a′) − Acc(q→a′) is the pooled, unadjusted gain, whereas LAS averages that difference separately over leaking and non-leaking examples and then takes the raw mean of the two groups. |
| [Peng et al. 2026](./#p=peng2026measuring) `peng2026measuring#1` | [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc) | partial | Cox et al. support post-hoc reasoning as the answer being fixed (decodable from activations) before the CoT is generated, but they study ordinary forward CoT and never examine reverse CoT generation with the answer visible, so they do not support the claim that RCG is susceptible to it. |
| [Plunkett et al. 2025](./#p=plunkett2025self) `plunkett2025self#3` | [Betley et al. 2025](./#p=betley2025tell) | partial | Betley et al. support that finetuned models describe behaviors never stated in their training data, but not that this 'must reflect privileged information': they classify behavioral self-awareness as out-of-context reasoning, i.e. deriving conclusions implicit in the training data, and leave a common-cause explanation open. |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) `pmlr-v235-chen24bl#6` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source supports plausibility as a matter of human judgement (how convincing an interpretation is to humans), but it never grounds plausibility in factual correctness or logical coherence, terms it does not use. |
| [Radhakrishnan et al. 2023](./#p=radhakrishnan2023question) `radhakrishnan2023question#2` | [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) | partial | The source defines simulatability (the predictive ability a rationale adds for predicting the model's label), but it uses simulatability as a measure of rationale quality, 'from the lens of utility to an end-user', and keeps it apart from its faithfulness tests; it never calls simulatability a type of faithfulness. |
| [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) `reddy2025principled#10` | [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | partial | The source documents the patterns (unacknowledged illogical shortcuts, facts altered to fit an answer, post-hoc rationalization) but not their frequency: it reports rates up to 13% of question pairs, says most responses remain faithful, and calls its cases 'real, though rare'. |
| [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) `reddy2025principled#11` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | The six hint types and their descriptions match Chen et al.'s Table 1, but Chen did not create the hints with the correct answer: its hints point to the correct or a wrong answer in the evaluation and only to factually incorrect options in the RL experiments. |
| [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) `reddy2025principled#5` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. support injecting hints and scoring whether the CoT verbalizes them, but not 'the same procedure': Chen's indicator requires that the CoT mention the hint and depend on it, and is checked by a Claude 3.5 Sonnet judge, whereas Reddy et al.'s judge asks only whether the hint is present and they wrongly state that Chen left the indicator unspecified. |
| [Senatorov et al. 2026](./#p=senatorov2026structural) `senatorov2026structural#1` | [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | partial | Tutek et al. state the notion and raise the concern, but they do not show that CoTs fail to reflect the internal process: they read their results as indicating the CoTs are generally faithful, with ff-hard a lower bound whose non-flips may reflect failed unlearning or alternative reasoning paths. |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) `somov2026breaking#1` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham's tests do measure whether the answer depends on the stated reasoning, but Lanham defines faithfulness as accurately representing the reasoning process and says explicitly that the sequential relation does not imply a causal one and that reasoning that drives the answer is neither required for nor a guarantee of faithfulness, so faithfulness 'i.e.' reasoning that causes the prediction is not its definition. |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) `somov2026breaking#1` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. study faithfulness but define it as explanations accurately representing the reasons behind predictions, not as the stated reasoning causing the prediction; they even find zero-shot CoT that does steer predictions toward the bias while remaining unfaithful. |
| [Sullivan & Koller 2026](./#p=sullivan2026language) `sullivan2026language#8` | [Boppana et al. 2026](./#p=boppana2026reasoning) | partial | Boppana et al. support performative CoT as the answer being fixed early while reasoning tokens continue, but not the motive 'to mimic their training distribution': they attribute it to outcome-reward training that does not penalize verbosity or failure to surface early commitment. |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) `sun2026investigating#10` | [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) | partial | Admoni et al. compare input attributions for the answer with those for a post-hoc explanation prompted after the answer, not attributions for CoT generation, so the citing paper's description of them as aligning 'input contributions to CoT generation' and answer prediction does not fit. |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) `sun2026investigating#14` | [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) | partial | Zaman & Srivastava restate the four corruptions but credit them to Lanham et al.; what they propose is the continuous prediction-score version and a diagnosticity test of the metrics, so they did not propose the four corruptions. |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) `sun2026investigating#18` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Replacing the CoT with ellipses is Lanham's procedure, but the faithful-if-the-answer-changes reading Sun attaches to it (§4.3.1) is not: Lanham's filler test measures accuracy as filler length grows, to test whether extra compute alone explains the CoT gain. |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) `sun2026investigating#5` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | The criterion matches Lanham for Early Answering, Adding Mistakes and Paraphrasing, but not for Filler Token: Lanham's filler test asks whether filler raises accuracy (test-time compute), never whether filler changes the original answer, and Lanham reports dataset-level rates rather than treating faithfulness as a binary per-answer property. |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) `sun2026investigating#6` | [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) | partial | Admoni et al. support the attribution criterion (an explanation should rest on the same input features as the answer) but only for post-hoc explanations generated after the answer; they never apply it to a CoT, which the citing paper attributes to them. |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) `sun2026investigating#8` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. show plausible CoTs that rationalize bias-driven answers, but they never show that the model decides the answer before writing the CoT; they report instead that zero-shot CoT itself steers models toward bias-consistent answers they get right without CoT. |
| [Tanneru et al. 2024](./#p=tanneru2024hardness) `tanneru2024hardness#5` | [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | partial | The source supports a reasoning chain executed by a deterministic solver, but not the matching criterion: its final answer is the solver's output, so no separate LLM answer is compared, and faithfulness holds by construction (its solvers are Python, Datalog, a PDDL planner and a relation engine, not only math). |
| [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) `turpin2025teaching#6` | [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) | partial | Skaf et al. study steganography but frame it as obfuscated reasoning that defeats CoT monitoring, never as a faithfulness failure: the word faithfulness does not appear, and their steganographic CoT is by definition load-bearing, i.e. causally used by the model. |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) `tutek2025measuring#17` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Early Answering is Lanham's post-hoc test, and Lanham agrees post-hoc reasoning can still be faithful, but Filler Tokens is not a post-hoc test in Lanham: it tests whether extra test-time compute explains the CoT gain by measuring accuracy, not whether the answer is unchanged. |
| [Viteri et al. 2024](./#p=viteri2026markovian) `viteri2026markovian#1` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. support the claim that a CoT can fail to reflect the true reason for the answer, but not the reason given: they perturb inputs, not the CoT, and credit the finding that answers ignore CoT edits to Gao and Lanham et al. |
| [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) `wang2023scott#6` | [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | partial | The source supports LAS as measuring how well rationales help a simulator predict the model's own predictions, but not the formula SCOTT gives for it: Acc(qr→a′) − Acc(q→a′) is the pooled, unadjusted gain, whereas LAS averages that difference separately over leaking and non-leaking examples and then takes the raw mean of the two groups. |
| [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) `wang2023scott#8` | [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | partial | The source supports a with-minus-without-rationale simulator accuracy gain, but not SCOTT's statement that LAS's 'core idea' is predicting the gold answers: LAS simulators are trained on and scored against the task model's outputs (the source argues against gold-label targets), and the stated formula also drops LAS's averaging over leaking and non-leaking groups. |
| [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case) `wang2026case#5` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Replacing all CoT tokens with '…' is Lanham's procedure, but Wang (§2) attributes to Lanham the evaluation of 'whether the answer changes'; Lanham's filler test measures accuracy as filler length grows, to test the test-time-compute hypothesis, not answer dependence on the CoT. |
| [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) `wang2026concept#1` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source supports 'accurately represents ... the underlying reasoning' and the contrast with merely plausible explanations, but not the disjunct 'or contributes to', which counts a CoT as faithful for causally contributing to reasoning it does not represent; the source has no causal criterion. |
| [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) `wang2026concept#16` | [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) | partial | Admoni et al. align input attributions for the prediction with those for a post-hoc explanation produced after the answer, not with those for a CoT, so the 'attributions for CoT' part of the description is unsupported. |
| [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) `wang2026concept#20` | [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | partial | Tutek et al. supply FUR's step-wise unlearning and the binary ff-hard (plus a probability-shift ff-soft), but not the continuous per-CoT share of steps whose unlearning changes the answer in Eq. 14, which is Sun et al.'s ff_cont. |
| [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) `wang2026concept#21` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | The four perturbation tests are Lanham's, but Lanham never returns binary faithful/unfaithful labels: it reports dataset-level answer-match rates, areas over the curve and accuracies, and says no single test is conclusive. |
| [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating) `yang2025investigating#12` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. (dated 2024 in Yang et al.) show reasoning models justifying hinted answers without stating the hint, but not a decide-first 'always say yes' pattern: that is Arcuschin et al. (2025)'s implicit post-hoc rationalization on Yes/No question pairs (arcuschin2025chain), and Chen never uses the term post-hoc rationalization. |
| [Young 2026a](./#p=young2026measuring) `young2026measuring#5` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. do define unfaithfulness as explanations that omit influences on the prediction, but never call it 'wrong reasoning' (the word does not occur) and stress that unfaithful explanations can be sound: 15% of those they annotate have no obvious errors. |
| [Young 2026b](./#p=young2026why) `young2026why#25` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. test whether models verbalize hints that influence their answers and report faithfulness varying by model and hint type, but they did not introduce the hint-injection method: they say it is inspired by Turpin et al. (2023) and Chua and Evans (2025). |
| [Yu et al. 2026](./#p=yu2026outcome) `yu2026outcome#7` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | partial | The source supports 'reflects the reasoning process the model used to arrive at its answer', but not causal importance: it never requires the explanation to influence the answer, and it evaluates post-hoc interpretations, which have no causal role, by the same faithfulness standard. |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) `zaman2025causal#5` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | The criterion matches Lanham for Early Answering, Adding Mistakes and Paraphrasing, but not for Filler Tokens, which Lanham uses to ask whether filler raises accuracy, never whether it alters the original prediction; and Lanham's measures are dataset-level rates, not binary per-explanation verdicts. |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) `zaman2025chainofthought#3` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Replacing the CoT with ellipses is Lanham's, but the criterion attributed to that metric (unfaithful if the prediction does not change, faithful if it does) is not: Lanham's filler test measures accuracy as filler length grows, to test whether extra compute alone explains the CoT gain. |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) `zeng2026evaluating#1` | [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | partial | The source supports the notion (self-explanations should faithfully reflect the model's reasoning) but not the empirical claim: it is a position paper that measures nothing, giving only illustrative GPT-3.5 examples and calling for faithfulness metrics, with no findings across model families or explanation styles. |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) `zeng2026evaluating#12` | [Plunkett et al. 2025](./#p=plunkett2025self) | partial | The source's task, reporting learned attribute weights scored against weights estimated from the model's choices, fits the self-report-versus-behavior template, but none of the paper's ten tasks is an attribute-weight report, so the suite contains no task taken from this source. |
| [Zhao et al. 2026](./#p=zhao2026robustness) `zhao2026robustness#13` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | partial | Chen et al. plant hints and find models use them without mentioning them, but do not report that the hints led to correct answers: their hints point to correct or wrong answers, and they single out models following incorrect hints with flawed justifications. |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) `agarwal2024faithfulness#4` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | renamed | Turpin et al. run this test under the name 'Perturbing Input Features Not Referenced by Explanations' (their §3) and report the fraction of examples whose prediction changes as '% Unfaithful Overall' (App. Table 10), though their headline BBH metric is the drop in accuracy. |
| [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) `anon2026grace#5` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | renamed | Turpin et al. call the cue a 'biasing feature' (their Suggested Answer adds 'I think the answer is <random_label> ...' after the question and answer choices, so it is appended rather than prepended) and score it mainly as an accuracy drop, with the fraction of changed predictions reported as '% Unfaithful Overall' in App. Table 10. |
| [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) `atkinson2026identifying#4` | [Plunkett et al. 2025](./#p=plunkett2025self) | renamed | The source computes this correlation between learned weights (logistic regression on the model's choices) and averaged self-reported weights, but calls it the accuracy of self-report, never faithfulness. |
| [Basu & Chakraborty 2026](./#p=basu2026measuring) `basu2026measuring#24` | [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | renamed | Akter et al. call the regularizer Counterfactual Sensitivity Regularization (CSR) and the measure of answer sensitivity to operator-level edits Counterfactual Outcome Sensitivity (COS); the citing paper's 'CSR quantifies answer sensitivity' describes COS under the regularizer's name. |
| [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) `bhan2025did#6` | [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | renamed | The source's version of this measure is CC-SHAP, which compares SHAP input contributions for the answer and for the explanation with a cosine measure and is framed as measuring self-consistency, not faithfulness; the paper groups it under the name Attribution Agreement and reads higher correlation as greater faithfulness. |
| [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) `chen2025consistent#4` | [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | renamed | What the citing paper calls explanation consistency is Chen et al.'s counterfactual simulatability, measured as simulation precision, and the citing paper's own footnote makes this mapping. |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) `chen2025reasoning#2` | [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | renamed | The score is the source's articulation rate among switched answers (recall): both condition on a switch from a non-cued to the cued answer and count the CoT only if it states the cue as the cause of that answer. |
| [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) `chua2024biasaugmented#4` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | renamed | Turpin et al. call this phenomenon (CoTs that rationalize bias-consistent answers without verbalizing the bias) systematic unfaithfulness; 'biased reasoning' is Chua et al.'s name for it, and they correctly note that Turpin et al. test three biases. |
| [Emmons et al. 2025a](./#p=emmons2025chain) `emmons2025chain#21` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | renamed | Chen et al. document this pattern (hinted answers justified by plausible CoTs that leave out the hint) as unfaithful CoT, with 'elaborate yet flawed justifications' and CoTs that are 'rarely load-bearing', but never call it post-hoc rationalization. |
| [Emmons et al. 2025a](./#p=emmons2025chain) `emmons2025chain#5` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | renamed | What Emmons calls 'inconsistency' is Lanham's evidence of post-hoc reasoning: answers that stay unchanged when the CoT is truncated or given a mistake (the model 'ignoring' the CoT); Lanham measures answer change, not whether the answer logically follows from the reasoning. |
| [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) `gema2026chainofthought#1` | [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | renamed | Guan et al. require only that traces be sufficient for monitoring the property of interest and explicitly do not assume faithfulness; the paper restates that sufficiency requirement as a faithfulness assumption. |
| [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) `haskins2026training#3` | [Emmons et al. 2025a](./#p=emmons2025chain) | renamed | The term "faithfulness floor" does not appear in Emmons et al.; the claim it names is their necessity condition (CoT-as-computation), under which they find that unfaithfulness issues are resolved. |
| [Jose et al. 2026](./#p=jose2026strategic) `jose2026strategic#11` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | renamed | Chen et al. measure the same quantity, how often the answer changes to the hint answer relative to the unhinted prompt, and call it hint usage. |
| [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) `li2025better#7` | [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | renamed | The source runs a causal mediation analysis of CoT and answer, but the quantity it reads as faithfulness is the indirect effect of the reasoning chain (natural or controlled, NIE/CIE), with the direct effect (NDE/CDE) of the question beside it, not a 'treatment effect'. |
| [Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when) `li2026when#3` | [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case) | renamed | The source measures these effects (filler replacement, early-answering truncation, counterfactual-CoT replacement) but treats them as CoT faithfulness, the strength of the CoT-to-answer path, never as 'trace use'. |
| [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) `luo2026removal#4` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | renamed | HVF is Chen et al.'s unnormalized score (switches with a_u ≠ h and a_h = h, scored by whether the CoT verbalizes the hint as a cause), which Chen calls the CoT faithfulness score. |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) `madsen2024self#11` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | renamed | The source describes the same category, textual explanations from a generator trained on human explanations, as 'interpretation via post-hoc text generation' for which 'plausibility is the dominating property, while there is no faithfulness guarantee'; it never uses the word 'rationalization'. |
| [Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing) `occhipinti2026probing#3` | [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | renamed | The source calls its test cue articulation, based on Turpin et al., and describes counterfactual simulation only as a related approach, while the paper files the source under counterfactual simulatability. |
| [Radhakrishnan et al. 2023](./#p=radhakrishnan2023question) `radhakrishnan2023question#3` | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | renamed | Turpin et al. report that explanations justify stereotype-aligned answers without mentioning the stereotypes and call this systematic unfaithfulness; 'biased reasoning' is Radhakrishnan et al.'s label. |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) `sun2026investigating#11` | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | renamed | Chen et al. state this test but call the injected items hints (and the measure the CoT faithfulness score), not Biasing Features. |
| [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) `sun2026investigating#11` | [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | renamed | The test is the source's, but the source calls it testing whether models articulate cues (based on Turpin et al.), not Biasing Features, which is Turpin et al.'s label. |
| [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) `wang2026concept#20` | [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) | renamed | Eq. 14 is the source's ff_cont, the share of CoT steps whose unlearning changes the answer; the source uses FUR only for the unlearning procedure and calls the continuous score ff_cont. |
| [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating) `yang2025investigating#6` | [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) | renamed | The source names this product the effective cue influence rate (ECR), the share of undetected reward hacks, never 'scheming'; and because the paper's VR is not conditioned on cue influence, its product no longer equals ECR's false-negative share. |
| [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) `chuang2026faithlm#7` | [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | unsupported | Madsen et al. do not run a simulatability evaluation: they measure faithfulness with self-consistency checks that re-run the model on its own explanation-edited input, and explicitly set this apart from simulatability (an observer predicting the model's behaviour), which they attribute to Chen et al. (2023). |
| [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) `gurarieh2026faithfulness#29` | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | unsupported | Lanham has no simulatability metric and never gives the CoT to a weaker simulator model (every test feeds the edited CoT back to the same model), so the simulatability attribution rests on co-cited [60] Chen et al., and Gurarieh itself notes that no prior work uses this exact setup. |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) `hong2026making#2` | [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary) | unsupported | Zhao & Daumé never test consistency across samples or under paraphrase: their PEX consistency scores how much a single explanation supports the prediction over its negation, and they sample explanations only to rank them by that score. |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) `karvonen2026would#17` | [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) | unsupported | Chua et al.'s BCT does not train the CoT to acknowledge the cue when it mattered: it fine-tunes the model on cued prompts paired with its own uncued reasoning so that the cue stops mattering, and lists teaching models to verbalize biases only as future work. |
| [Nguyen et al. 2026b](./#p=nguyen2026generalization) `nguyen2026generalization#5` | [Boppana et al. 2026](./#p=boppana2026reasoning) | unsupported | Boppana et al.'s reasoning theater (performative CoT) is a model that is already internally committed to its answer continuing to write reasoning without disclosing that commitment; they never describe verbalizing a factor that played no role in the computation, which is what the citing paper labels reasoning theater. |
| [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) `reddy2025principled#10` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | unsupported | The source is an opinion piece that predates chain-of-thought prompting and reports no study, so it cannot show that CoTs often omit key steps or fabricate rationales; the nearest it says is that explanations from a generator trained on human explanations are plausible with 'no faithfulness guarantee'. |
| [Sanyal et al. 2022](./#p=sanyal2022fairr) `sanyal2022fairr#2` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | unsupported | The source defines faithfulness as a property of interpretations, how accurately they represent the model's reasoning process, and never discusses whether models learn the task or depend on shortcut patterns. |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) `somov2026breaking#1` | [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | unsupported | The source defines faithfulness as how accurately an interpretation represents the reasoning process behind the prediction and evaluates post-hoc interpretations, which play no causal role, by the same standard; it never requires stated reasoning to cause the prediction and uses no causal vocabulary. |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) `somov2026breaking#18` | [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | unsupported | Matton et al. never edit or truncate the chain of thought: they edit concepts in the input question and compare each concept's effect on the answer with how often the explanations cite it, so the citation belongs with the input-perturbation methods, not with trace perturbation. |
| [Yu et al. 2026](./#p=yu2026outcome) `yu2026outcome#2` | [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | unsupported | Lyu et al. never perturb a reasoning trace and measure output changes: they make the answer causally depend on the chain by construction (a deterministic solver executes it), and their only removals are prompt ablations scored by accuracy. |

</details>

### Tensions: questions on which the papers disagree

1. **Is an answer that survives a corrupted chain a sign of unfaithfulness?** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) read an answer that stays the same after mistakes are inserted as a sign of more post-hoc reasoning and lower faithfulness, while an answer that changes suggests that "the model is likely not ignoring the CoT". They caution that "Reasoning not being post-hoc does not guarantee faithfulness, nor does being post-hoc exclude faithfulness." Definitions that apply the test to single traces drop this caution. [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) write "If the prediction of the model changes, the CoT is considered faithful", and [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) require that "A faithful model, upon processing the logically inconsistent trace, should change its prediction or at least become less certain." [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc) accept the verdict while noting its cost: answering correctly despite a flawed scratchpad "is perhaps the preferred behavior, but it would constitute unfaithful reasoning". [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) rejects it: "corruption cannot distinguish unfaithfulness from justified correction, since ignoring a broken chain is good behavior". [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) separate silent bypass (the model ignores the corrupted chain without saying so) from self-correction, and call a high bypass rate "consistent with faithful reasoning" in the sense that the CoT reflects the computation. In contrast, the stance consistency of [Han et al. 2026b](./#p=han2026rfeval) accepts a change of stance only when the output identifies the flaw, so that silent correction counts as unfaithful ("unfaithful-correct cases, such as silent corrections").

2. **If a cue influences the answer and the CoT does not mention it, is the CoT unfaithful or only incomplete?** For group 2 this is the standard case of unfaithfulness: "a CoT that does not verbalize the hint while using the hint is clearly unfaithful" ([Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning); likewise [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek), [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)). [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) argue that the test "confuses unfaithfulness with incompleteness", because a linear narrative compresses distributed computation. They call hint verbalization "a test of verbalized sensitivity to a known intervention" rather than faithfulness itself. They report that many flagged CoTs are judged faithful by other metrics, "exceeding 50% in some models", and that larger sampling budgets raise the probability of obtaining at least one CoT that verbalizes the hint to "up to 90% in some settings". They call the original analysis "too strong for drawing conclusions about CoT faithfulness" and qualify their positive findings with "We do not claim all CoTs are faithful". [Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one) echo the incompleteness objection: "A trace that omits the cue may be incomplete rather than unfaithful". [Nguyen et al. 2026b](./#p=nguyen2026generalization) use cue acknowledgment as their operational definition but warn that "cue acknowledgment does not establish faithfulness in the conventional sense", since a mentioned factor may not have influenced the computation.

3. **Which cue cases are scored, and is mentioning the cue enough?** An unchanged answer is faithful for [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), whose models must acknowledge biases or "give predictions unaffected by bias", and for [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching): "If the model’s answer does not change between the prompts, we say it is faithful." [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) and [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) score only the cases where the cue switches the answer ("We only test for faithfulness on the switched examples"), while [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) also score cases without a switch and count a model that does not discuss the cue as "not being faithful". Mention alone is enough for [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) and [Nguyen et al. 2026b](./#p=nguyen2026generalization). [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), [egan 2026](./#p=egan2026hint) and [Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one) also require that the CoT present the cue as a reason, and [Young 2026a](./#p=young2026measuring) shows that the two requirements are different constructs ("mention" vs "dependence").

4. **Is agreement between reasoning and answer faithfulness?** Group 4 includes the definition of faithfulness by [Zhao et al. 2026](./#p=zhao2026robustness) as "whether the model’s final answer is consistent with its generated chain-of-thought reasoning", and the criterion of [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) that the correctness of the CoT and the correctness of the answer agree. [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded) define faithfulness through the "true decision process of the model" and use agreement between answer and explanation as evidence for their method. Systems that derive answers from reasoning chains claim faithfulness by construction ([Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful); [Pan et al. 2023](./#p=pan2023logiclm); [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer)), and Pan et al. qualify their guarantee with "as long as the problem formulation is correct". [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) call reasoning that is "coherent and consistent with its prediction" merely plausible, and possibly unfaithful. [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) write that "self-consistency alone does not establish causal reliance", and [Emmons et al. 2025b](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) list "thought-response consistency" as distinct from faithfulness. [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) concede the point for unconstrained chains ("this still does not imply that the reasoning chain is a faithful explanation"), and [Zhao et al. 2026](./#p=zhao2026robustness) call their own measure weaker than "the stronger notion of explanation faithfulness in the interpretability literature".

5. **Are behavioral tests enough, or are the model's internals needed?** Behavioral notions appear in groups 2, 3 and 6. [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) call their measures "behavioural faithfulness", and [Malladi & Srivastava 2026](./#p=malladi2026directly) define "behavioral faithfulness" through reproduction of observable input-output behavior. Group 3 also includes interventions on parameters ([Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)) and on activations ([Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms)). In the view of [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring), tests that perturb the input or the CoT measure self-consistency, "a necessary, but not sufficient test for faithfulness". [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) make a narrower claim, that "intervention-based proxies for measuring causality are insufficient measures for faithfulness". They attribute the broader self-consistency argument to Parcalabescu & Frank, and add that "causal faithfulness metrics are still valuable and worth measuring". [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) write that behavioral tests "cannot separate a model whose answer is supported by a different mechanism than its CoT suggests from one whose answer is simply robust to perturbation", and [Han et al. 2026b](./#p=han2026rfeval) write that apparent unfaithfulness "is not a sufficient condition for unfaithfulness in the underlying process". [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) add the opposite case: "Faithfulness differs from mechanistic relevance", since CoT text "can causally impact behavior even if it does not straightforwardly" explain model internals.

6. **Should correctness be part of the definition?** Seven papers in group 4 tie faithfulness to correct reasoning or correct answers: [Xu et al. 2024](./#p=xu2024faithful) ("’unfaithful’ if the answer is correct but the process is not"), [Wang et al. 2025c](./#p=wang2025enhancing) ("unfaithful trajectories that guess the correct option after a faulty chain of thought"), [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better), who compare the CoT with a gold rationale, [Mohammadi et al. 2025](./#p=mohammadi2025evaluating), who check whether the reasoning "logically entails the reference explanation", [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal), who report the "Percentage of correct answers accompanied by flawed reasoning" as natural unfaithfulness, [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith), whose faithful step follows from prior steps "without unexplained jumps or errors", and [Creswell & Shanahan 2022](./#p=creswell2022faithful), whose faithful reasoner's computations "mirror standard definitions of logical validity". Twelve papers exclude correctness. [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) hold that "the evaluation of faithfulness should not involve human-provided gold labels", [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) require agreement "regardless of whether the answer is correct, since CIA measures faithfulness rather than correctness", [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) object to indicators based on correctness because "reasoning correctness and faithfulness are not equivalent", [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) stress that "faithfulness does not guarantee correctness", [Han et al. 2026b](./#p=han2026rfeval) state that "accuracy and RF are not logically tied", and [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) write that "Consistency, not correctness, is ultimately the key requirement for faithfulness". The other six are [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards), [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring), [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), [Hong et al. 2023](./#p=hong2023faithful), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) and [Anonymous 2026 (do what you say)](./#p=anon2026what). [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) count a CoT that names an incorrect bridge entity (the intermediate entity in a two-step question) as "faithful but wrong" if a probe also detects that entity internally. [Xu et al. 2024](./#p=xu2024faithful), [Wang et al. 2025c](./#p=wang2025enhancing) and the categorical criterion of [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) instead label flawed reasoning paired with a correct answer unfaithful.

7. **Is faithfulness the same as honesty?** [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) tie the two conditionally: if models can recognize the influence of the biasing features, "unfaithful CoT explanations may be a form of model dishonesty, as opposed to a lack of capability". [Walden & Wanner 2026](./#p=walden2026reasoning) adopt "a lenient notion of honesty", under which a CoT is honest when it states an intended or actual use of the hint. They note that the stated use "may or may not correspond" to how the hint is used "in a mechanistic sense". [Sherburn et al. 2024](./#p=sherburn2024language) frame self-explanation the same way ("This paper focuses on honesty"). [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) reject this reading for step faithfulness, under which "the step would be considered faithful unless the model knowingly and deceptively lied", and adopt the mechanistic one. [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) and [Joglekar et al. 2025](./#p=joglekar2025training) separate honesty from accuracy: "models can sincerely describe their actions incorrectly", and Joglekar et al. claim only that honest confessions are easy to produce, not accurate ones ("This is not to say that producing accurate confessions is an easy task, only honest ones").

8. **What makes a statement a lie or deception: a belief, an intent or a mismatch with action?** [Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating) call a statement a lie if the model "believes it to be false", requiring neither "Deceptive intent" nor "Active awareness of falsehood at generation time", and note that this "differs somewhat from the colloquial notion of lying". [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) call a claim that the model does not believe deception "whether or not an operator instruction asked for it", while holding that "Not all context-dependent non-literal behavior is deception", such as roleplay or fiction. The white-box audit of [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55), which has access to the model's internals, requires knowledge: a model that "Holds a reason or judgment that contradicts what it presents, or knowingly leaves out something the reader needs to know". [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) define a deceptive response without any statement, as "code specifically designed to pass the visible test cases without solving the general problem", and [Bu et al. 2026](./#p=bu2026spadebench) define it as "a spontaneous inconsistency between an agent’s declared plan (what it says it will do) and its executed action (what it actually does)". Monitor rubrics call the claim of an action that was not taken "lying to the user" ([Anonymous 2026 (implementation)](./#p=anon2026implementation); [Chidambaram et al. 2026](./#p=chidambaram2026corrupted)), while [Dietz et al. 2026](./#p=dietz2026split) note that "fabrication may occur without the model representing the output as false" and [Shiromani & Richter 2026](./#p=shiromani2026false) separate action-faithfulness from "whether the stated intent is honest".

9. **Is an accurate self-report enough?** [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) count self-modeling as successful "whenever its self-reports agree with its observed input-output behavior", whether the report draws "on internal access, on cues in the prompt, or on general knowledge of how language models behave". [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) call agreement between self-report and behavior faithfulness, and reserve introspection for reports that also have causal grounding: "Grounding requires reporting the true cause". They suggest that, if attribution similarity measures grounding, a faithful adapter with low similarity "might be a correct confabulator", one that reports accurately through an ungrounded mechanism. [Anonymous 2026 (introspection routes)](./#p=anon2026introspection) write that "a self-report can be accurate without being introspective". Under the privileged-access test the evidence is split: [Blandfort & Pawar 2026](./#p=blandfort2026strangers) find that "Verbal testimony describes other models about as well as the self", while [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) find that "the target model is better explained by itself than by other explainer models".

## Counterfactual

This section covers 292 definitions related to the term *counterfactual*, from 87 of the 217 papers in the map (this site's collection of papers). By type, 109 definitions are operational (they define a procedure or a metric), 84 informal (stated in words), 45 formal (stated mathematically), 29 criteria (necessary or sufficient conditions for a notion, or assumptions it rests on), 19 taxonomies (classifications into types) and 6 failure modes (named ways in which a counterfactual or an explanation goes wrong). By origin, 160 are proposed by the paper itself, 86 are adopted unchanged from earlier work and 46 are adapted from earlier work. Of the 292 definitions, 163 define a counterfactual notion (162 of them have the word "counterfactual" in the term they define) and 129 define a related notion that the paper ties to counterfactuals, such as a validity criterion, an effect measure or a simulation metric. A second reading of every paper in full, made after an earlier version of this page, added 163 definitions and 14 papers, and 113 of the added definitions are related notions. The definitions are sorted by meaning into the 12 groups below; each group holds definitions that use counterfactuals in a similar way. Group 2 tests whether an observer’s "simulation matches the model’s actual output" on counterfactual inputs ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl)), and group 3 defines the inputs on which that test is run. Eight further groups (4 to 9, 11 and 12) cover the model's predictions of its own behavior, edits to the input, measures of causal effect, conditions for a valid counterfactual, tests of whether an explanation mentions an edit, edits to the reasoning, alternative internal states and parameters of the model, and comparisons for judging covert behavior. In group 1, the counterfactual explanation is defined by what an edited input "would lead" the model to do ([Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) also map the model’s "decision boundary" to assess validity and minimality (whether the edit changes the decision, and whether it is the smallest edit that does). In group 10, counterfactuals supply training data or reward signals, as in [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott). One definition in group 12 runs no variant at all: in the counterfactual autorater of [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training), a judge asks whether the reasoning "could ... plausibly have been produced in a scenario where no defect existed". The groups differ on four points: what is varied; whether the variant must change the output; how a change in output becomes a causal claim; and what the comparison judges (an explanation, the model's reliance on a feature, a self-report, a training target or a covert behavior).

In the tables, text in quotation marks is copied from the paper, with omissions marked by an ellipsis. In the tables of definitions, text without quotation marks is a short paraphrase, not a quote. When a table does not show all of a group's definitions, the others are listed below it by paper, with the term each one defines in parentheses. Where the papers give formulas, a list at the end of the group restates them.

### 1. Counterfactual explanation: an edited input that would change the model's decision

This is the sense used in explainable AI, which [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) and [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) take from Wachter et al. 2017. The counterfactual is an alternative input that would lead the model to a different decision, and that input is itself the explanation. In 5 of the 10 papers the model writes the edit itself, which gives a self-generated counterfactual explanation (SCE): [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self), [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can), [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms), [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) and [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating). The edit is judged by validity (whether the model's own prediction on the edited input reaches the target; [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) require that the edited input be re-evaluated in a new context window), minimality (how small the edit is) and closeness (how near the edited input stays to the original). [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) separate validity from correctness, which asks whether the edit "actually evaluates to" the target by human judgment. Three papers make validity the condition for a trustworthy explanation: [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) ("If satisfied, the counterfactual is faithful"), [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) ("faithfulness can be evaluated by checking whether the generated counterfactuals indeed produce the prediction change") and [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) ("Without validity, the explanation is a misleading representation of the model’s counterfactual behaviour"). Three definitions use "counterfactual explanation" for a statement about how behavior would change, not for the edited input: [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) and [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency). This group holds 28 definitions from 10 papers (12 proposed, 13 adopted, 3 adapted).

> A counterfactual explanation $\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}}$ is a modified version of the original input $\mathbf{x}$ that would lead the model to change its decision, that is, $f(\mathbf{x})\neq f(\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}})$ .
>
> ([Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can), §3.1 (Step 2))

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | counterfactual explanation | formal | “a modified version of the original input $\mathbf{x}$ that would lead the model to change its decision” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | counterfactual explanation | informal | “an alternative input $x^{\prime}\neq x$ that would lead to a specific alternative output $y^{\prime}\neq y$” |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | counterfactual explanation | informal | “Counterfactual explanations replace content, to get the opposite prediction” |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | faithful counterfactual | criterion | “we can re-evaluate the prediction with the counterfactual and check that this goal is satisfied. If satisfied, the counterfactual is faithful.” |
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | self-generated counterfactual explanation (SCE) | informal | “we do not solve an optimization problem and instead ask the model itself to generate the counterfactual explanation” |
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | counterfactual validity (Val) | operational | “the percentage of times the SCE actually produces the intended target label” |
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | SCE correctness | operational | “whether the counterfactual $\mathbf{x}_{CE}$ actually evaluates to $\hat{y}_{CE}$” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | validity (of SCEs) | criterion | “the revised input should change the model’s prediction when re-evaluated in a new context window” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | minimality (of SCEs) | criterion | “they should make the smallest edit required to change the outcome” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | necessary criteria for valid and minimal SCEs | criterion | “(iii) they must be able to accurately self-predict their behaviour” |
| [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) | counterfactual-style self-explanation | informal | “faithfulness can be evaluated by checking whether the generated counterfactuals indeed produce the prediction change” |
| [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) | counterfactual pseudo-ground truth | operational | “replacing w∗ with another word wȳ associated with the second most probable prediction ȳ” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | self-generated counterfactual explanation | informal | “the model edits its own input until the prediction flips” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Edit-Proposal (E8) | operational | “the example counts as flipped if the fraction of resamples that differ from baseline exceeds $0.5$” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | counterfactual explanations (self-explanation type) | informal | “how different inputs might lead to different LLM responses” |
| [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) | counterfactual explanation | informal | “how behaviors change under an edit to its input (e.g. removal of a hint)” |
| [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) | counterfactual explanation (self-description) | informal | “explain the input difference that induced the change or behavioral change itself” |

The group's 11 other definitions: [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded) (counterfactual examples); [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) (counterfactual examples (explanations)); [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (minimality of counterfactual explanations); [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (edit distance (ED) of SCEs); [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (generation percentage (Gen)); [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) (counterfactual-style self-explanation faithfulness); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (self-generated counterfactual explanation (SCE)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (minimal counterfactual); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (Validity (Val)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (Excess Distance (ED)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (Exact Match (EM)).

The papers state these notions as formulas, in which $f$ gives the model's decision, $\mathbf{x}$ is the original input and $\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}}$ the edited input:
- Counterfactual condition: $f(\mathbf{x})\neq f(\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}})$, with a chosen target $f(\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}})=y_{\text{CE}}$ when the counterfactual is generated ([Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can)).
- Validity: the share of instances with $f(\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}})=y_{\text{CE}}$, computed without ($\mathrm{Val}$) and with ($\mathrm{Val}_C$) the earlier turns in context ([Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) require the flip in a new context window.
- Minimal counterfactual ([Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms)): $z_{i,\text{M}}\in\arg\min_{z_i'\in\mathcal{Z}} d(z_i,z_i')$ subject to $f(\phi(z_i'))=y'$, with $d$ Gower's distance (a distance for records with both numeric and categorical features); excess distance $\mathrm{ED}_i=d(z_i,z_i')-d(z_i,z_{i,\text{M}})$ over valid SCEs, and Exact Match the share of instances whose SCE is a minimal counterfactual.
- Edit-Proposal ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating)): the model's edit counts as a flip (a change of answer) when more than half of $K$ resamples (new samples of the answer) on the edited prompt differ from the baseline answer; no target label is given.

### 2. Counterfactual simulatability: an explanation lets an observer predict the model's outputs on other inputs

Simulatability is the degree to which an observer can predict a model's outputs. Here the counterfactual is the test input, not the explanation. An explanation given on $x$ is judged by whether an observer who reads it predicts the model's output on other inputs $x'$. [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) define the notion for free-text explanations and give two metrics, precision and generality, both computed over the counterfactuals the observer judges simulatable (those for which the observer can infer the model's output from the explanation): precision is the share of them on which the observer's prediction matches the model's output, and generality is one minus the average similarity between two of them. The observer is a human in Chen et al.'s definition (approximated by GPT-4), "a human or model" in [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching), LLM judges and a human study in [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm), and a simulator model in [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual). What the observer predicts also varies: the model's answer ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl)), whether a new request is refused or complied with ([Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl)), or whether a named prompt edit moves a behavior rate ([Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would)). [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) add a first phase in which the observer predicts without the explanation, and score the difference in accuracy between the two phases. The definitions disagree on whether the notion measures faithfulness, plausibility or usefulness (disagreement 7 at the end of this section). This group holds 34 definitions from 15 papers (12 proposed, 14 adopted, 8 adapted).

> we propose to evaluate counterfactual simulatability of natural language explanations: whether an explanation can enable humans to precisely infer the model’s outputs on diverse counterfactuals of the explained input.
>
> ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl), Abstract)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “whether an explanation can enable humans to precisely infer the model’s outputs on diverse counterfactuals of the explained input” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | mental model / simulation | formal | “$h_{x,e_{x},o_{x}}(x^{\prime})$ denotes what the human infers to be $M$ ’s output on $x^{\prime}$ (simulation)” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | simulation precision | formal | “the fraction of simulatable counterfactuals where humans’ simulation matches the model’s actual output” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | simulation generality | formal | “one minus the average similarity between two simulatable counterfactuals” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | wrong mental model (misleading explanation) | failure-mode | “humans form a wrong mental model of GPT-4 (i.e., incorrectly infer how GPT-4 answers relevant counterfactuals)” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “whether model explanations on one input help humans predict what predictions models will give on other inputs” |
| [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) | counterfactual simulatability notion of faithfulness | criterion | “an explanation is faithful if and only if the explanation allows a human or model to accurately predict how the model will behave on other relevant inputs” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “the extent to which a model’s explanation on a certain input allows a user to predict the model’s answer for a different input” |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “rewarding CoTs that allow for a simulator model to predict a model’s answer on a counterfactual input” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “This tests whether an observer can predict a model’s behavior given its explanation.” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “an explanation is considered useful if it helps people anticipate how a model’s prediction would change on counterfactual inputs” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | counterfactual simulatability (acc_improvement) | formal | “comparing Phase 2 (with explanation) against Phase 1 (without explanation)” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | in-context bias toward the original answer (anchoring) | formal | “the judge’s prediction matches the original answer” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | explanation (counterfactual-testable behavioral claim) | informal | “a behavioral claim testable by counterfactual experiments, not an explanation of the model’s internal computation” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | counterfactual claim | operational | “a specific prompt edit changes how often a specified behavior occurs by at least 30 percentage points” |
| [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl) | counterfactual refusal and compliance accuracy | operational | “the fraction of examples where responses match the predicted behavior” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | counterfactual simulatability | operational | Restates Chen et al.’s simulation generality and simulation precision. |

The group's 17 other definitions: [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) (counterfactual simulatability precision (explanation consistency)); [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) (simulated human expectation (follow-up answer from the explanation)); [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) (counterfactual simulation); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (LLM-as-a-judge counterfactual simulation); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (simulation accuracy (guess_rate, selective accuracy, overall accuracy)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (human simulation user study (two-phase)); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (counterfactual simulatability); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (counterfactual simulatability test (tool uplift)); [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) (counterfactual inputs (counterfactual simulation training)); [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) (predicting LLM behavior on counterfactual questions (counterfactual simulatability)); [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) (counterfactual inputs from explanations (logical predicates)); [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (generalizable and precise explanation (desiderata)); [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (human simulation as logical entailment); [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (LLM simulator (simulation prompt)); [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (contrastive simulation); [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl) (counterfactual simulatability evaluation); [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl) (predictive view of explanation (counterfactual inputs)).

The papers' formulas, from [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) unless noted. In them, $h$ is the observer's mental model: from the input $x$, its explanation $e_x$ and the model's output $o_x$, it gives the observer's guess of the model's output on another input $x'$.
- Mental model: $h_{x,e_x,o_x}:X\to O\cup\{\perp\}$, where $h(x')=\perp$ marks $x'$ as unsimulatable.
- Simulation precision: $\frac{1}{|C^*|}\sum_{x'\in C^*}\mathbf{1}[h_{e_x}(x')=o_{x'}]$ over the simulatable counterfactuals $C^*$.
- Simulation generality: $1-\mathbb{E}_{x',x''\sim p}[\alpha(x',x'')]$, with $\alpha$ a similarity metric over simulatable counterfactuals.
- Gain over a no-explanation phase ([Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm)): $\Delta\mathrm{OverallAcc}=\mathrm{OverallAcc}^{\text{P2}}-\mathrm{OverallAcc}^{\text{P1}}$, with cannot_guess counted as incorrect.
- Anchoring of the simulator, the rate at which the judge predicts the original answer ([Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm)): $\widehat{\mathrm{bias}}^{\text{orig}}(p)=\frac{1}{N_p}\sum_{i:\,p_i=p}B_i$, with $B_i=\mathbb{I}[\hat{y}_i=a_i]$.
- Counterfactual claim label ([Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would)): true if the edit moved the behavior rate by at least 50 percentage points, false if by at most 15.

### 3. The counterfactual set: the inputs on which simulatability is tested

These definitions say on which inputs $x'$ an explanation from group 2 is tested (the counterfactual set). Six papers give five ways to build these inputs:
- follow-up questions that an LLM writes from the explanation, instructed to write one "that you can confidently guess the robot’s answer to" ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl)), extended with exact paraphrases by [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) and used as the "non-pragmatic baseline" by [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm);
- the input with a planted cue (text inserted into the prompt that points to a particular answer) removed ([Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual));
- related questions generated by an LLM and kept only if "the task model and simulator disagree on the correct answer" ([Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual));
- four manipulations of Grice's conversational maxims: presupposition flip, lexical substitution, scalar adjustment and contextualization ([Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm));
- real records from a dataset that are close to the original in Hamming distance, the number of features that differ ([Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm)), which [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient) reuse as "similar real inputs from the same dataset rather than synthetic perturbations".

[Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) state that "the validity of any evaluation hinges on this choice", restate three earlier designs as counterfactual sets (a biased prompt, random word insertion, single-concept edits) and name three pitfalls. [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) concede that "LLMs most likely do not cover all simulatable counterfactuals". This group holds 26 definitions from 6 papers (15 proposed, 6 adopted, 5 adapted).

> For a question $x$ , we construct a set of counterfactuals $C(x)$ : inputs that are similar to $x$ , but differ in some way, such that a faithful explanation of the reference model’s behavior on $x$ should help predict its behavior across $C(x)$ .
>
> ([Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm), §2.2)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | counterfactual (distribution) | informal | “any simulatable counterfactual in the natural language distribution” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | counterfactual generation (LLM prompt) | operational | “write a follow-up yes or no question that you can confidently guess the robot’s answer to based on its answer to the starter question” |
| [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) | follow-up questions (related vs. rephrased) | taxonomy | “related questions and rephrased questions” |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | cue-based counterfactual | informal | “The counterfactual input is the input without the cue.” |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | model-based counterfactual | informal | “generate diverse, generic counterfactuals (our model-based counterfactuals) that test a model’s understanding of a topic by asking closely related questions” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | natural language counterfactuals | informal | “minimal what-if variations of an input” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | pragmatic counterfactual taxonomy | taxonomy | “Each counterfactual corresponds to a controlled manipulation of one or more maxims.” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | counterfactual (edited input x̂) | informal | “manually edit test examples in small but meaningful ways that usually flip the gold label” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | data-driven counterfactuals | operational | “quantifying closeness with Hamming distance: the number of dataset features that differ between two inputs” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | too little perturbation | failure-mode | “Most prior approaches vary only a single concept at a time” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | irrelevant or incoherent perturbations | failure-mode | “often produce counterfactuals that are irrelevant or drift from the natural data distribution” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | testing external knowledge | failure-mode | “predicting the reference model’s behavior requires inferring its world knowledge rather than purely applying its stated reasoning” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | biased-prompt test as a single-element counterfactual set | operational | “testing some point $x_{\text{no bias}}$ with the counterfactual set $C(x)=\{x_{\text{bias}}\}$” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | counterfactual pair (Moral Machines) | operational | “share the same set of character types and binary flags but differ in the counts of those characters” |
| [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient) | counterfactual input (similar real input) | informal | “similar real inputs from the same dataset rather than synthetic perturbations” |

The group's 11 other definitions: [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) (counterfactual input (cue-based vs model-based)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (counterfactual reasoning); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (LLM automatic counterfactual generation); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (presupposition flip (counterfactual type)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (lexical substitution (counterfactual type)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (scalar adjustment (counterfactual type)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (contextualization (counterfactual type)); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (counterfactual set C(x)); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (pitfalls in choosing the counterfactual set); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (random word-insertion counterfactuals); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (concept space perturbation).

The formulas of [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm), where $C(x)$ is the counterfactual set of the input $x$:
- Data-driven set: $C(x)$ holds dataset records within Hamming distance $\le 2$ of $x$, balanced by label.
- A cue test as a set with one element: $C(x)=\{x_{\text{bias}}\}$ for the point $x_{\text{no bias}}$.
- Word insertion: $C(x)$ holds $x_w^{(i)}$, the input with word $w$ inserted at sampled position $i\in\{1,\dots,m\}$.

### 4. Counterfactual self-prediction: the model predicts how it would behave under an edit

In this group the observer of group 2 is the model itself. It predicts its behavior under an edit as a judgment of whether its behavior would change, a flip rate (how often its answer would change), a probability of a behavior or a "post-perturbation judge score" ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating)), and these reports are scored against runs on the edited input. [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) call this self-modeling, "an LLM’s ability to answer questions about its own behavior", and introduce a benchmark represented here by four tasks (Flip-Decision, Flip-Rate, Perturbation-Choice and Component-Attribution); their fifth definition in this group, Score-Prediction, describes a separate multi-turn BLOOM training task. [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) train counterfactual prediction as "causal self-report"; [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) train an observer role sharing the assistant’s weights and reward its counterfactual behavior-probability predictions with a Brier score (the squared error between a predicted probability and the outcome). [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) score the model's reports of how much each input factor matters against counterfactual audits (checks of whether replacing the factor changes the answer; group 6) and show that the auditing rule decides whether truthful reporting pays: "report-dependent auditing creates a suppression incentive". [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) name self-prediction as one of three necessary criteria for valid and minimal self-generated counterfactuals (group 1). Self-reports are checked against measured behavior using categorical accuracy and continuous scores: [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) report accuracy and AUROC (the area under the ROC curve, which measures how well a score separates two classes) for Yes or No counterfactual predictions, while [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) score scalar reports "by distance from the behavioral ground truth"; this makes the group the counterfactual counterpart of the definitions of honesty and self-report under the other two terms. This group holds 11 definitions from 5 papers (6 proposed, 1 adopted, 4 adapted).

> In Flip-Decision, we test whether a model can predict whether a prompt perturbation will change its own aggregate behavior.
>
> ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating), §2.4.1)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | counterfactual question (Flip-Decision) | operational | “whether a model can predict whether a prompt perturbation will change its own aggregate behavior” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Flip-Rate (E3) | operational | “How likely is this perturbation to flip your output?” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Perturbation-Choice (E6) | operational | “The behavioral ground truth label is the option with the highest flip rate.” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Component-Attribution (E7) | operational | “remove each component in turn and measure how often the model’s sampled answer changes relative to its baseline behavior” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Score-Prediction (BLOOM self-modeling task) | operational | “predict the post-perturbation judge score without observing the full original or perturbed trajectory” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | counterfactual prediction (causal self-report) | operational | “posed as a follow-up turn on its own transcript, the model is shown one counterfactual claim” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) | counterfactual prediction | informal | “questions about how the assistant’s behavior would change with a specific edit to the input” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) | counterfactual prediction reward (Brier) | formal | “the reward was defined as 1−(p−y)2 , where p is the model prediction and y is the target” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) | forward vs. reverse counterfactual items | taxonomy | “needs to predict the reverse counterfactual” |
| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | Counterfactual Brier Score (CBS) | operational | “a coordinate-wise Brier score applied to counterfactual audit outcomes” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | self-prediction | informal | “models are attempting to predict their own behaviour, i.e. self-prediction” |

The papers' formulas:
- Observer reward ([Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight)): $1-(p-y)^2$, where $p$ is the observer's prediction and $y=(k+\tfrac12)/(n+1)$ is the smoothed share of $n=10$ assistant samples showing the behavior.
- Counterfactual Brier score ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit)): $S=-\sum_j(p_j-\hat{D}_j)^2$ between reported factor importances $p_j$ and counterfactual flip fractions $\hat{D}_j$ (group 6); the score is strictly proper (truthful reporting alone maximizes its expected value) under exogenous verification (verification that does not depend on the report) "when the verification estimates are unbiased".

### 5. Editing the input: the same input with one cue or factor changed

This is the group with the most papers. A counterfactual is the original input with one specified element removed, inserted or replaced, with everything else held fixed, and a difference between the two outputs is read as that element's influence. Besides general prompt edits ([Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would), [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating)), the definitions name seven kinds of element:
- a planted hint, cue or biasing feature ([Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek), [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length), [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training), [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness), [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective), [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary), [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain), [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability), [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm), [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training));
- a concept or structured factor ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal), [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases), [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit));
