- a "protected feature" that an instruction discourages the model from relying on: the query's solution in an added math book or a profession that cues a stereotyped pronoun ([Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful)), or the user's background ([Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency));
- the evidence passage ([Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when));
- an earlier turn of a conversation ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating));
- a note tying a valued outcome to the answer ([Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight));
- an edit that changes the correct answer ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal), [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case)).

Three definitions name earlier uses of this design. [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) call it "standard in the bias and fairness literature", [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) take it from Kaushik et al. 2019, and [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) note that it has "been widely used to assess chain-of-thought faithfulness" and monitorability. [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) state the edit with Pearl's do-operator, which denotes setting a variable by intervention, as a direct effect (the effect of changing one concept while all other concepts and all other aspects of the question stay fixed). This group holds 45 definitions from 30 papers (19 proposed, 15 adopted, 11 adapted).

> Let $\mathbf{x}_{c_{m}\rightarrow c_{m}^{\prime}}$ denote the counterfactual input that results from an intervention that changes the concept $C_{m}$ from $c_{m}$ to $c_{m}^{\prime}$ but keeps all other aspects of the question $\mathbf{x}$ (including the values of all other concepts) the same.
>
> ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), §2)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | counterfactual input (concept intervention) | formal | “changes the concept $C_{m}$ from $c_{m}$ to $c_{m}^{\prime}$ but keeps all other aspects of the question $\mathbf{x}$ (including the values of all other concepts) the same” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | removal-based counterfactual | operational | “edit $\mathbf{x}$ so that the value of a concept $C_{m}$ cannot be determined” |
| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | counterfactual replacement (perturbed input) | formal | “The perturbed input $x^{\setminus j,v}$ is identical to $x$ except that factor $j$ is replaced by $v$ .” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | two types of counterfactuals | taxonomy | “model predictions are insensitive to features not referenced by explanations … model predictions are sensitive to features that are relied on by their explanations” |
| [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | cue (prompt insertion) | informal | “an insertion into the question prompt that points to a particular response” |
| [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) | cue-ablated input | formal | “$x_{\setminus C}$ for the cue-ablated input obtained by removing $C$ from $x$” |
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) | intervention-based evaluation (input with vs. without intervention) | informal | “compare the prediction of a model when given an input with ( $X=1$ ) and without ( $X=0$ ) some intervention applied to it” |
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | matched or counterfactual inputs (intervention vs. control arm) | informal | “Evaluations that compare how a model’s behavior shifts across matched or counterfactual inputs are standard in the bias and fairness literature” |
| [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) | counterfactual test of cue reliance | criterion | “evidence that the protected feature (which was omitted when producing $y^{\prime}$ ) exerts causal influence on $\operatorname{pred}(y)$” |
| [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) | nearby counterfactual (the hint caused the answer) | criterion | “Comparing W to a no-hint prompt would confound the hint’s content with its presence” |
| [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) | counterfactual (hint-induced answer flip) | formal | “If the model’s new answer with the hint $a_{1}$ differs from the original $a_{0}$ ( $a_{0}\neq a_{1}=hint$ ), we regard this as a counterfactual” |
| [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability) | counterfactual hint effect | criterion | “per-problem no-hint / correct-hint / incorrect-hint generations defining influence from the counterfactual answer shift” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | fork (counterfactual conversation variant) | informal | “rewind to a previous turn, modify one element, replay to completion” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | perturbation (prompt edit) | informal | “a modification to a prompt that may or may not change the model’s answer” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | input contrast sets (contrast consistency / spurious invariance) | operational | “the model correctly changes its answer when the input contrast requires it” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) | counterfactual intervention (value leakage) | operational | “adding a a Note paragraph stating that some (non-factually) important event depends on the model’s estimate” |

The group's 29 other definitions: [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) (counterfactual interventions (on input features)); [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) (counterfactual inputs (simulating)); [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) (counterfactuals (causal lens for evaluation)); [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) (counterfactual input variations (paired concept interventions)); [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) (attribute-absent condition (X=0)); [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) (counterfactual prompt); [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) (user-bias intervention); [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) (contrary hint); [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) (counterfactual (unhinted input X=0)); [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) (counterfactual (prompt)); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (counterfactual experiment (prompt edit)); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (cue influence (hint-setting ground truth)); [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient) (counterfactual intervention (sycophancy cue, FACE-Eval)); [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient) (counterfactual intervention (MedQA cognitive-bias cue)); [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) (counterfactual perturbations (to questions)); [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) (input ablation (x \ x̃)); [Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when) (counterfactual answer (edited evidence)); [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) (hint intervention (unhinted vs hinted run)); [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) (counterfactual (concept-edited question)); [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) (removal counterfactual); [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) (value-change counterfactual); [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) (counterfactual question as direct-effect intervention (do-operator)); [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) (counterfactual context); [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) (helpful-hint dependence); [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) (injection-variant dependence label (adversarial vs benign context)); [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) (biasing features (Answer is Always A, Suggested Answer)); [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case) (counterfactual question (for CDE)); [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) (intervention prompt (vs. control prompt)); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (fork types (multi-turn counterfactual edits)).

The papers write the edits and their effects as follows:
- Factor replacement: $x^{\setminus j,v}$ equals $x$ except that factor $j$ takes the value $v\sim\mathcal{P}_j$ ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit)).
- Cue ablation: $x_{\setminus C}$ ([Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective)); input ablation $T_{\text{input}}(\mathcal{M},x,\tilde{x})=d(\mathcal{M}(x),\mathcal{M}(x\backslash\tilde{x}))$ ([Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training)).
- Insertion: $x'=x\oplus\Delta$ ([Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training)).
- Paired concept variants $x^{+}_{c}$ (promoting $c$) and $x^{-}_{c}$ (diminishing $c$) ([Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)).
- Direct effect of a concept ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk)): $\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y\mid do(C_m=c_m',\{C_i=c_i\}_{i\neq m},V=v)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y\mid do(\{C_i=c_i\}_{i\in 1,\ldots,M},V=v)]$.
- Hint flip: $a_0\neq a_1=\text{hint}$ ([Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness)).

### 6. Turning a comparison of runs into a causal claim: flips, total effects and potential outcomes

These definitions state how runs on an original input and on a variant become a claim that the edit caused the outcome. They give four ways to read the comparison: a flip label on one pair or a fraction of flips (a flip is a change of answer), a rate over repeated samples compared with thresholds, effects on answer distributions or on aggregate rates, and bounds on causal recall (the share of the outcomes caused by the intervention that a monitor detects):
- a label saying whether the answer flips on one pair ([Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary)), or a flip fraction over $n=3$ sampled replacements per factor by default ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit));
- a rate estimated from repeated samples and compared with thresholds ([Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would): positive when the cue raises the rate by at least 50 points, negative when it moves the rate by at most 15; [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating): three bands of flip fraction);
- distributional or aggregate effects: differences in the rate of the target answer ([Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain), [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought)), the rate of answer switches caused by the hint, under the paper's assumption of uniform switching ([Little 2026](./#p=little2026length)), the gap in acceptance rate between variants that promote and variants that diminish a concept ([Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)), or the "concept effects for a specific question" of [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), a KL divergence (a measure of how much one probability distribution differs from another) between counterfactual and original answer distributions averaged over alternative concept values;
- upper and lower bounds on the monitor's recall on "intervention-caused outcomes", $r_c=\Pr(Z(1)=1\mid Y(1)=1,Y(0)=0)$, where $Y(x)$ and $Z(x)$ are the potential outcome and the monitor's decision when $X$ is set to $x$; the bounds assume a single stable structural causal model (a set of equations that fix how each variable depends on the others), consistency (the observed outcome is the potential outcome of the condition that was run) and $p_1>p_0$ ([Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving)).

Three papers explain why a single flip is not enough. [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) reject the criterion that labels each response by whether it flips, because it "ignores the stochastic and chaotic nature of LLM output sampling". [Angdembay et al. 2026](./#p=angdembay2026two) note that sampling the two conditions independently records "an observed conditional flip rather than a within-pair counterfactual". [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) show that separate samples from the two conditions (arms) "identify the arm distributions but generally not this cross-world coupling", that is, not the link between what the same case would do under each condition. [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) also find that a third alternate candidate answer "flips 11-23% of verdicts", so their dependence label depends on which alternate answer defines the counterfactual. This group holds 23 definitions from 13 papers (17 proposed, 2 adopted, 4 adapted).

> Consider the rate at which the model predicts the answer that matches the hint when the hint is present, versus when it is absent; we term the difference in these two rates the total effect, $\mathrm{TE}$ .
>
> ([Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), §4)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) | total effect (TE) of the hint | operational | “we term the difference in these two rates the total effect” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought) | total effect (hint intervention vs. control arm) | formal | “Total effect is P (y = 1\|x = 1) - P (y = 1\|x = 0).” |
| [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) | total effect (attribute removal) | formal | “a lower bound on the rate of target-answer responses with systematic attribute influence” |
| [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) | per-response flip criterion (rejected) | criterion | “This criterion, however, ignores the stochastic and chaotic nature of LLM output sampling.” |
| [Angdembay et al. 2026](./#p=angdembay2026two) | within-pair counterfactual (vs. observed conditional flip) | criterion | “the label records an observed conditional flip rather than a within-pair counterfactual” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) | counterfactual target (intervention-caused outcome) | informal | “detecting intervention-caused outcomes is a counterfactual target” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) | potential outcome and monitor decision under do(X = x) | formal | “Let Y (x) and Z(x) denote the potential outcome and monitor decision under do(X = x)” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) | cross-world coupling (non-identification of causal recall) | criterion | “Separate arm samples identify the arm distributions but generally not this cross-world coupling.” |
| [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) | single-factor intervention (identification assumption) | criterion | “$\Delta$ is the only controlled difference between $X$ and $X^{\prime}$ , so any change in behavior can be attributed to it” |
| [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) | hint-caused switch rate (hint influence) under uniform switching | formal | “$\alpha=I/p$ is the fraction of switches to $h$ that the hint caused” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | causal concept effect (CE) | formal | “The Kullback-Leibler divergence between $\mathcal{M}$ ’s answer distribution in response to counterfactual input” |
| [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) | same-answer counterfactual (dependence label) | operational | “a trace is dependent if the model does not answer h under the matched control and independent if it does” |
| [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) | ambiguous example (verdict depends on the alternate candidate) | criterion | “the dependence verdict flips depending on which alternate wrong candidate defines the counterfactual” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | cue-influence label (hint setting) | operational | “positive cases are those in which the cue raises the rate of the cue-consistent answer by at least 50pp” |
| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | empirical influence estimate (counterfactual flip fraction) | formal | Share of $n$ sampled replacements of factor $j$ that change the answer ($n=3$ by default). |

The group's 8 other definitions: [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) (Assumption 1 (one SCM, consistency)); [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) (effect size Δ (bias strength)); [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) (direct effect on the early answer (frozen CoT state)); [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) (pair-centered causal effect (role-swapped benchmark)); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (empirical flip rate); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (flip (label mode vs. feature mode)); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (perturbation categories (flip_inducing / boundary / non_flip)); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (score-changing perturbation (BLOOM flip)).

The papers' formulas:
- Total effect: $P(y=1\mid x=1)-P(y=1\mid x=0)$ ([Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought)); $\mathrm{TE}:=p_{11}-p_{01}$, which, when $\mathrm{TE}\ge 0$, lower-bounds the rate of target-answer responses with "systematic attribute influence" among all $(X=1,T=1)$ responses, assuming that the target-answer rate without such influence in this condition is at most $p_{01}$ ([Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain)).
- Hint-caused switch rate under uniform switching: $I=p-q/(\bar{n}-2)$ and $\alpha=I/p$ ([Little 2026](./#p=little2026length)).
- Causal concept effect: $\mathrm{CE}(\mathbf{x},C_m)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{C}'_m|}\sum_{c'_m}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(Y\mid\mathbf{x}_{c_m\to c'_m})\,\|\,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(Y\mid\mathbf{x})\big)$ ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk)).
- Empirical flip rate: $g(x)=\frac{1}{K^2}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\mathbf{1}[a_b^{(i)}(x)\neq a_\ell^{(j)}(x)]$ ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating)).
- Influence estimate: $\hat{D}_j=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[M(x^{\setminus j,v_i})\neq y]$ ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit)).
- Bias strength: $\Delta=p_{\text{pos}}-p_{\text{neg}}$ ([Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)).
- Direct effect on the early answer with the CoT state frozen: $\mathrm{DE}_{\mathrm{EA}}^{(i^*)}=P(Y=1\mid X=1,do(H^{(i^*)}=H_0^{(i^*)}))-P(Y=1\mid X=0)$ ([Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain)).
- Pair-centered effect: $\log p(h_1\mid W(h_1))-\log p(h_1\mid G(h_2))$ minus the pair mean ([Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary)).

### 7. What makes a counterfactual valid: one change only, edit quality and controls

These definitions describe assumptions, validity checks, quality measures and controls for constructing counterfactual variants and interpreting their effects, including a locality measure used "as a soft filter" ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval)). Thirteen papers contribute definitions, which fall into seven categories:
- isolation: only the target changes ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit), [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases), [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would));
- quality of a generated edit: coherence and completeness ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk)), four criteria for injected reasoning ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval)), semantic opposition of a contrary hint ([Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm)), label flip rate and textual similarity ([Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm)), and residual leakage of the original answer ([Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when));
- on-policy edits: text "the model could have plausibly produced" ([Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought));
- informativeness: the injected reasoning must oppose the model's baseline stance ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval));
- executability: both plans must succeed when the agent is forced to execute them ([Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan));
- controls: null edits that preserve the meaning and should not change the answer ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal)) and matched swaps that keep the claim fixed and swap only the evidence ([Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable));
- for a parameter edit, efficacy and specificity of unlearning ([Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)): the unlearned reasoning step becomes less likely, and the model's answers on other, unrelated questions from the same dataset do not change.

Five papers report how much their checks remove: the isolation check of [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) "removed $42$ % of candidate concepts"; [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) "started with 8,499 instances and removed 1,313"; the single-factor filter of [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) "retains $\sim$ 90% of claims"; the validity gates of [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) reduce 96 retail tasks to 62 ("17 not ratified, 17 not force-executable"); and the edit filters and verifier of [Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when) keep 683 of 1,246 HotpotQA and 417 of 973 MuSiQue candidates. This group holds 25 definitions from 13 papers (22 proposed, 2 adopted, 1 adapted).

> Good perturbations should preserve the coherence of the input, vary the target factor enough to change the answer when that factor matters, and leave the other factors fixed.
>
> ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit), §3.2)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | counterfactual distribution (perturbation design) | criterion | “vary the target factor enough to change the answer when that factor matters, and leave the other factors fixed” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) | confound (in counterfactual variations) | failure-mode | “changes to the input that are unrelated to the target concept but could independently affect the model’s decision” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) | not a valid counterfactual (variation rating 1) | taxonomy | “Multiple or major changes; cannot attribute differences to the concept.” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | confounded / unstable counterfactual | criterion | “A counterfactual may be confounded, editing several variables at once.” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | disentangled concepts (assumption for concept interventions) | criterion | “each concept $C_{m}$ can be changed without affecting other concepts $C_{n\neq m}$” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | counterfactual quality (coherency, completeness, minimality) | criterion | “Coherency: Is the question grammatically correct and semantically coherent?” |
| [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) | on-policy intervention (vs. off-policy) | informal | “We call an intervention on-policy if the model could have plausibly produced the new text from the upstream CoT.” |
| [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) | proper counterfactual contrast (contrast precondition) | criterion | “For causal identifiability, we impose a contrast precondition” |
| [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) | valid counterfactual reasoning (validation rubric) | criterion | “the reasoning is sufficient to steer a reader toward exactly one specific incorrect answer” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | accepted causally-invalidating edit | formal | “accepted by the verifier as breaking the trace validity” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | null interventions (answer preservation / spurious flip rate) | operational | “semantics-preserving transformations that should not change the answer” |
| [Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable) | matched counterfactual (claim fixed, evidence swapped) | operational | “holding the claim text fixed while swapping only the evidence passage” |
| [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) | forced execution (validity gate) | criterion | “behavioral proof that both outcomes are achievable rather than merely plausible” |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | valid intervention (efficacy and specificity controls) | criterion | “the target information is removed (high efficacy), but the model retains its general capabilities” |
| [Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when) | residual leakage (counterfactual evidence) | failure-mode | “an edited version can still imply $a_{0}$ in ways that the string checks do not catch” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | label flip rate | operational | “counterfactuals that preserve the surface form of $Q$ but flip the label are considered more challenging and informative” |

The group's 9 other definitions: [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) (valid counterfactual (variation isolation check)); [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) (clean isolation (variation rating 5)); [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) (contrary-hint quality (semantic opposition check)); [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) (validity gates (ratification, forced execution, success-only scoring)); [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) (success-only scoring (validity gate)); [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) (contrast coverage c(M)); [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) (externality penalty E(r′) (locality of counterfactual reasoning)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (minimal counterfactual (textual similarity)); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (verified explanation (counterfactual support score)).

The papers' formulas:
- Contrast precondition and coverage ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval)), where $S$ gives the stance of a piece of reasoning: $\delta(x,r';\mathcal{M})=\mathbf{1}\{S(r)\neq S(r')\}$ and $c(\mathcal{M})=\Pr_{(x,r')\sim\mathcal{D}}(\delta(x,r';\mathcal{M})=1)$.
- Externality penalty ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval)): $E(r')=1-\mathrm{Jaccard}(\mathcal{V}_x,\mathcal{V}_{r'})$.
- Accepted edits ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal)): $q\triangleq\Pr(A)$ and $\mu_A\triangleq\mathbb{E}[D(p(Y\mid X,T),p(Y\mid X,T'))\mid A]$, where $A$ is the event that the edit is proposed and accepted as breaking trace validity.

### 8. The counterfactual test of explanations: does the explanation mention the edit?

These definitions use the same kind of counterfactual as group 5, an edit to the input, but they judge the explanation, not the model's reliance on the edited element. The procedure is that of Atanasova et al. 2023, which 13 of the 17 definitions cite: words are inserted into the input, and if the prediction changes while the explanation of the edited instance does not mention the inserted words, the explanation counts as unfaithful. Siegel et al. 2024, cited by 8 definitions, replace the binary outcome with a correlation between mentions and the shift in the label distribution (the Correlational Counterfactual Test, CCT). Neither paper is in the map. Variants insert GPT-4o-generated adjectives or adverbs, with 20 interventions per original instance ([Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique)), or inject naturalistic hints and check explanations for "distinctive keywords" associated with each hint type ([Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness)); [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) describe these tests as methods that generate counterfactual texts with auxiliary models. Two definitions are proposed, both by [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique): the counter rate (defined below) and the use of the inserted word as "the true reasoning factor". This group holds 17 definitions from 12 papers (2 proposed, 12 adopted, 3 adapted). In the quotes below, NLE stands for natural-language explanation.

> The counterfactual test works by making an intervention to the original instance to get an intervened instance. The evaluation then consists of two steps: (1) Identify counter instances: intervened instances whose prediction changes compared to the original instance. (2) Identify unfaithful instances: counter instances whose NLEs (generated by baseline methods or SR-NLE) do not contain the intervened word (determined by string matching).
>
> ([Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique), §4.3)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | Counterfactual Edits (test) | operational | “If the LLM changes its prediction after the counterfactual intervention, and the explanation does not mention the inserted words, the explanation is judged unfaithful” |
| [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) | counterfactual test | operational | “counter instances whose NLEs (generated by baseline methods or SR-NLE) do not contain the intervened word (determined by string matching)” |
| [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) | intervention (inserted adjective/adverb) | operational | “For nouns, we prepend a random adjective, and for verbs, we prepend a random adverb.” |
| [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) | intervened word as the true reasoning factor | criterion | “the intervened word (i.e., the true reasoning factor that made the label change)” |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) | counterfactual edit (faithfulness test) | operational | “insert tokens that flip the prediction and check whether explanations reflect these edits” |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) | Counterfactual Edits (faithfulness metric) | operational | “assess changes in explanations resulting from input modifications” |
| [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) | Counterfactual Tests (CT) (original) | operational | “making small edits to the input, typically only a few tokens, and then checking whether those tokens appear in the explanation” |
| [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) | Counterfactual Test (CT) | operational | “verifying whether the explanation explicitly mentions a small set of distinctive keywords associated with each hint type” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) | Correlational Counterfactual Test (CCT) | operational | “considers the total shift in predicted label distributions rather than just binary outcomes” |
| [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) | CCT (correlational counterfactual test) | informal | “computes population-level correlations between counterfactual interventions and explanation mentions” |
| [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) | CCT / Phi-CCT (correlational counterfactual measure) | informal | “refined this into a correlational measure, aligning mentions with the graded influence of interventions” |
| [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) | Counterfactual Intervention (CI) faithfulness test | operational | “The self-NLE is deemed faithful if it aligns with the specific CI that caused the prediction change.” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | counterfactual edits (faithfulness test) | operational | “they assess the faithfulness of explanations given to the counterfactual questions” |
| [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) | counterfactual approaches (explanation faithfulness tests) | informal | “test whether free-text explanations reflect model decisions through input edits, reconstruction, or output-distribution shifts” |
| [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift) | counterfactual generation (perturbed input) | informal | “we modify key parts w of the inputs I and examine the resulting changes in the generated rationales” |

The group's 2 other definitions: [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) (counterfactual tests); [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) (counter rate).

In formulas, restating [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) and [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique): let $x'$ be the edited input and $w$ the inserted words; an instance is unfaithful when $f(x')\neq f(x)$ and $w$ does not occur in the explanation $e(x')$. [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) check occurrence by string matching and report the counter rate, the share of 20,000 intervened instances per model and dataset whose prediction changes.

### 9. Editing the reasoning: a changed, truncated or resampled chain of thought

This is the group with the most definitions. Of the 49 definitions, 41 concern methods applied to the CoT and 8 concern generated mediators (intermediate outputs between the input and the answer), seeded plans, rationale or evidence spans, or reasoning traces paired with evidence other than their own; not all of these methods edit the tested model's own output or hold its input fixed, since [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) include "Option Shuffling", [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) uses "Fixed template plans", and [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) generate counterfactual reasoning from the problem. Thirteen of the 49 definitions cite [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring). The main interventions are of eight kinds; other definitions cover "Removing Steps" and the replacement of a single token followed by regeneration of the rest of the sentence ([Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting)):
- truncating the CoT and forcing an early answer ([Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop), [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace), [Wang et al. 2026c](./#p=wang2026verbalising), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting));
- an inserted mistake or a single decisive error ([Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting), [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp));
- paraphrase ([Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting));
- a contradicting step, operator swap, changed conclusion or irrelevant fact ([Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project), [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal), [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), [Swaroop et al. 2025](./#p=swaroop2025lift));
- a substituted whole rationale or injected flawed reasoning ([Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc), [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval), [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith));
- resampling the CoT from a sentence onward, and transplanting the start of a CoT written with a hint into the prompt without the hint ([Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought));
- counterfactual chains in a causal mediation analysis, which separates the effect that passes through the reasoning from the direct effect ([Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making), [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept), [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought)), tests that [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms) restate without the mediation framing as replacing the CoT "with counterfactual content";
- edits to other intermediates: a generated mediator ([Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking)), a seeded plan ([Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan)), a rationale or evidence span ([Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable)), a stored trace answered against edited evidence ([Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when)).

If the answer follows the edit, it is taken to depend on the reasoning. [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) write that "Higher COS indicates greater faithfulness" (COS, Counterfactual Outcome Sensitivity, is the share of correctly answered questions whose answer changes after a logical edit to the reasoning); [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) describe such tests as ones that "implicitly treat faithfulness as a causal metric" and argue that for monitorability the relevant question is whether CoT tokens "explain the target variable", not whether they cause it (disagreement 9). This group holds 49 definitions from 26 papers (27 proposed, 18 adopted, 4 adapted).

> Also, it should be constructed to admit localized output-level counterfactual edits to the reasoning trace while holding the input fixed, allowing attribution of ensuing output changes to the stated reasoning.
>
> ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval), §3.1)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) | output-level counterfactual reasoning intervention | informal | “localized output-level counterfactual edits to the reasoning trace while holding the input fixed” |
| [Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project) | counterfactual thought | formal | “$do(s_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k})$ , where $s^{\prime}_{k}$ is a counterfactual thought generated to contradict the original reasoning” |
| [Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project) | counterfactual answer | formal | “we generate a counterfactual answer $a^{*}$ by re-executing the agent from the point of intervention” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | Counterfactual Outcome Sensitivity (COS) | operational | “the percentage of correctly-answered questions where logical perturbations change the final answer” |
| [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc) | counterfactual rationale | informal | “a counterfactual rationale that entails the opposite label (substitution)” |
| [Swaroop et al. 2025](./#p=swaroop2025lift) | counterfactual intervention (irrelevant-fact step swap) | operational | “we replace $r_{i}$ with a domain-matched but irrelevant fact” |
| [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) | counterfactual draft | informal | “we alter the draft’s conclusion to create a counterfactual version $T^{\prime}$” |
| [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) | counterfactual resampling | operational | “resampling from position $i$ onward, keeping $S_{1},\ldots,S_{i-1}$ as prefix” |
| [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) | transplant resampling | operational | “transplant portions of said CoT into a model prompted with the same question but without any hint” |
| [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) | prefix counterfactual (forced answering) | formal | “append a stop-and-answer instruction to prefix $y_{1:t}$ and greedily decode an answer” |
| [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) | counterfactual operator Δ (counterfactual-based methods) | formal | “If $a^{\prime}\neq a$ , the perturbed reasoning content is considered causally influential to the prediction.” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp) | targeted semantic perturbation c_err | operational | “contains exactly ONE decisive error introduced at a single reasoning step” |
| [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | counterfactual reasoning (intervention on input or mediator) | informal | “measure their effect on the reasoning outcome $Y$ by keeping $R$ fixed (direct effect)” |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) | counterfactual intervention (CNF) | informal | “A faithful model should shift its output accordingly.” |
| [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) | counterfactual plan seeding | criterion | “swaps between two genuinely valid, agent-endorsed plans” |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | intervention methods (causal faithfulness tests) | informal | “implicitly treat faithfulness as a causal metric by intervening on latent representations and observing changes in target variables” |

[Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable) (counterfactual example (proof/rationale cross-wiring)); [Angdembay et al. 2026](./#p=angdembay2026two) (graph-targeted counterfactual interventions); [Anonymous 2026 (DCO)](./#p=anon2026generating) (counterfactual target (intervention contexts)); [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) (counterfactual perturbation regimes); [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) (paired graph experiment (perturbed CoT c′)); [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) (CoT perturbation (ablation, mistake insertion)); [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) (validity-gated counterfactual test); [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) (multi-turn counterfactual injection (closed-API approximation)); [Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project) (intervention modalities (counterfactual step types)); [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms) (counterfactual CoT content); [Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when) (crossed trace-evidence conflicts (matched and crossed cells)); [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith) (step-level counterfactual verification); [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith) (counterfactual reasoning intervention (RFEval)); [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) (counterfactual importance); [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) (counterfactual++ importance); [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) (resilience); [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) (sentence-cluster causal effect); [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) (mathematical counterfactual (edited CoT)); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (counterfactual necessity score); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (counterfactual sentence); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (counterfactual-based methods (perturbation strategies)); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Adding Mistakes); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Option Shuffling); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Removing Steps); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Early Answering); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Paraphrasing); [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) (adversarial counterfactual sensitivity vs. constructive sensitivity); [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) (intervened mediator with deterministic counterfactual target); [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) (counterfactual reasoning-chain interventions); [Wang et al. 2026c](./#p=wang2026verbalising) (counterfactual-based baseline (Early Ans)); [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) (counterfactual interventions on the CoT).

The papers' formulas:
- Counterfactual Sensitivity, for an input $X$, a reasoning trace $T$, its edited version $T'$ and the answer $Y$: $\mathrm{CS}(X;T\to T')=D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(p(Y\mid X,T)\,\|\,p(Y\mid X,T')\big)$; Counterfactual Outcome Sensitivity (COS) is its binary-flip version ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal)).
- Step intervention $do(s_k=s'_k)$, with counterfactual answer $a^*=f_a(q,\{s_1,\dots,\iota(s_k),\dots,s^*_n\};\theta)$, where later steps are re-sampled ([Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project)).
- Counterfactual importance $D_{KL}[p(A'_{S_i}\mid T_i\not\approx S_i)\,\|\,p(A_{S_i})]$ and counterfactual++ importance $D_{KL}[p(A'_{S_i}\mid \forall j\ge i: T_j\not\approx S_i)\,\|\,p(A_{S_i})]$ ([Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought)).
- Sentence-cluster effect: $\mathrm{Effect}(C)=\mathbb{E}_{s_i\in C}[p(A_{\text{yes}}\mid\mathrm{CoT}_{1\dots i})-p(A_{\text{yes}}\mid\mathrm{CoT}_{1\dots i-1})]$ ([Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought)).
- Counterfactual operator: $\mathcal{C}'=\Delta(\mathcal{C})$ and $a'=\mathrm{M}_{\text{Ans}}(q,\mathcal{C}')$ ([Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting)); counterfactual sentence $r_t^{(\mu\to\tilde{o})}=(o_{t,<\mu},\tilde{o}_{t,\mu},\hat{o}_{t,>\mu})$ with the suffix regenerated ([Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting)).
- Paired graph experiment $E=(x,c,c',y)$, with attribution graphs $G_0=G(x,c,y)$ and $G_1=G(x,c',y)$ over one feature space ([Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace)).

### 10. Counterfactuals as training data or reward

These definitions use counterfactuals as training data or as a reward signal, not as a test. Some objectives set an answer or response target, others a smoothed answer distribution or a divergence to maximize. The training objectives of 7 of the 9 papers encourage sensitivity to reasoning changes, invariance to misleading context, or both, as [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case) combine counterfactual-CoT and biased-instruction objectives:
- sensitivity: the model is trained to follow a rationale that leads to a different answer ([Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott), [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making), [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case)), to increase KL divergence between answer distributions under original and edited traces ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal)), or to lower its confidence when the rationale is masked or corrupted ([Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto));
- invariance: the model is trained to give, under a biasing or pressuring context, the response it gives without that context ([Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented), [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning)). [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) call the targets "the model’s own honest response" and add an acknowledgment of the pressure to the trace.

[Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) use counterfactual predictions to add a disclaimer about the use of the cue to the inputs of a reward model, under two rules: strategy D appends it whenever the predictions differ, while strategy C appends it only when the original prediction is cued (correct or stereotypical) and the prediction without the cue is not. This group holds 15 definitions from 9 papers (12 proposed, 3 adapted).

> we train the student to conduct counterfactual reasoning Roese (1997), i.e., answer accordingly when the rationale is leading to a different answer.
>
> ([Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott), §3.2)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) | counterfactual reasoning | informal | “answer accordingly when the rationale is leading to a different answer” |
| [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) | counterfactual rationale / counterfactual reasoning loss | formal | “a counterfactual rationale $r^{{}^{\prime}}$ that leads to the wrong answer $a^{{}^{\prime}}$” |
| [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | counterfactual chains | informal | “alternative chains that can lead to different outcomes” |
| [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case) | counterfactual CoT | informal | “the answer is replaced by a stronger-LLM-generated counterfactual answer $y_{i}^{\prime}$ satisfying $y_{i}^{\prime}\neq y_{i}$” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | counterfactual trace | informal | “penalizes the model if predictions remain unchanged” |
| [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) | counterfactual regularization (perturbed rationales) | informal | “the reasoning module is regularized to output less confident predictions when the rationale is not utilized properly” |
| [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) | token masking (rationale ignored) | operational | “To simulate this, we mask out the rationales in the input.” |
| [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) | token replacement (rationale misused) | operational | “we randomly replace $k\%$ of the rationale tokens with other tokens” |
| [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) | counterfactual inputs (bias augmentation) | informal | “a more diverse range of counterfactual inputs than just the presence vs. absence of biasing augmentations” |
| [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) | counterfactual distillation (CD) | formal | “teacher-forces the model’s own neutral response under the trigger” |
| [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) | pressure-aware counterfactual targets | informal | “the model’s own honest response, with a trace that registers the pressure and declines to be moved by it” |
| [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) | counterfactual-augmented reward model (strategies D and C) | operational | “we append the disclaimer whenever $\operatorname{pred}(y)\neq\operatorname{pred}(y^{\prime})$” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | counterfactual consistency (reversed-question pairs) | informal | “counterfactual consistency signals, analogous to those provided by our reversed-question methodology, can substantially improve faithfulness at training time” |

The group's 2 other definitions: [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) (pressure-aware counterfactual target (ℓ_aware)); [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) (counterfactual regularization loss).

The papers' training objectives as formulas:
- Counterfactual loss ([Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott)): $\mathcal{L}_{counterfactual}=-\sum_i\log P(t_i\mid q,r',t_{<i})$ over the answer tokens of a wrong answer $a'$ that the rationale $r'$ supports.
- Counterfactual regularization ([Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto)): $\mathcal{L}_{\text{c-reg}}=-\sum_{a_i\in A}Q'(a_i\mid q,A)\log P(a_i\mid q,A,R')$, with label-smoothed targets $Q'$ on perturbed rationales $R'$.
- Counterfactual distillation ([Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning)): $\ell_{\mathrm{CD}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}_i\log\pi_\theta(y^H_i,t^H_i\mid x_i,c^+_i)$ with $(y^H_i,t^H_i)\sim\pi_0(\cdot\mid x_i,c^-_i)$; the pressure-aware target $\ell_{\mathrm{aware}}$ prepends an acknowledgment $a(c^+_i)$ to $t^H_i$.

### 11. Counterfactual attention weights, activations and parameters

The definitions cover alternative attention distributions, activations and model parameters, as well as knowledge edits given in context ([Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal)) and text with permuted tokens, used to compare Jacobians, the matrices of derivatives of outputs with respect to inputs ([Wang et al. 2025a](./#p=wang2025causallyenhanced)):
- an attention distribution, permuted or adversarially searched with the encoder states held fixed ([Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention)), and the same framework as read by [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention);
- activations taken from a counterfactual input and patched into the original run ([Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training), [Bhupatiraju & Nyaupane 2026](./#p=bhupatiraju2026stated), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective));
- an edited knowledge triple (a fact stated as subject, relation and object), injected in context or by weight editing ([Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal));
- a token-permuted span at which a Jacobian is taken ([Wang et al. 2025a](./#p=wang2025causallyenhanced));
- the model's parameters after a reasoning step is unlearned ([Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)).

In activation patching, selected activations are transferred from a donor run (on the counterfactual input) into the original run, whose text is kept; the counterfactual also defines the expected answer in [Bhupatiraju & Nyaupane 2026](./#p=bhupatiraju2026stated) and contrasting ground-truth explanation labels in [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective). Two definitions change the weights: [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) unlearn a reasoning step to obtain a modified model $\mathcal{M}^{*}$, and [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) inject edited knowledge with MEMIT, which is "directly modifying specific model weights", as well as in context. This group holds 11 definitions from 8 papers (8 proposed, 3 adopted).

> scenarios corresponding to alternative (counterfactual) attention weights. The idea is to investigate whether the prediction would have been different, had the model emphasized (attended to) different input features.
>
> ([Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention), §4.2)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) | counterfactual attention weights | informal | “whether the prediction would have been different, had the model emphasized (attended to) different input features” |
| [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) | adversarial attention | operational | “maximally distinct from $\hat{\alpha}$ but that nonetheless yields an equivalent prediction” |
| [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) | attention permutation (counterfactual attention) | operational | “we simply scramble the original attention weights $\hat{\alpha}$” |
| [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention) | counterfactual attention weights (counterfactual framework) | criterion | “if a model is capable of producing multiple sets of diverse attention weights for the same prediction, then the relationship between inputs and outputs used to make predictions is not understood by attention analysis” |
| [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) | counterfactual input (activation patching) | informal | “a counterfactual sentence $x^{\prime}$ with the same relation but differing subject and object” |
| [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) | activation patching (intervention T_patch) | formal | “replace the activation $h_{\ell,t}(x)$ with the corresponding activation $h_{\ell,t}(x^{\prime})$” |
| [Bhupatiraju & Nyaupane 2026](./#p=bhupatiraju2026stated) | counterfactual (per-hop knowledge-base edit) | informal | “a single fact resolving hop $k$ which is further altered” |
| [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) | counterfactual inputs (source/target pair for activation patching) | operational | “share the same hint cue $C=C^{\prime}$ , but have different cue-included behavioral responses” |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) | counterfactual (knowledge edit) | informal | “These counterfactuals yield the same answer but are based on different reasoning.” |
| [Wang et al. 2025a](./#p=wang2025causallyenhanced) | source-side counterfactual (token permutation) | operational | “permuting the tokens of $A$ (and of $M$ when present)” |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | parameter intervention (unlearning a reasoning step) | informal | “its modified version $\mathcal{M}^{*}$ should not produce the same predictions or reasoning that $\mathcal{M}$ did” |

The papers' formulas:
- Activation patching ([Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training)): $\mathcal{M}(x;\,h_{\ell,t}(x)\leftarrow h_{\ell,t}(x'))$, scored by $d(\mathcal{M}(x),\mathcal{M}(x;\,h_{\ell,t}(x)\leftarrow h_{\ell,t}(x')))$.
- Adversarial attention ([Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention)): the largest Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD) from the original attention $\hat\alpha$ among attention weights whose output stays within $\epsilon$ of the original output in total variation distance (TVD), with $\epsilon=0.01$ for classification and $0.05$ for QA.

### 12. Counterfactual conditions as baselines for judging scheming, obfuscation and dishonesty

These definitions use a counterfactual condition as the baseline against which a covert behavior, or an alternative explanation of it, is judged. The definitions cover six behaviors:
- sandbagging (deliberate underperformance): the environment's incentive is reversed, and the behavior is compared across the two conditions ([Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress));
- sabotage: the same coding task is framed as for the model itself or for its successor, and "o4-mini SAB underperforms on its successor’s code at a significantly higher rate than on its own" ([Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress));
- evaluation awareness: awareness is injected into or removed from the CoT to measure its "causal effect ... on the pursuit of covert actions" ([Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress));
- steganographic reliance (information hidden in the text): an ordinary reinforcement learning (RL) agent that "learns to evade the monitor to secretly encode information" shows a large gap between the original and the "counterfactual" reward when only the applicant's nationality is changed ([Farquhar et al. 2025](./#p=farquhar2025mona));
- obfuscated denial: a judge asks whether reasoning "could ... plausibly have been produced in a scenario where no defect existed"; it labels "93.8% of behavior-only CoTs as impossible without the defect, compared to only 38.4% for the monitor-aware model" ([Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training));
- dishonesty and false self-reports: a neutral variant of each query verifies what the model knows before honesty is scored under pressure ([Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating)); when review targets are generated for an honest persona, biased and unbiased prompts verify "whether specific factors actually influenced behavior" ([Dietz et al. 2026](./#p=dietz2026split)); a persona system prompt tests whether a model's denial that its prefilled output was tampered with reflects "a fixed “safe and reliable assistant” persona" ([Nguyen et al. 2026](./#p=nguyen2026can)).

In these definitions the counterfactual term meets the definitions of scheming and obfuscation in the [monitorability](#monitorability) section and the definitions of honesty in the [faithfulness](#faithfulness) section. This group holds 8 definitions from 6 papers (8 proposed).

> The non-counterfactual condition incentivizes low performance by stating that high performance leads to negative outcomes, while the counterfactual condition reverses this incentive.
>
> ([Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress), App. F.3)

| Paper | Term defined | Type | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | counterfactual condition | operational | “The non-counterfactual condition incentivizes low performance by stating that high performance leads to negative outcomes, while the counterfactual condition reverses this incentive.” |
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | self vs. successor counterfactual | operational | “we compare the self vs. successor counterfactual” |
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | counterfactual interventions on the CoT | informal | “counterfactual interventions on the CoT, where we inject or remove situational awareness” |
| [Farquhar et al. 2025](./#p=farquhar2025mona) | counterfactual reward (nationality intervention) | operational | “applications that are identical to the training data apart from nationality” |
| [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) | counterfactual autorater | operational | “could the model’s reasoning plausibly have been produced in a scenario where no defect existed?” |
| [Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating) | counterfactual samples (neutral query variant) | informal | “the need to verify the model’s knowledge using counterfactual samples” |
| [Dietz et al. 2026](./#p=dietz2026split) | A/B prompt method (biased vs unbiased system prompt) | operational | “by comparing responses to biased versus unbiased prompts, we can verify whether specific factors actually influenced behavior” |
| [Nguyen et al. 2026](./#p=nguyen2026can) | persona counterfactual | operational | “a fixed “safe and reliable assistant” persona that claims its outputs and denies tampering whatever the prior turn contained” |

### Who borrows which definition from whom

A definition is borrowed when its paper adopts or adapts it from earlier work. Of the 292 definitions, 132 are borrowed (86 adopted, 46 adapted), and they come from 62 papers. Of these, 76 cite at least one source in the map and 56 cite only sources outside it. Counting each definition once for each source it cites, 13 sources are borrowed by at least 5 definitions:

| Source | Borrowing definitions | Borrowing papers | In the map | Group with the most borrowing definitions |

| Atanasova et al. 2023 | 18 | 14 | no | 8 (13 definitions) |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | 17 | 10 | yes | 2 (14 definitions) |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | 15 | 9 | yes | 9 (13 definitions) |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | 12 | 9 | yes | 5 (9 definitions) |
| Siegel et al. 2024 | 9 | 8 | no | 8 (8 definitions) |
| Pearl (2001, 2009, 2021, 2022) | 9 | 7 | no | 6 (3 definitions) |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | 7 | 4 | yes | 5 (3 definitions) |
| Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 | 7 | 6 | no | 2 (7 definitions) |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | 5 | 4 | yes | 5 (3 definitions) |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | 5 | 5 | yes | 2 (2 definitions) |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | 5 | 4 | yes | 1 (3 definitions) |
| Hase & Bansal 2020 | 5 | 3 | no | 2 (5 definitions) |
| [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | 5 | 5 | yes | 9 (4 definitions) |

Eight of the 13 sources are in the map and five are not. The most-borrowed source, Atanasova et al. 2023, is outside the map, and so is Siegel et al. 2024, the second source of the same group (group 8). Atanasova et al. and [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) are borrowed by 18 and 17 definitions, but 13 of the definitions that borrow from Atanasova et al. fall in group 8 and 14 of those that borrow from Chen et al. fall in group 2, and no definition cites both. [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) come closest: they list Atanasova et al. and Siegel et al. among works that "measure faithfulness through the framework of counterfactual simulatability".

The chains of borrowing, by topic (in a chain, "A to B" means that B borrows from A):
- **Simulatability.** Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 and Hase & Bansal 2020 are cited by [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) for the concept's "long history". Chen et al. are borrowed by 10 papers: [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness), [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent), [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek), [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain), [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching), [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm), [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) and [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would). Two second-hand links follow: [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) borrow from [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would), and [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient) from [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm). [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) cite Doshi-Velez & Kim directly and are borrowed by 5 papers: [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective), [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would), [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) and [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl).
- **Self-generated counterfactual explanations.** Wachter et al. 2017 supply the definition in [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms), an alternative input "that would lead to a specific alternative output"; [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) cite them, with Mothilal et al. 2020 and Verma et al. 2024, for generating a counterfactual by first choosing a counterfactual output and then optimizing the input toward it, and for closeness as "a key desideratum of a counterfactual explanation". The self-generated form passes through four papers, each borrowing from the one before: [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) to [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (adapted), to [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms), to [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating). Madsen et al. are also borrowed directly by [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating), [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) and [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating).
- **Counterfactual test.** Atanasova et al. 2023 are borrowed by 14 papers, Siegel et al. 2024 by 8 and Siegel et al. 2025 by 2 ([Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training)). [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) merge the test with the hint pairs of [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) and cite both.
- **Cue pairs.** [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) are borrowed by 9 papers, [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) by 4 ([Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness), [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length), [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm)) and [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) by 2 ([Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual)). [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) are borrowed by [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought), [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) and [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), and Xiong et al. in turn by [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight). [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) are borrowed by [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective), and [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) by [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient).
- **Concept edits.** [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) are borrowed by [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal), [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) and [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating).
- **Edits to the reasoning.** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) are borrowed by 9 papers: [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning), [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp), [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace), [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training), [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self), [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) and [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting). [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) are borrowed by [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting), [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case), [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) and [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought); [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) by [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith); Bogdan et al. 2025 by [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought).
- **Others.** [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) are borrowed by [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention), and [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) by [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) and [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning).

The definitions change in six ways as they are passed from paper to paper.

1. **Simulatability changes status.** [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) ask whether an explanation lets humans "precisely infer the model’s outputs on diverse counterfactuals of the explained input", and write that "simulatability is a special case of faithfulness" and "a faithful explanation is not necessarily simulatable". [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) call it "The counterfactual simulatability framework of explanation faithfulness". In [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) it becomes a biconditional, a condition that is both necessary and sufficient: "an explanation is faithful if and only if the explanation allows a human or model to accurately predict how the model will behave on other relevant inputs". [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) assess explanation usefulness by the difference in simulation accuracy with and without explanations, while observers "predict the model’s outputs on counterfactual variants". [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) change the counterfactuals: Chen et al. are said to argue that "a good explanation should help an observer predict behavior under counterfactual edits". Three things shift along the chain: the observer goes from humans to "a human or model", the counterfactuals go from LLM-written related questions to edits, and the notion goes from a special case of faithfulness to a necessary and sufficient condition for it. The check of borrowed definitions against their sources (described in the next part) records the biconditional as adapted from Chen et al., meaning that Turpin et al. modify a definition that Chen et al. give.
2. **The counterfactual explanation gains a target output, changes author and loses the target again.** In [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can), after Wachter et al., it is an input that "would lead the model to change its decision". [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) tie it to "a specific alternative output", and [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) to "the opposite prediction". The edit is then no longer found by an optimizer: [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) "ask the model itself to generate the counterfactual explanation", and [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) add "editing-distance constraints specified in a prompt". [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) recast it as "the propose-and-verify form of Edit-Proposal" and drop the target label: the edit counts as a flip if "the fraction of resamples that differ from baseline exceeds $0.5$".
3. **The counterfactual test comes to cover more methods.** [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) describe Atanasova et al. as training "a helper model to insert words into the LLM input". [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) generate the inserted modifiers with GPT-4o. [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) describe counterfactual approaches as testing explanations "through input edits, reconstruction, or output-distribution shifts", [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) as using "auxiliary models to generate counterfactual texts designed to change the LLM outcome", and [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) as using "controlled interventions, such as the introduction of biasing hints". By this point the label "counterfactual test" also covers the hint pairs that [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) had filed under counterfactual simulatability. [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) restate random word insertion as a counterfactual set and list it with unconstrained LLM-generated edits under "Irrelevant or incoherent perturbations", approaches that "often produce counterfactuals that are irrelevant or drift from the natural data distribution".
4. **Borrowers add the word "counterfactual".** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) use "counterfactual" once in the text, for the cited work of Madaan & Yazdanbakhsh. Of the 15 definitions that borrow from Lanham et al., 6 definitions from 5 papers ([Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning), [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace), [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting)) call its tests counterfactual in the quoted passage, and [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) name truncation "prefix counterfactuals". [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) use the word once, for Paul et al., yet [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) write of the same hint-flip filter "we regard this as a counterfactual". [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) never use the word, and [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) list them among works that "apply counterfactual interventions to input features".
5. **The label obtained by removing the cue becomes stricter in one line of papers and looser in another.** [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) "primarily use greedy decoding" (always choosing the most likely token) for the original and counterfactual inputs. [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) take their cue types but label with 10 samples per condition, counting a case positive only if "the cue raises the rate of the cue-consistent answer by at least 50pp". In the other direction, [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) hold that "we cannot label individual samples as having been causally influenced" unless the base rate (the rate at which the model gives the hinted answer without the hint causing it) is zero, while [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), who cite Guan et al., use "the counterfactual prediction without the hint" to get "a per-example target label".
6. **Lanham et al.'s tests get new settings and new variants.** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) truncate the CoT "after each step (here, each sentence)"; [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) truncate "at 25/50/75% token levels". [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp) have a generator write a chain with "exactly ONE decisive error". [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) decode greedily to find a commitment point. [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) list five tests under Lanham et al., two of which (Option Shuffling and Removing Steps) Lanham et al. do not run (see the next part).

### Checking borrowed definitions against their sources

Of the 132 borrowed definitions, 76 cite at least one source in the map. They give 96 cases in which a paper cites a source in the map for one of its definitions (called citations below), from 43 papers to 22 sources, and each citation was checked against the source's full text. The check covers every such citation of a counterfactual definition, so the counts below are rates for all of them, not for a sample.

Each check gave one of seven verdicts: *supported* (the source gives this definition), *adapted* (the source gives a definition that the citing paper modifies, for example by narrowing or extending it), *renamed* (the source defines the same thing under another name), *partial* (the source supports only part of it), *unsupported* (the source does not give it), *misdirected* (the definition comes from a different paper than the one cited) and *other-claim* (the citation supports a claim other than the definition, such as a finding stated next to it). A citation holds when it is supported or adapted.

| Verdict | Citations | Share |
|---|---|---|
| supported | 49 | 51% |
| adapted | 32 | 33% |
| partial | 7 | 7% |
| renamed | 5 | 5% |
| unsupported | 2 | 2% |
| misdirected | 1 | 1% |
| other-claim | 0 | 0% |
| **total** | **96** | |

The verdicts support six conclusions.

1. **84% of the checked citations hold.** 81 of the 96 citations are supported or adapted. The 15 others fall on 14 definitions from 11 papers; [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) account for 4 of them and [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) for 2. On 7 citations, the origin recorded for the definition and the verdict disagree: 4 citations of definitions recorded as adopted came back adapted, and 3 citations of definitions recorded as adapted came back supported.
2. **[Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) is the source with the most failed checks.** Of its 15 citations, 6 are not supported (2 unsupported, 3 partial, 1 renamed), against 1 of 17 for [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) and 1 of 12 for [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language); all 5 citations of [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) are supported. The main-text baseline list in [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) cites only [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) for five methods, including Option Shuffling and Removing Steps; Appendix C adds collective references to other papers. Lanham's four tests are Early Answering, Adding Mistakes, Paraphrasing and Filler Tokens, without separate option-shuffling or selected-step-deletion tests. Shen's paraphrasing permits "preserving or slightly altering their semantics", whereas Lanham's prompt requires "conveying exactly the same information". [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) list Lanham et al. among works that "apply counterfactual interventions to input features", but Lanham et al. write "We intervene on the CoT produced by the model rather than few shot prompt examples". [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) describe the Adding Mistakes test as a check on "mathematical counterfactuals", although Lanham et al. run it on all eight of their tasks. [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift) cite Lanham et al. with Atanasova et al. for modifying "key parts w of the inputs I"; the check records this citation as adapted, since the input edit comes from Atanasova et al.
3. **[Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) is cited for a notion it does not define.** Both papers that cite it for counterfactual simulatability, [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) (misdirected) and [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (partial), fail the check. Its simulator predicts the output on the explained input, with correctness $\mathbf{1}[\hat{y}_i\mid x_i,\hat{e}_i]$, and the word "counterfactual" does not occur in its text. [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) place it among works in which "the simulation input is the explained input" and cite Hase & Bansal 2020, which is outside the map, for the concept's "long history". The two other uses of Hase et al. 2020 by [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm), for "simulatability gain" (§2.2) and for a baseline "without explanation access" (§6), do match the source.
4. **Three of the five renamed citations attach the word "counterfactual" to an evaluation procedure whose source does not use the word for it.** They are [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) for the hint filter of [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) for the Adding Mistakes test of [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), and [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) for the input perturbations of [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring). The other two renamed citations concern a metric and a training method: [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) call the simulation precision of [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) “explanation consistency”, and [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) apply the training procedure of [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) to pressure contexts under the name “counterfactual distillation”.
5. **Three partial verdicts concern a detail of the source.** [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) place the suggested-answer cue of [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) on "an MMLU question", but Turpin et al. use BIG-Bench Hard and BBQ. [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) credit [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) with "$+35$ pp monitoring accuracy using question-inversion counterfactuals", but Hase & Potts report the 35-point gain in monitor accuracy for cue-based counterfactuals, and a 2-point gain in simulatability for their model-generated counterfactuals, which include inverted questions. [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) group [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) with methods that judge "a self-NLE to justify its new prediction" (a self-NLE is the model's own natural-language explanation), while Matton et al. "consider only the explanations for original questions".
6. **The biconditional of [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) is an adaptation, checked against one of three sources.** The check records it as adapted from [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl), who write that "a faithful explanation is not necessarily simulatable". The two other sources Turpin et al. cite for it, Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 and Hase & Bansal 2020, are outside the map and were not checked.

One misattribution falls outside the checks because the collected definition quotes a different passage. In §1, [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) cite [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) (their [40], with three other works) for "counterfactual interventions , where parts of the CoT are perturbed to examine their effect on the final prediction"; Turpin et al. perturb only the input, comparing predictions on "unmodified inputs" with predictions on "inputs that have a biasing feature applied". The 66 references outside the map, cited by 74 definitions, have no stored full text and were not checked. The most cited are Atanasova et al. 2023 (18 definitions), Siegel et al. 2024 (9) and 2025 (4), works by Pearl (9), Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 (7), Hase & Bansal 2020 (5) and Wachter et al. 2017 (4), and no other outside reference is cited by more than 2 definitions.

<details><summary>All 15 citations that fail the check</summary>

The number after each citing paper identifies the definition among all the definitions collected from that paper, under the three terms.
