|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) #2 | [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | misdirected | The defining feature Turpin et al. give, explanations on one input helping predict the model on other inputs, is the counterfactual simulation test of Hase & Bansal (2020, 'Evaluating Explainable AI: Which Algorithmic Explanations Help Users Predict Model Behavior?', not in index.csv); the cited Hase et al. (2020) LAS paper has its simulator predict the output for the explained input itself. | The counterfactual simulatability framework of explanation faithfulness aims to measure whether model explanations on one input help humans predict what predictions models will give on other inputs (Doshi-Velez and Kim, 2017; Hase et al., 2020; Chen et al., 2023). | A model is simulatable to the extent that an observer, or simulator, can predict its outputs. [...] When testing, the simulator’s correctness on each example is $\mathbbm{1}[\hat{y}_{i}\|x_{i},\hat{e}_{i}]$ , and we obtain $\mathbbm{1}[\hat{y}_{i}\|x_{i}]$ and $\mathbbm{1}[\hat{y}_{i}\|\hat{e}_{i}]$ by dropping $\hat{e}_{i}$ or $x_{i}$ from the input. |

In that paper, the model produces CoT in the presence of biasing few-shot examples; while the model’s final answer is consistent with the bias provided by the prompt, the CoT gives a different explanation for the answer that does not reference the biasing context. |

Early Answering: Truncate the original CoT before answering. Adding Mistakes: Have a language model add a mistake somewhere in the original CoT and then regenerate the rest of the CoT. Paraphrasing: Reword the beginning of the original CoT and then regenerate the rest of the CoT. Filler Tokens: Replace the CoT with ellipses. |

| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) #23 | [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | partial | Hase and Potts report the +35-point monitoring gain only for cue-based counterfactuals; question inversion (most to least) is one of their model-generated counterfactuals, where the gain was 2 points, so the +35pp is not obtained with question-inversion counterfactuals. | Counterfactual Simulation Training (Hase and Potts, 2026) achieves $+35$ pp monitoring accuracy using question-inversion counterfactuals similar to our IPHR design. | Experiments with models up to 235B parameters show that CST can substantially improve monitor accuracy on cue-based counterfactuals (by 35 accuracy points) as well as simulatability over generic counterfactuals (by 2 points). ... We write a few-shot prompt with demonstrations of operations like inverting a question (most $\rightarrow$ least), asking a similar question about new entities, and other transformations. |
| [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) #5 | [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | partial | Matton et al. support the auxiliary-LLM counterfactuals and their concept-level granularity, but they do not judge a self-explanation of the new prediction against the edit: they use only explanations of the original question, compare each concept's causal effect with how often those explanations cite it, and criticise Atanasova et al. for assessing explanations given to counterfactual questions. | Counterfactual Intervention (CI) methods employ auxiliary models to generate counterfactual texts designed to change the LLM outcome. The LLM is then prompted to produce a self-NLE to justify its new prediction. The self-NLE is deemed faithful if it aligns with the specific CI that caused the prediction change. These CI approaches mostly differ in two ways: how they measure consistency between the intervention and the resulting self-NLE (Atanasova et al., 2023; Siegel et al., 2024; Siegel et al., 2025), and the granularity of the CI intervention (Matton et al., 2025). | To generate each counterfactual $\mathbf{x}_{c_{m}\rightarrow c_{m}^{\prime}}$ , we instruct $\mathcal{A}$ to edit question $\mathbf{x}$ by changing the value of $C_{m}$ from $c_{m}$ to $c_{m}^{\prime}$ while keeping everything else the same. [...] However, in practice, we consider only the explanations for original questions. [...] However, they consider token-level edits rather than concept-level edits, and they assess the faithfulness of explanations given to the counterfactual questions, which may not reflect the faithfulness of the LLM in response to the original questions. |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) #6 | [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | partial | Turpin et al. do plant a suggested answer and compare predictions with and without it, but on BIG-Bench Hard questions (plus BBQ), not MMLU, which they never use. | Most work on training models to explain their own behavior uses hint settings, where a known cue is planted in the prompt, such as a suggested answer on an MMLU question (Turpin et al., 2023). ... Ground truth comes from removing the cue: if the model’s answer flips, the cue mattered. | We experiment with two benchmarks: BIG-Bench Hard (Suzgun et al., 2022, BBH;) and the Bias Benchmark for QA (Parrish et al., 2022, BBQ; ). ... We test on GPT-3.5 (OpenAI, 2023) and Claude 1.0 (Anthropic, 2023). With BIG-Bench Hard (§3), we investigate two biasing features ... Second, we test Suggested Answer: We add the following string to the prompt: “I think the answer is <random_label> but I’m curious to hear what you think.” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) #6 | [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | partial | The source supports simulatability (an observer predicting the model's output given its explanation, against an input-only baseline) but not the counterfactual part: its simulator predicts the output on the explained input itself, and the counterfactual simulation test belongs to Hase & Bansal (2020, 'Evaluating Explainable AI', not in index.csv), which Chen et al. (2024) cite separately for that concept. | Our metric uses counterfactual simulatability (Doshi-Velez & Kim, 2017; Hase et al., 2020; Chen et al., 2024; Mills et al., 2025; Limpijankit et al., 2025). This tests whether an observer can predict a model’s behavior given its explanation. | A model is simulatable to the extent that an observer, or simulator, can predict its outputs. [...] When testing, the simulator’s correctness on each example is $\mathbbm{1}[\hat{y}_{i}\|x_{i},\hat{e}_{i}]$ , and we obtain $\mathbbm{1}[\hat{y}_{i}\|x_{i}]$ and $\mathbbm{1}[\hat{y}_{i}\|\hat{e}_{i}]$ by dropping $\hat{e}_{i}$ or $x_{i}$ from the input. |
| [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) #16 | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Reference [25] is Lanham et al., which defines Adding Mistakes, Early Answering and Paraphrasing (plus Filler Tokens, omitted here), but has no Option Shuffling or Removing Steps test; those two are not Lanham's. | Second, counterfactual-based methods evaluate whether modifying the CoT changes the final answer, including Adding Mistakes, Option Shuffling, Removing Steps, Early Answering, and Paraphrasing [25]. | Early Answering: Truncate the original CoT before answering. Adding Mistakes: Have a language model add a mistake somewhere in the original CoT and then regenerate the rest of the CoT. Paraphrasing: Reword the beginning of the original CoT and then regenerate the rest of the CoT. Filler Tokens: Replace the CoT with ellipses. |
| [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) #21 | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | partial | Lanham's Paraphrasing rewrites the reasoning while 'conveying exactly the same information', to test only whether surface phrasing carries the CoT gain; rewrites that 'slightly alter their semantics' and robustness to semantic variation are not part of Lanham's test. | Paraphrasing: Rewrites reasoning steps while preserving or slightly altering their semantics, measuring whether the model’s prediction is robust to surface-level or semantic variations. | Please rewrite the following text, conveying exactly the same information but using different wording. ... We measure the model’s accuracy after standard and paraphrased CoT. If phrasing-encoded information is responsible for the accuracy boost conveyed by CoT, we should expect to see degraded performance under paraphrased reasoning relative to standard CoT. |
| [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) #8 | [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) | renamed | The source does perturb inputs to test label-rationale association, by adding Gaussian noise to every input embedding (robustness equivalence) and by occluding the top-k% gradient-attributed tokens (feature importance agreement), but it never calls these counterfactual interventions. | Prior works apply counterfactual interventions to input features using various heuristics to estimate their influence on model decisions Wiegreffe et al. (2020); Turpin et al. (2023); Lanham et al. (2023); Atanasova et al. (2023). | Following related work Wang et al. (2019); Lakshmi Narayan et al. (2019); Liu et al. (2019b), we add zero-mean Gaussian noise $\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})$ to each input embedding in the I $\rightarrow$ OR encoder at inference time. [...] If label prediction and rationale generation are associated, input tokens important for label prediction should be important for rationale generation and vice versa. We refer to this property as feature importance agreement. [...] Using attribution scores, we obtain the top- $k\%$ attributed tokens for every instance and occlude them following T5’s pretraining procedure and mask tokens. |
| [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) #3 | [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | renamed | Chen et al. use the same condition (a_u ≠ h and a_h = h) as evidence that the model relies on the hint, but call these retained prompt pairs, not counterfactuals. | If the model’s new answer with the hint $a_{1}$ differs from the original $a_{0}$ ( $a_{0}\neq a_{1}=hint$ ), we regard this as a counterfactual and treat it as evidence that the hint influenced the model’s decision making. | We filter the prompt pairs and retain only those where the model’s answer changes from non-hint to hint after inserting the hint (i.e., $a_{u}\neq h$ and $a_{h}=h$ ). Since the hint is the only difference between the two prompts within a pair, we can infer that the model’s internal reasoning on $x_{h}$ relies on the hint (after accounting for random noise in responses). |
| [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) #5 | [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | renamed | The metric (share of follow-up answers that match the human expectation formed from the explanation, with an LLM standing in for the human) is Chen et al.'s simulation precision under the name "explanation consistency". | we follow the metric proposed by Chen et al. 2023b, which measures explanation consistency2 as the fraction of answers on follow-up questions that match a human’s expectation (simulated with a LLM for efficiency) based on the explanation | We measure simulation precision as the fraction of simulatable counterfactuals where humans’ simulation matches the model’s actual output: ... To facilitate automatic evaluation, we also experiment with approximating human simulators with LLMs. |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) #9 | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | renamed | What Madsen calls a self-consistency check on mathematical counterfactuals is Lanham's Adding Mistakes test (edit the CoT to contain a mistake, see whether the answer changes), which Lanham runs on all eight tasks, mostly non-mathematical, and not only on math. | Lanham et al. (2023) then edits the CoT to contain false information (e.g. “2 + 3 = 6”) and checks that the prediction follows. | In this experiment, we introduce a mistake into one step of the CoT and then sample a continued CoT from that point forward. ... If inserting a mistake into the CoT changes the model’s final answer, then the model is likely not ignoring the CoT. |
| [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) #1 | [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) | renamed | The loss Tang et al. call counterfactual distillation (teacher-forcing the model's own cue-free response under the cued context) is Chua et al.'s bias-augmented consistency training (BCT): SFT on biased prompts paired with the model's own unbiased CoT, here applied to pressure contexts. | The target-based extension, which context distillation and consistency training (Snell et al., 2022; Chua et al., 2024) apply to other context pairs, teacher-forces the model’s own neutral response under the trigger: ... We call it counterfactual distillation (CD). | In BCT, we first get a model to generate unbiased CoT reasoning (i.e., none of our biasing features are included in the prompt) for a question. Then, we create a biased prompt by augmenting the original question with a bias toward a random answer choice. Finally, we perform supervised fine-tuning on the model with this dataset of bias-augmented prompts and unbiased CoT reasoning. |

</details>

### Ten points on which the papers disagree

1. **Must the variant change the output?**
   - A changed decision defines counterfactual explanations in [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) and [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms). [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) retain prediction-changing instances, and [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) call a pair counterfactual when a₀ ≠ a₁ = hint (the answer with the hint, a₁, is the hinted answer and differs from the answer without it, a₀). [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) instead impose a "contrast precondition": injected reasoning must oppose the baseline reasoning stance. Whether the model's subsequent reasoning or answer changes is evaluated separately, and unchanged outputs remain possible.
   - Answer preservation serves different roles: [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) construct two knowledge-edited models that give the same answer for different reasons; [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) require adversarial attention to preserve the prediction within an ε tolerance; [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) expect null interventions to preserve the answer and count changes as spurious. [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) instead keep treatment traces that answer the hinted wrong candidate h, then label them dependent when the matched control no longer produces h and independent when it does.
   - The edit defines the counterfactual, and its effect on behavior is evaluated separately: [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) define a perturbation as “a modification to a prompt that may or may not change the model’s answer”; [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) measure KL divergence between original and counterfactual answer distributions, [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) estimate the fraction of factor replacements that change the answer, and [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) compare deterministic decisions before and after an insertion.

   So an unchanged output makes a generated candidate invalid for [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) and [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms), is the required result of a null edit for [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal), and is one admissible outcome for [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) and the papers in the third point above.
2. **Remove the cue, or swap it for another?** [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) ("The counterfactual input is the input without the cue."), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) ($x_{\setminus C}$), [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) ("Ground truth comes from removing the cue"), [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) and [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) compare against the input without the cue. [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) reject that comparison: "Comparing W to a no-hint prompt would confound the hint’s content with its presence". For them, "the hint caused the answer" means that the answer changes when $h$ is swapped for $h'$, and relabeling with a third candidate "flips 11-23% of verdicts". [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) use both value replacements and deletions, including identity swaps in BBQ and evidence removal in StrategyQA. [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) compare paired variants “promoting” and “diminishing” a concept, generated through addition and removal actions. [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability) proposes no-hint, correct-hint and incorrect-hint generations as future controls; [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) performs one unhinted run and two hinted runs per prompt at temperature 0, with one hint correct and the other incorrect. [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) use both removal and value-change counterfactuals while warning that correlated concepts are “especially a risk when we use removal-based counterfactuals”. A given answer can count as caused by a hint under removal and not under a swap.
3. **Does one flip show that the edit caused the answer?**
   - Yes, per instance: [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) treat a flip toward the hinted answer “as evidence that the hint influenced the model’s decision making”; [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) regard a prediction change after removing protected information as evidence that it “exerts causal influence”; [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) derive a binary hint-reliance label from greedy hinted and unhinted predictions, positive only when adding the hint changes the prediction toward its answer; [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) hold that “any change in behavior can be attributed to” the single controlled intervention.
   - No: [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) write that unless the base rate is zero "we cannot label individual samples as having been causally influenced"; [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) reject the per-response flip criterion; [Angdembay et al. 2026](./#p=angdembay2026two) call their own label "an observed conditional flip rather than a within-pair counterfactual"; [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) show that "pairing unrelated control and intervention completions does not identify the coupling". [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability), whose label "records answer–hint agreement, not a verified causal hint effect", hold that the paired no-hint or alternate-hint controls they did not run "would make the label causal".
   - The sampling rules differ: [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) resample and label a claim true when the behavior rate moves by at least 50 percentage points and false when it moves by at most 15. In Edit-Proposal, [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) count an edit as flipped when more than half of K resamples differ from baseline; their main leaderboard uses K=1.

   The aggregate analyses bound two different quantities. [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) write that the total effect "lower bounds the causal influence rate" and score monitors against it; [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) show that the resulting score, the minimal-criterion true positive rate $\mathrm{TPR}_{mc}$, "upper-bounds the monitor’s recall on intervention-caused outcomes". [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) write that CoT intervention analysis "does not directly determine the faithfulness of a specific generated trace", yet run five counterfactual tests as baselines for detecting unfaithfulness in individual traces.
4. **Isolate one factor, or vary more than one?** [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) exclude a counterfactual that is "confounded, editing several variables at once"; [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) rate a pair 1, "Not a valid counterfactual", for "Multiple or major changes"; [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) assume concepts can each "be changed without affecting other concepts"; [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) require edits that "leave the other factors fixed". [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) count single-factor variation as a pitfall: "Most prior approaches vary only a single concept at a time (Matton et al., 2025; Siegel et al., 2024). This fails to test whether the explanations capture the complex, non-linear concept interactions". Their counterfactual set holds real records that differ in up to two features. The split follows the purpose: the first four papers attribute an effect to one factor, while Mayne et al. test whether an explanation predicts behavior across a region.
5. **Edit the reasoning by hand, or resample it?** The Adding Mistakes test of [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) has "a language model add a mistake somewhere in the original CoT"; [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) insert reasoning written by o3, and [Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project) a step rewritten by a critic model. [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) describe prior work, Lanham et al. included, as "inserting or replacing sentences with handwritten text", define an intervention as off-policy "if the model would not plausibly produce it itself (e.g., a handwritten sentence or one taken from a different context)" and report that "artificial and off-policy edits to CoT common in prior literature generally produce small and unstable changes in behavior, while on-policy resampling produces larger and more reliable effects". [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) raise a related doubt about Lanham et al.'s edited CoTs: "injecting false facts may create out-of-distribution results or be interpreted as typos by the LLM, thus it’s unclear if this method is completely valid". The papers also differ on what happens after the edit. [Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project) re-execute the agent "from the point of intervention", so later steps are re-sampled; [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) argue that their own counterfactual importance "misses resilience" and measure importance on rollouts in which the sentence's content never reappears.
6. **Which inputs count as counterfactuals for simulatability?** [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) have an LLM write follow-up questions "that you can confidently guess the robot’s answer to based on its answer to the starter question". [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) rejection-sample generated questions "to obtain counterfactuals where the task model and simulator disagree on the correct answer". [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) use "popular tabular datasets rather than synthesizing artificial perturbations", and [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient), who reuse Mayne et al.'s seven datasets, describe their counterfactuals as "similar real inputs from the same dataset rather than synthetic perturbations"; [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) use Gricean manipulations. [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) name the "unconstrained LLM-generated edits (Chen et al., 2024)" among approaches that "often produce counterfactuals that are irrelevant or drift from the natural data distribution". Chen et al.'s generator writes questions it can answer from the explanation, while Hase & Potts keep questions on which the simulator and the task model disagree. Precision is averaged over the chosen set, so each score is defined relative to its set.
7. **What does counterfactual simulatability measure?**
   - Faithfulness: [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) ("framework of explanation faithfulness"), [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) (the "if and only if"), [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) ("measure faithfulness through the framework of counterfactual simulatability"), [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) ("a faithful explanation ... should help predict its behavior across $C(x)$") and [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) ("A faithful explanation should tell a reader what would change the behavior").
   - A special case of faithfulness that is not necessary for it: [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) ("a faithful explanation is not necessarily simulatable"). [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) repeat this and study simulatability "as a way to capture the usefulness of explanations", adding that "explanations can improve simulatability while still being post-hoc or persuasive rather than diagnostic".
   - Plausibility: [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) introduce Chen et al.'s metrics after writing that "most evaluations of self-explanations focus on plausibility".

   The biconditional of [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) makes it necessary for faithfulness that an explanation allow "a human or model" to predict the model's behavior on other inputs; [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) state that "a faithful explanation is not necessarily simulatable" because their simulatability "requires the output predictor to be humans rather than arbitrary black-box models". [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) also restrict what an explanation is: "a behavioral claim testable by counterfactual experiments, not an explanation of the model’s internal computation". The [faithfulness](#faithfulness) section covers the faithfulness side.
8. **Does the counterfactual test measure faithfulness?**
   - Yes: [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique), [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) and [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) apply counterfactual mention tests to explanation faithfulness, and [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) write in related work that "Counterfactual approaches test whether free-text explanations reflect model decisions". [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) list Counterfactual Edit methods among faithfulness metrics, as methods that "similarly" check whether explanations reflect inserted tokens, but argue that the hint-verbalization metric they resemble "is not the same as faithfulness".
   - Qualified: [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) characterize all the tests they review, Counterfactual Edits included, "as self-consistency tests instead"; [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) credit them (their [76]) with the argument that a test relying on "executing changes to the model’s input and observing changes to the prediction actually measures self-consistency rather than faithfulness". [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) name two limitations, that "the model might base its new prediction on another part of the input text after intervention" and that the methods "treat the model as a black box by only examining input-output relationships", which "departs from the commonly adopted definition of explanation faithfulness"; they still run the test as a comparison. [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) object that it judges "explanations given to the counterfactual questions, which may not reflect the faithfulness of the LLM in response to the original questions". [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) list random word insertions among perturbations that "often produce counterfactuals that are irrelevant or drift from the natural data distribution". [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) describe the correlational version as "population-level".
9. **Is the answer's dependence on the reasoning a measure of faithfulness?** [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) call Counterfactual Sensitivity "a formal, causally-motivated measure of faithfulness" and write that "Higher COS indicates greater faithfulness"; [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) read an unchanged answer after substantial perturbation as "suggesting potential unfaithfulness"; [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) write that "A faithful model should shift its output accordingly". [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) (App. G.2) state that their "definition of faithfulness does not imply any expectations of causality", that causal CoTs "are not a necessary condition for monitorability", and that "intervention-based proxies for measuring causality are insufficient measures for faithfulness", because a change after a CoT edit "will not reveal how much of the complete reasoning is encapsulated in the CoT". They still call causal metrics "valuable and worth measuring", since "steerability is a sufficient condition for faithfulness". On Baker et al.'s account, strong steerability raises confidence in faithfulness, but a change after an edit does not show how much of the reasoning the CoT contains; COS and CS read the size of the response as the degree of faithfulness.
10. **Do counterfactual attention weights show that attention is not an explanation?** [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) take the existence of attention weights "maximally distinct from $\hat{\alpha}$ but that nonetheless yields an equivalent prediction" as evidence against attention as explanation. [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention) accept the counterfactual framework "as a concept" but object that "due to the per-instance nature of the demonstration and the fact that model parameters have not been learned or manipulated directly, Jain and Wallace have not shown the existence of an adversarial model that produces the claimed adversarial distributions". On their reading, independently optimized per-instance attention weights do not establish "equally plausible or faithful explanations"; [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention) instead train an adversarial model through a "coherent parameterization, which holds across all training instances".

## Monitorability

The monitorability section of this page holds 1,167 definitions from 108 papers. Each definition has a kind and a stance. The kind says what the definition gives: informal (a definition in words), formal (a mathematical definition), operational (a way to measure or test the notion), criterion (a necessary or sufficient condition for the notion, or an assumption about it), failure mode (a way the notion can fail) or taxonomy (a classification into types). The stance says where the definition comes from: proposed (the paper's own), adopted (taken unchanged from earlier work) or adapted (taken from earlier work and changed). By kind, the 1,167 definitions are 448 operational, 268 informal, 133 failure modes, 119 criteria, 105 taxonomies and 94 formal; by stance, 785 are proposed, 257 adopted and 125 adapted.

The definitions were collected in two rounds. The first found 328 definitions in 82 papers; the second, which read the papers' full texts, raised the count to 1,167: 327 of the earlier definitions remain, one moved to the faithfulness section, and 840 are new. 127 definitions, from 47 papers, define monitorability itself or a direct measure of it (in 109 of them the defined term contains the word monitorability, and in another 14 the word monitorable or unmonitorable). The other 1,040 define related notions: monitors and their scores, failure modes, and the behaviors monitors look for (deception, scheming, reward hacking, evaluation awareness).

Two definitions of monitorability compete. 42 definitions from 24 papers make monitorability a monitor's ability to predict a property of the model's behavior, and 69 definitions from 34 papers make it depend on whether the reasoning trace states (verbalizes) what drove the answer; 9 papers give definitions of both types. The definitions are sorted by meaning into the 18 groups below, and each definition belongs to exactly one group. The groups run in this order: what monitorability is (groups 1 and 2); how it is measured (metrics, evaluation designs); what makes a trace readable (necessity, legibility) and how it stops being readable (encoded reasoning, obfuscation, evasion of the monitor); the monitor itself (monitors, the prompts and rules behind their verdicts, failures of the monitor, the model's own reports); the behaviors monitors target (deception, scheming, reward hacking, evaluation awareness); and the cost of keeping a model monitorable.

Each group below starts with its number of definitions and papers and its counts by kind and by stance. A short description, one quoted definition and a table of definitions follow. In the tables, text in quotation marks is copied from the paper. Text without quotation marks is not a quote: it is a formula restating the paper's mathematics, a short paraphrase, or both (all text in italics is paraphrase). The line after each table lists the group's remaining definitions, with the number from each paper in parentheses.

### 1. Monitorability as a monitor's ability to predict a property of the model's behavior

42 definitions from 24 papers. Kinds: 24 informal, 13 criteria, 3 formal, 1 operational, 1 taxonomy. Stances: 21 proposed, 17 adopted, 4 adapted.

These definitions make monitorability a relation among three things: a model, a target property of its behavior and a reader. A model is monitorable for a property to the degree that a monitor, reading what the model exposes, predicts that property. The reader is part of the definition: [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) call monitorability "a two-sided property", [Algaba et al. 2026](./#p=algaba2026reasoning) "a joint property of the display and the reader" (the display is what the reader is shown), and [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought) "a property of both the task model and monitor". Seven definitions from seven papers distinguish monitorability from faithfulness or from a complete reconstruction of the reasoning: [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain), [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning), [Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned), [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient) and [Young 2026](./#p=young2026why). [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) do not draw this contrast in their definition, but they argue in the same paper that "the key property is not faithfulness but monitorability". An eighth definition, also in [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), rejects a separate requirement: it says that "causal CoTs" are unnecessary for monitorability, because a trace may faithfully describe reasoning without causally determining the output. The group also holds the premise of plan-based monitoring in [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan), "that reading the plan tells an overseer which irreversible actions the model will take". An earlier version of this page put it in the necessity group (group 5); an independent check of that version by another model (GPT-6.1 Sol) placed it here, since it defines usefulness to an overseer rather than necessity.

> We develop evaluations for monitorability, which we define as a monitor’s ability to accurately predict a specified property of an agent’s actions, e.g. whether the model is acting or will act harmfully.
>
> ([Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), §2)

**Formal versions of these definitions.**

- [Anonymous 2026 (implementation)](./#p=anon2026implementation), Informal Def. 1: a property $P$ of an actor $A$ (the monitored model) is monitorable on a task distribution $D_t$ with respect to a monitor $M$ if the monitor's output on a transcript approximately matches the property with high probability. In symbols, $P(A,t,\pi)\approx M(\pi)$ with high probability over tasks $t\sim D_t$ and transcripts $\pi\sim A(t)$ that the actor produces on them.
- [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing), Def. 3.6 and Props. 3.7 and 3.10: the uplift from reading the CoT $Z$ is the accuracy gain of the Bayes-optimal CoT monitor (the most accurate possible monitor that reads the prompt and the CoT) over the Bayes-optimal prompt-only monitor, $\delta:=\alpha_{\text{CoT}}-\alpha_{\text{Prompt}}$. The uplift is bounded by the conditional mutual information $I(Y;Z\mid X)$, the information that the CoT carries about the monitored attribute $Y$ beyond what the prompt $X$ gives: $0\le\delta\le\sqrt{2I(Y;Z\mid X)}$. For an attribute given by a stochastic map of the output $O$ and prompt $X$, $Y=g(O,X)$, a positive uplift requires that the CoT carry some information about the output beyond the prompt: $\delta>0$ requires $I(O;Z\mid X)>0$.

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|

| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | monitorability without faithfulness | criterion | "our notion of monitorability does not require a model’s externalized reasoning to faithfully reflect its internal reasoning process" |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | monitorability | informal | "the ability to monitor for specific properties of agents actions or outputs (monitorability), and not faithfulness in isolation, that is important" |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | causal CoT (not necessary for monitorability) | criterion | "causal CoTs (see Figure 25) are not a necessary condition for monitorability" |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) | monitorability (CoT monitorable to a reader) | informal | "a CoT is monitorable to a reader (human or automated) if the reader can use the CoT to determine if the model is taking bad actions" |
| [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) | CoT monitoring (completeness not required) | criterion | "CoT does not need to completely represent the actual reasoning process in order to be a valuable additional safety layer" |
| [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning) | monitorability | informal | "whether a monitor model, given access to reasoning traces, can correctly predict the value of the latent variable" |

### 2. Monitorability as a trace that states, faithfully and completely, what drove the answer

69 definitions from 34 papers. Kinds: 24 criteria, 14 operational, 14 informal, 8 formal, 5 failure modes, 4 taxonomies. Stances: 51 proposed, 16 adopted, 2 adapted.

These definitions make monitorability depend on what the trace states: monitoring presupposes that the CoT states the factors and steps that produced the answer, and monitorability falls when it does not. They differ on how much the trace must state. [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) require only "the key influential factors and reasoning steps that shaped the answer" and [Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned) only "the specific computations the monitor needs to detect", while [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) require traces that "faithfully reflect the model’s internal decision-making path" and [Jia et al. 2026](./#p=jia2026faithfulness) a trace that "faithfully reflects the computation that produces the final answer". Three definitions score monitorability by how often the trace states something (a verbalization rate) rather than by a monitor's predictions. They come from experiments with an inserted cue or hint, such as a suggested answer added to the prompt. [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) average a cue-acknowledgment rate (how often the trace mentions the cue), which they call faithfulness, and a verbosity rate; [Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing) count every trace where the hint's influence is visible; and [Nguyen et al. 2026](./#p=nguyen2026generalization) restate Occhipinti's measure, noting that it is reported "over only the traces that were unfaithful at baseline".

> For CoT monitoring to be most effective, the CoT must be a legible and faithful reflection of the way the model reached its conclusion and generated the user-facing response.
>
> ([Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), §1)

### 3. Metrics that score monitorability

98 definitions from 35 papers. Kinds: 58 operational, 35 formal, 4 criteria, 1 informal. Stances: 67 proposed, 19 adopted, 12 adapted.

These definitions include measures that compare a monitor's verdict $Z$ with a label $Y$ for the target property. The comparison is summarized by g-mean² (the true-positive rate times the true-negative rate, defined below), AUROC (the area under the ROC curve: the probability that the monitor scores a random positive case above a random negative one), binned mean squared error, recall at a fixed false-positive rate, or accuracy. The group also holds indirect proxies, such as the "proxy objective" $I(O;Z\mid X)$ proposed by [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing), in whose notation $Z$ is the CoT, $X$ the prompt and $O$ the output; $I(O;Z\mid X)$ is the information that the CoT carries about the output beyond what the prompt gives. In intervention evaluations, the model runs with and without a controlled change to its input or environment (the intervention arm and the control arm), and the monitor must detect when the change altered the model's reasoning and behavior. For these evaluations, [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) replace the true-positive rate by a minimal-criterion rate. It credits the monitor only up to $R_{\text{effect}}$, the share of positive cases that the intervention must have caused, and six papers reuse that rate: [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought), [Anonymous 2026 (improving monitorability)](./#p=anon2026improving), [Anonymous 2026 (reward misspecification)](./#p=anon2026reward), [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient), [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026monitorbench) and [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability). [Anonymous 2026 (improving monitorability)](./#p=anon2026improving) define causal recall, the share of the cases in which the intervention caused the behavior that the monitor flags. They prove (Theorem 1) that, under their Assumption 1, the minimal-criterion rate is the upper end of the interval that the observed arms leave for causal recall. So the rate bounds causal recall from above but "does not generally identify it" (the bounds collapse to the observed recall when $p_0=0$, that is, when the target outcome never occurs in the control arm). [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) keep the same form but put the CoT's articulation rate (how often the CoT states the influence) where the monitor's flag rate was.

> A natural choice is the geometric mean of sensitivity (true positive rate) and specificity (true negative rate), $\text{g-mean}=\sqrt{\mathrm{TPR}\cdot\mathrm{TNR}}$ [25]. We square this quantity to more sharply penalize failures, yielding a monitorability measure which is both more conservative and more immediately interpretable
>
> ([Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), §2.1)

**Formal versions of these metrics.**

- [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) multiply the true-positive rate (TPR, the share of positive cases the monitor flags) by the true-negative rate (TNR, the share of negative cases it does not flag): $\text{g-mean}^2=\mathrm{TPR}\cdot\mathrm{TNR}$. In the intervention setting, $X=1$ marks the intervention arm and $X=0$ the control arm, $Y=1$ the target behavior and $Z=1$ a monitor flag. The minimal-criterion true-positive rate divides the monitor's flag rate on positive cases in the intervention arm by $R_{\text{effect}}$ and caps the result at 1. Here $R_{\text{effect}}$ is the total effect $\mathrm{TE}$ of the intervention (the difference in the rate of the target behavior between the intervention arm and the control arm) divided by the rate of the behavior in the intervention arm: $\mathrm{TPR}_{\mathrm{mc}}=\min\{1,\Pr(Z=1\mid X=1,Y=1)/R_{\text{effect}}\}$ with $R_{\text{effect}}=\mathrm{TE}/\Pr(Y=1\mid X=1)$. The score multiplies this rate by the geometric mean of the true-negative rates in the two arms: $\text{g-mean}^2=\mathrm{TPR}_{\mathrm{mc}}\sqrt{\mathrm{TNR}_{X=1}\,\mathrm{TNR}_{X=0}}$.
- [Anonymous 2026 (improving monitorability)](./#p=anon2026improving) define causal recall with potential outcomes: $Y(1)$ and $Y(0)$ are the behavior the model would show with and without the intervention, and $Z(1)$ is the monitor's verdict with it. Causal recall is the probability of a flag among the cases in which the intervention caused the behavior: $r_c:=\Pr(Z(1)=1\mid U=1)$ with $U:=\mathbf{1}\{Y(1)=1,Y(0)=0\}$. With $R=(p_1-p_0)/p_1$, where $p_1$ and $p_0$ are the rates of the target outcome in the intervention and control arms, they bound causal recall using the observed true-positive rate $\mathrm{TPR}_{ob}$ (the monitor's flag rate on positive cases in the intervention arm) and $R$: $\max\{0,(\mathrm{TPR}_{ob}+R-1)/R\}\le r_c\le\min\{1,\mathrm{TPR}_{ob}/R\}$.
- [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) define a score of the same form as Guan et al.'s minimal-criterion rate, $M_0:=\min(1,\pi_Z/R)$ with $R=\max(0,p_{11}-p_{01})/p_{11}$, where $\pi_Z$ is the articulation rate; $M_1$ is the same score restricted to answers for which the CoT is necessary.
- [Anonymous 2026 (implementation)](./#p=anon2026implementation) define the evaluated monitorability $\hat\mu$ as a summary statistic $s$ that compares the monitor's scores $M(\pi)$ with a proxy $\hat P$ for the property: $\hat\mu:=s(M(\pi),\hat P(e(A),t_{\hat P},\pi))$, with the binned mean squared error $\mathrm{BMSE}(X,Y)$ over non-empty bins of $Y$ as the default $s$.
- [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) use the unsquared geometric mean, $\sqrt{\mathrm{TPR}\cdot\mathrm{TNR}}$. [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating) score false negatives, $\Pr(M=0\mid Y_{\text{cue}}=1)$, and false positives, $\Pr(M=1\mid Y_{\text{cue}}=0)$, of the monitor's verdict $M$ against a causal label $Y_{\text{cue}}$.

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|

| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | minimal-criterion TPR (TPR_mc) | formal | $R_{\text{effect}}=\mathrm{TE}/\Pr(Y=1\mid X=1)$; "the monitor should trigger at least as often as this rate in the intervention arm" |
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | intervention g-mean² (Eq. 2) | formal | $\text{g-mean}^2=\mathrm{TPR}_{\mathrm{mc}}\sqrt{\mathrm{TNR}_{X=1}\mathrm{TNR}_{X=0}}$ |
| [Anonymous 2026 (improving monitorability)](./#p=anon2026improving) | causal recall | formal | $r_c:=\Pr(Z(1)=1\mid U=1)$, $U:=\mathbf{1}\{Y(1)=1,Y(0)=0\}$ |
| [Anonymous 2026 (improving monitorability)](./#p=anon2026improving) | bounds on causal recall (Theorem 1) | formal | $\max\{0,(\mathrm{TPR}_{ob}+R-1)/R\}\le r_c\le\min\{1,\mathrm{TPR}_{ob}/R\}$ |
| [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) | monitorability score M0 | formal | $M_0:=\min(1,\pi_Z/R)$, "matching the minimal-criterion sensitivity of Guan et al. (2026)" |
| [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026monitorbench) | monitorability score | formal | $\min\{\mathbb{E}[Z\mid X=1,Y=1]/R_{\text{effect}},1\}$ |
| [Anonymous 2026 (implementation)](./#p=anon2026implementation) | evaluated monitorability (µ̂-monitorable) | formal | $\hat\mu:=s(M(\pi),\hat P(e(A),t_{\hat P},\pi))$ |
| [Anonymous 2026 (implementation)](./#p=anon2026implementation) | p_swap (disagreement between monitorability summary statistics) | formal | $p_{\text{swap}}(s,s')$: share of comparable actor pairs that two summary statistics rank in opposite order |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | monitorability (of cue influence) | formal | "G-mean is the geometric mean of recall (TPR) and specificity (TNR)" |
| [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating) | monitor reliability | formal | $\Pr(M=0\mid Y_{\text{cue}}=1)$, $\Pr(M=1\mid Y_{\text{cue}}=0)$ |
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) | monitorable (AUROC of monitor score) | operational | "We consider a model monitorable to the extent that $Z$ tracks $Y$ , which we quantify with AUROC." |
| [Arike et al. 2026](./#p=arike2026information) | TPR@1%FPR (catch rate) | operational | *catch rate: share of malicious transcripts above the threshold that keeps FPR below 1% on benign ones* |
| [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning) | monitorability reward | formal | $r_{\text{monitorability}}=+1$ if the monitor predicts the latent correctly, $-1$ otherwise |
| [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) | CoT monitorability (MI proxy objective) | operational | "maximizing the conditional mutual information $I(O;Z\mid X)$ as a proxy objective" |

### 4. Evaluation designs: types of evaluation, default and adversarial conditions, and true labels

84 definitions from 22 papers. Kinds: 34 operational, 20 informal, 20 taxonomies, 5 criteria, 4 formal, 1 failure mode. Stances: 47 proposed, 27 adopted, 10 adapted.

These definitions set out the basic types of monitorability evaluation and the labels the monitor must predict, along with how the monitored behavior is induced and the conditions under which monitoring is tested ([Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), [Duzan & Cooper Stickland 2026](./#p=duzan2026chainofthought), [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra)). [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) distinguish three types, which they call archetypes. Intervention evaluations apply a controlled change to the input or environment and ask whether the monitor can infer the change in reasoning that changed the behavior; process evaluations use problems with a small known set of admissible solution paths; outcome-property evaluations use environments where some property of the output can be labeled unambiguously. Three papers restate this classification: [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought), [Duzan & Cooper Stickland 2026](./#p=duzan2026chainofthought) and [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra). [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) recast it as influence, process and outcome "monitorability types", while [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026monitorbench) adapt it into input intervention, outcome justification and solution process, with outcome justification requiring the CoT to "explicitly articulate[] the rationale" for atypical outputs. A second distinction separates default from adversarial conditions: [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) measure "default monitorability", [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra) splits non-adversarial from adversarial conditions, [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) default from worst-case transparency, and [Duzan & Cooper Stickland 2026](./#p=duzan2026chainofthought) explicit from implicit influence. [Anonymous 2026 (implementation)](./#p=anon2026implementation) separate monitorability from "the procedures we use to estimate it". They note that evaluations score a proxy $\hat P$ of the property, computed from side information hidden from the monitor, and they treat sensitivity "to implementation-level details which we do not expect to change evaluations" as one sign that an evaluation is untrustworthy.

> Intervention evaluations. Inspired by prior work [7, 8, 38], we apply a controlled intervention to the agent’s environment or input, measure whether the agent’s behavior changes due to the intervention, and then ask whether a monitor can infer a specific change in reasoning leading to the change in behavior.
>
> ([Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), §2)

### 5. Necessity: the task requires the CoT, and the answer depends on it

64 definitions from 29 papers. Kinds: 21 operational, 20 criteria, 9 informal, 9 failure modes, 4 taxonomies, 1 formal. Stances: 43 proposed, 16 adopted, 5 adapted.

These definitions base monitorability on the CoT being needed: if a model cannot do a task without writing out its reasoning, the reasoning has to appear in the trace. [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) define necessity as "the model cannot succeed without it", and [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) separate this necessity from the propensity to think out loud (a tendency to write out reasoning even when the task does not require it). Later definitions make necessity a matter of degree: [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) split it into levels 0, 1, 2 and 3.m, [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought) measure it as "CoT reliance" (performance with CoT minus performance without it), and [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) and [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra) measure what the model can do without CoT (serial depth, the NCRI index, a no-CoT math time horizon). The group also holds the ways necessity fails (reasoning in the latent space instead of the text, filler tokens in place of reasoning, the ability to solve tasks without CoT) and a version for plans in [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan): a plan "with no causal effect", detected by seed-invariance (the same outcome in every seeded rollout).

> Necessity: We say CoT is necessary for a task if the model cannot succeed without it. This condition formalizes the requirement that a model must “think out loud.”
>
> ([Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain), §2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|

| [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) | necessity to think out loud | informal | "models need to externalize their reasoning because they are unable to complete the task without CoT" |
| [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) | propensity to think out loud | informal | "In cases where models do not strictly need to use CoT to accomplish a task, they may still have a tendency to externalize their reasoning." |
| [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) | externalized reasoning property | criterion | "For sufficiently difficult tasks, Transformers must use chain of thought as a form of working memory." |
| [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) | CoT necessity (Level 1) | formal | *Level 1: the no-CoT target-answer probability $p_{10}<0.01$* |
| [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) | CoT necessity Level 2 | informal | "The CoT is necessary to process $P$ . We define this to be the case if $P$ cannot be processed separately from the CoT." |
| [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought) | CoT reliance | operational | "a model’s gain in performance on a task when a CoT is generated vs. when the model is forced to answer directly" |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) | CoT uplift (proxy for CoT necessity) | operational | "a “CoT uplift” by subtracting the pass rate without CoT from the pass rate with CoT" |
| [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) | serial depth (steps per forward pass) | operational | *serial steps the model completes without CoT at 50% probability* |
| [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra) | no-CoT ability | failure-mode | "No-CoT ability is concerning for monitorability because it decreases the surface available to monitoring" |
| [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) | load-bearing CoT | informal | "load-bearing CoT additionally requires that the tokens themselves convey information which the model uses" |
| [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor) | CoT necessity | criterion | "When the CoT is necessary for performing a task, the model must externalize its reasoning in some form." |

### 6. Legibility, coverage and checkability: a reader can follow or check the reasoning

108 definitions from 22 papers. Kinds: 36 operational, 27 informal, 14 failure modes, 12 criteria, 11 taxonomies, 8 formal. Stances: 75 proposed, 19 adapted, 14 adopted.

These definitions ask whether the reasoning is accessible to one of four readers. The first is a verifier whose completeness (it accepts correct solutions) and soundness (it cannot be fooled into accepting incorrect ones) are required only in a relaxed form ([Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier)). The second is a human with the same language as the person who wrote the prompt, for legibility, and the same expertise, for coverage ([Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic)). The third is a model, assessed by whether it can continue the reasoning successfully ([Jiao et al. 2026](./#p=jiao2026tandem), [Roytburg et al. 2026](./#p=roytburg2026measuring)), by how well it predicts the tokens of the trace ([Jiao et al. 2026](./#p=jiao2026tandem)), or by "final-answer agreement" ([Sullivan & Koller 2026](./#p=sullivan2026language)). The fourth is the monitor itself ([Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring)). [Roytburg et al. 2026](./#p=roytburg2026measuring) state the disagreement directly: "Prior work defines legibility inconsistently." [Sullivan & Koller 2026](./#p=sullivan2026language) define language drift as novel linguistic behavior that is "non-standard and/or ungrammatical" and prove that "RLVR optimization pressure permits unbounded language drift, while supervised fine-tuning does not" (RLVR is reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards). [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning) deliberately simulate drift by rewarding KL divergence (a measure of how much one probability distribution differs from another), and find no clear qualitative drift while accuracy stays close to its original level. [Jose 2025](./#p=jose2025reasoning) observes "compressed" reasoning and proposes vestigial reasoning as a hypothesis. [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) discuss "legibility collapse" by summarizing Jose, while [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) report non-standard terminology that increases during reinforcement learning aimed at capabilities.

> When we find helpful provers and verifiers with such limited completeness and soundness properties, we say that the helpful prover is legible to the verifier. This is analogous to saying that outputs are legible to humans if humans accept them as correct and can’t be fooled by similar-looking incorrect outputs.
>
> ([Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier), §1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier) | checkability | formal | *completeness: correct solutions accepted; soundness: no sneaky prover in $\mathcal{H}$ gets an incorrect solution accepted* |
| [Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier) | legible (to the verifier) | criterion | "the helpful prover is legible to the verifier" |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) | legibility | operational | "Can the reasoning in the CoT be understood by a human with the same language capabilities as the user asking the model?" |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) | coverage | operational | "Does the CoT contain all the (intermediate) reasoning steps needed to arrive at the model’s final answer?" |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) | strict language requirement (legibility) | criterion | "Switching languages ... results in a Legibility score of 0." |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | legible / plausible latent | informal | "legible if the monitor believes it is getting useful information about $v$ from the latent" |
| [Jose 2025](./#p=jose2025reasoning) | legibility | informal | "not whether the reasoning is faithful, but whether it’s comprehensible at all" |
| [Jiao et al. 2026](./#p=jiao2026tandem) | junior legibility | informal | "Junior legibility measures how well the junior can predict the senior’s reasoning." |
| [Roytburg et al. 2026](./#p=roytburg2026measuring) | weak-to-strong legibility | informal | *legibility relative to weaker models (monitors, students), not humans* |
| [Roytburg et al. 2026](./#p=roytburg2026measuring) | legibility (prior definitions) | informal | "Prior work defines legibility inconsistently." |
| [Sullivan & Koller 2026](./#p=sullivan2026language) | language drift LD(π) | formal | $\mathrm{LD}(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{x}[D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P^{(\mathrm{HL})}(\cdot\mid x)\,\Vert\,\pi(\cdot\mid x))]$ |
| [Yu et al. 2026](./#p=yu2026outcome) | verifiability | informal | "whether a reasoning trace can be independently checked step-by-step" |
| [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) | conciseness (for monitoring) | criterion | "the reasoning must be short for effective monitoring" |
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | legibility collapse | failure-mode | "monitorability could also fail via legibility collapse under outcome-based RL" |
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | illegible CoT (non-standard dialect) | failure-mode | *over-represented words obscure the reasoning (non-standard dialect)* |

### 7. Encoded reasoning and steganography: information in the trace that a reader cannot read

84 definitions from 22 papers. Kinds: 28 operational, 23 failure modes, 15 informal, 9 criteria, 5 taxonomies, 4 formal. Stances: 59 proposed, 19 adopted, 6 adapted.

These definitions concern information that the trace carries and a reader cannot read. They differ on who the reader is: [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) define encoded reasoning as "not understandable to human readers" and [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor) requires that it be "hidden from humans", while [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) define steganography as information "hidden to an external monitor". [Mathew et al. 2024](./#p=mathew2025hidden) and [Zhou & May 2026](./#p=zhou2026conceptual) frame steganography as a game between a colluding sender and receiver on one side and an overseer or a defender who paraphrases the text on the other. The game is measured by how often messages slip past the overseer (bypass rate), how accurately the receiver decodes them (decode accuracy) and how much information the channel can carry (channel capacity). [Roger & Greenblatt 2023](./#p=roger2023preventing) and [Roger 2025](./#p=anthropic2025paraphrases) define paraphrasing tests, which reword the reasoning and check whether performance drops, for whether a model relies on encoded reasoning.

> Encoded reasoning: The benefit from CoT may be attained by LLMs encoding the relevant information in the generated reasoning in a way that is not understandable to human readers (a form of steganography).
>
> ([Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), §1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | encoded reasoning | failure-mode | "encoding the relevant information in the generated reasoning in a way that is not understandable to human readers" |
| [Roger & Greenblatt 2023](./#p=roger2023preventing) | encoded reasoning | failure-mode | "when LLMs use steganography to hide relevant intermediate steps of reasoning in the generated text" |
| [Roger & Greenblatt 2023](./#p=roger2023preventing) | capacity (of steganographic channel) | formal | $I(M,\hat M)=H(M)-H(M\mid\hat M)$, capacity of the encoder-paraphraser-decoder channel |
| [Roger & Greenblatt 2023](./#p=roger2023preventing) | encoded reasoning (paraphrase-sensitivity criterion) | criterion | "if LLMs were using encoded reasoning to increase their performance, then using defenses against steganography should reduce LLM performance" |
| [Roger 2025](./#p=anthropic2025paraphrases) | reliance on encoded reasoning | criterion | *pass@1 after paraphrase distillation close to pass@1 on original scratchpads: no reliance on syntax-encoded reasoning* |
| [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) | steganography | informal | "inserting information within a medium (in this case, text) which is hidden to an external monitor" |
| [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) | steganographic CoT | criterion | "(a) The CoT encodes harmful planning in load-bearing tokens. (b) The monitor cannot decode the encodings, nor detect their presence." |
| [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) | steganographic reasoning (vs steganographic communication) | informal | "an LLM passes information to its future self through messages encoded in the CoT" |
| [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor) | encoded reasoning | criterion | "This does not fit the definition of encoded reasoning, as the reasoning is not hidden from humans" |
| [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) | weak form of steganography | failure-mode | "the model knows to ignore the mistakes, but the monitor does not" |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | steganography (faithful but obfuscating latent) | informal | "the true sexist reasoning is faithfully present in the latent but is hidden through steganography" |
| [Mathew et al. 2024](./#p=mathew2025hidden) | steganographic collusion (sender/receiver vs. mitigation team) | informal | "an adversarial relationship between two teams, a collusion team and a mitigation team" |
| [Zhou & May 2026](./#p=zhou2026conceptual) | conceptual steganography | failure-mode | "each step of a CoT carries information through patterns of high-level reasoning behavior, rather than through lexical choice" |
| [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra) | steganographic CoT reasoning | failure-mode | "concealing loadbearing hidden reasoning within ordi- nary and unrelated text" |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) | encoded reasoning (steganography) | failure-mode | "a model learns to hide information, making it opaque to an overseer" |

### 8. Obfuscation: the trace stops revealing a behavior that persists

95 definitions from 33 papers. Kinds: 41 failure modes, 13 operational, 12 taxonomies, 11 formal, 10 informal, 8 criteria. Stances: 64 proposed, 20 adopted, 11 adapted.

These definitions describe failures in which a behavior persists while the evidence for it in the CoT is suppressed or disguised, or in which changes in the model's activations defeat a probe (a classifier trained on the activations) even though the model produces "conventional deceptive text" ([Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation)). [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) named it for training against a CoT monitor ("obfuscated reward hacking"). [Drori et al. 2025](./#p=drori2025output) show that penalties on the output alone can produce it ("feedback spillover"). [Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned) predict that monitorability degrades when the reward on the CoT and the reward on the output are "in-conflict" for the monitored computations and optimization achieves high values for both rewards. [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) distinguish obfuscated policies from obfuscated activations when the monitor is a probe. The definitions disagree on whether leaving out the evidence counts as obfuscation and on whether obfuscation is intentional (see the disagreements at the end of this section).

> such an objective could cause agents to learn to hide their intent within the CoT while continuing to exhibit misaligned behavior, a phenomenon we will call obfuscated reward hacking.
>
> ([Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), §1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | obfuscating latent | informal | "The latent is obfuscating (O) if it actively encourages the monitor the believe in the incorrect value of $v$ ." |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | obfuscated reward hacking | failure-mode | "learn to hide their intent within the CoT while continuing to exhibit misaligned behavior" |
| [Drori et al. 2025](./#p=drori2025output) | obfuscated CoT | failure-mode | "unwanted behaviors persist in a way that is undetectable by the monitor" |
| [Drori et al. 2025](./#p=drori2025output) | feedback spillover | formal | $\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[M_{\text{CoT}}]<\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{\text{control}}}[M_{\text{CoT}}]$ |
| [Drori et al. 2025](./#p=drori2025output) | conditional feedback spillover | formal | $\nabla_{\text{CoT}}\mathbb{E}_\theta[M_{\text{out}}]\cdot\nabla_{\text{CoT}}\mathbb{E}_\theta[M_{\text{CoT}}]>0$ |
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | CoT obfuscation (generic) | failure-mode | "having CoT contents that would make it hard to predict its later actions" |
| [Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned) | in-conflict (reward pair) | informal | "CoT text which achieves a high $R_{\text{cot}}$ cannot describe the CoT computations which are needed to achieve a high $R_{\text{out}}$" |
| [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) | internalized reasoning | failure-mode | "Harmful planning is not present at all in the CoT tokens." |
| [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor) | obfuscated reasoning | failure-mode | "Models learn to still reward hack while not reasoning about it in their CoT: this is obfuscated reasoning" |
| [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) | obfuscation via suppression | failure-mode | "they learn to omit critical reasoning steps from their CoT" |
| [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) | CoT obfuscation (vs. reasoning suppression) | criterion | *undetected rate above the no-reasoning baseline: the CoT is still used while evading the monitor* |
| [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026monitorbench) | omission (vs obfuscation) | failure-mode | "the model largely omits explicit references to the decision-critical factors in its CoT, rather than obfuscating them" |
| [Mao et al. 2026](./#p=mao2026taming) | CoT obfuscation | informal | "an operational, non-intentional training outcome in which reward or accuracy rises while operational monitorability declines" |
| [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) | obfuscated activations | failure-mode | "produces conventional deceptive text while modifying its internal representations to avoid triggering the detector" |
| [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) | obfuscated policy | failure-mode | "reward-hack with text that doesn’t trigger the probe even on the original model" |

### 9. Evading the monitor: stress tests and a model's control over its CoT

81 definitions from 21 papers. Kinds: 38 operational, 20 failure modes, 13 taxonomies, 6 informal, 4 criteria. Stances: 60 proposed, 12 adopted, 9 adapted.

These definitions describe what it takes for a model or an attacker to defeat a monitor: stress tests in which an actor (the monitored model) tries to finish a side task alongside its main task while avoiding detection, the evasion strategies (jailbreaking the monitor, burying evidence, reframing, camouflage, collusion between a monitor and the agent), and the rates that score them. CoT controllability, "a reasoning model’s ability to control properties of its CoT" ([OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra)), is measured by [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra), [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) and [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) as a capability that bears on evasion, and [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) report it "closely correlated with obfuscation success across the eight models studied".

> We thus propose stress-testing (Shah et al., 2025, Section 6.7) monitors by having “actor models” try to succeed at (proxies for) bad behaviors while evading detection by “monitor models.”
>
> ([Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain), §4)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) | monitor evasion (red-team success criterion) | operational | "how easily the red team prompts can decrease monitor performance relative to the CoT baseline while keeping the side task success rate significantly above the No CoT baseline" |
| [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) | obfuscation stress test | operational | "whether CoT would be less monitorable if models know their chain of thought is monitored" |
| [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra) | CoT controllability | informal | "a reasoning model’s ability to control properties of its CoT" |
| [Anthropic 2026](./#p=anthropic2026opus55) | CoT controllability | informal | "gaining the ability to intentionally control aspects of its extended thinking" |
| [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) | CoT controllability | informal | "a model’s ability to reshape its own reasoning trace under an imposed constraint" |
| [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) | worst-case monitorability | operational | "we test its monitor evasion capabilities in common real-world-inspired control environments" |
| [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor) | monitor jailbreaking | failure-mode | "monitors fail to flag side task reasoning, while the reasoning remains completely transparent to human readers" |
| [Chidambaram et al. 2026](./#p=chidambaram2026corrupted) | stealth (hint success criterion) | criterion | "the chain-of-thought $y$ does not reveal reliance on the hint, as judged by a separate monitor model" |
| [Lee et al. 2026](./#p=lee2026training) | adversarial strategies against detection (denial, reframing, camouflage) | taxonomy | "Denial ... Reframing ... Camouflage embeds covert actions within benign-looking operations" |
| [Xiao & Phuong 2026](./#p=xiao2026bootstrapped) | collusion (untrusted monitor) | failure-mode | "assign low suspicion scores to help the agent avoid detection, while producing reasoning that appears honest to $T$" |
| [Arike et al. 2026](./#p=arike2026information) | post-hoc justifications | failure-mode | "the agent convinces the monitor that its misbehavior was intended by the user" |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) | needle-in-a-haystack strategy (hiding incriminating reasoning) | failure-mode | "hide incriminating bits of the CoT amidst benign text using a “needle-in-a-haystack” strategy" |
| [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026monitorbench) | stress tests (direct concealment, monitor-aware evasion) | taxonomy | "In direct concealment, the model is explicitly instructed to avoid verbalizing the specific decision-critical factors" |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | challenges to CoT-monitoring safety cases (sandbagging, evasion) | failure-mode | "models could sandbag and pretend they cannot solve the misaligned task without CoTs when they actually can" |
| [Li et al. 2025](./#p=li2026spilling) | evasion success rate | operational | "the proportion of transcripts that received a suspiciousness score above a certain threshold from the LLM monitor" |

### 10. Monitors: what they read and the oversight setups they work in

137 definitions from 45 papers. Kinds: 54 informal, 45 operational, 17 taxonomies, 14 formal, 7 criteria. Stances: 98 proposed, 26 adopted, 13 adapted.

These definitions describe the reader. A monitor is an automated system, usually an LLM and sometimes a probe, a regular expression or the monitored model itself, that reads the trace and other observations and flags misbehavior. The definitions also cover its scope, that is, what it reads (only the CoT, only the actions, all messages, or activations), and the oversight protocol it works in (trusted, untrusted, bootstrapped, used during training). 8 definitions in this group cite [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) and 9 cite [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring).

> A CoT monitor is an automated system that reads the CoT of a reasoning model and other relevant information and flags suspicious or potentially harmful interactions.
>
> ([Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain), §1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) | CoT monitor | informal | "an automated system that reads the CoT of a reasoning model and other relevant information and flags suspicious or potentially harmful interactions" |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | monitor | informal | "The monitor tries to determine the value for a particular property of outputs, $v$ ." |
| [Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned) | CoT monitor | informal | "an LLM-based classifier which takes the CoT of a model as one of its inputs" |
| [Anonymous 2026 (implementation)](./#p=anon2026implementation) | monitor (chain-of-thought monitor) | informal | "outputs a suspicion score M (π) ∈ [0, 1] which aims to track P (A, t, π)" |
| [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) | Bayes-optimal CoT monitor | formal | $m_g^\pi(Y\mid X,Z):=\mathbb{E}_{o\sim\pi(O\mid X,Z)}[g(Y\mid X,o)]$ |
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | monitor scopes | taxonomy | "CoT-only, action-only, and all-messages" |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | CoT Monitor vs Action Monitor (monitor scope) | taxonomy | "Action Monitor, which only observes agent actions ... and CoT Monitor, which observes the full agent trajectory" |
| [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra) | CoT-only monitor | operational | "Its recall rate measures the CoT monitorability of the model." |
| [Algaba et al. 2026](./#p=algaba2026reasoning) | display (access levels of the observability ladder) | formal | $\mathcal{O}_1=q\oplus R$, $\mathcal{O}_2=q\oplus R\oplus S$, $\mathcal{O}_3=q\oplus R\oplus S\oplus C$, $\mathcal{O}_4=\mathcal{O}_3\oplus Z$ |
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) | monitor trace type and approach | taxonomy | "characterized by what it reads, which we call trace type, and by how it maps the input to an outcome" |
| [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) | regex monitor | operational | "a simple string check of the reasoning trace against a pre-defined set of forbidden strings" |
| [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) | policy-as-monitor | informal | "it may be advisable to use the policy model as monitor model" |

| [Lee et al. 2026](./#p=lee2026training) | blackbox monitoring | informal | "inspects only agent outputs and must infer intent from external traces" |
